- **Raw trees local-only, node count shared.** The raw per-page trees (potentially large) go to the local-only cache (`accessibility.json`); the run summary carries only the total accessible-node count. The tree's accessible *names* are visible-label text (the same class of data the DOM already exposes to extraction), not credentials, so this isn't a secret-bearing capture like storage state — but the aggregate-only-in-shared-output rule is kept uniform across all signals regardless.
- **Node count as a resilience signal.** §2c frames the tree as doubling as a tier-1/3 resilience signal: a near-zero node count (e.g. a canvas-rendered UI) is an early warning that selector-based extraction will struggle, which is why the count — not just the raw tree — is the surfaced aggregate.

### Decision note — §2c third signal: response headers (implemented)

The response-header fingerprint (CSP, HSTS, X-Frame-Options, `Server`, `X-Powered-By`, CORS) follows the same opt-in, browser-tier, one-run-directory pattern. It forced the first §2c §2h fork the count-only signals (console, accessibility) had sidestepped, because for headers the *values* are the intelligence — and some headers (`set-cookie`, `authorization`) are known secret shapes. **Decision (user-confirmed): presence/counts only in shared output.**

- **Header values are never surfaced to shared output.** The raw full headers — all values, `set-cookie` included — are written to the local-only run cache (`headers.json`). The run summary carries only non-sensitive *derived* facts: the count of distinct header names, and presence booleans for the key security headers (CSP / HSTS / X-Frame-Options). This keeps the count-only line the earlier signals held and needs no secret-pattern redaction yet.
- **Value-surfacing is deferred to the §2h redaction slice.** Surfacing useful non-secret header values (e.g. `Server`, CSP contents) to shared output is worth doing, but it's exactly a redaction decision — an allowlist/denylist of header names is a form of the redaction pipeline — so it waits until that pipeline is built rather than being decided ad hoc here. The alternatives weighed were a curated fingerprint allowlist (rejected: makes the §2h call prematurely) and all-local-only (rejected: needlessly hides the posture signal).

### Decision note — §2c fourth signal: client-side storage state (implemented)

Client-side storage — the browser context's cookies + per-origin localStorage, via `context.storage_state()` — is the last free/near-free §2c signal and the first that *cannot* sidestep redaction: session cookies and localStorage tokens are themselves the sensitive data, so it is the deliberate forcing function for the §2h redaction pipeline (built first as its own slice — see the secret-redaction-pipeline note in §2h). Same opt-in (`capture.storage: true`), browser-tier-only, one-run-directory, off-by-default shape as the other §2c captures.

- **Raw local-only, redacted-entries shared.** The raw, unredacted storage state (every cookie and localStorage value) is written to the local-only run cache (`storage_state.json`) and never shared. The shareable `StorageStateSignal` carries non-sensitive counts (cookies / localStorage entries / origins) *and* the entries as redacted `name=value` strings: the name is kept (a structural signal — which cookies and storage keys the app uses), the value is passed through the §2h `redact` primitive. This is a genuine step past the header signal's counts-only line — real entries reach shared output — made safe by the now-existing redaction pipeline.
- **Why reconstruct `name=value` before redacting.** Redaction recognizes secret *shapes*; a bare cookie value (opaque hex) matches none. Reconstructing `name=value` lets the pipeline's cookie/auth-key rules fire on the name (`sessionid`, `access_token`, …) while its token-shape rules still catch a JWT/API-key value under any key. This is why the pipeline was built first — the signal consumes it rather than reinventing an ad-hoc scrub.
- **The residual risk is stated, not hidden.** Because storage entries *are* surfaced (values, redacted — unlike the header signal's counts-only), the known-shape boundary has teeth here: a secret value in no recognized shape, under an *unrecognized* name (e.g. a framework cookie like `laravel_session=<opaque>`), matches nothing and would reach shared output **unredacted**. This is consistent with §2h's own model — shared output is *known-pattern* redacted, and the raw full state (all values) additionally lives in the local-only cache — but §2h is explicitly not an entropy detector, so surfacing entry values accepts this residual. It is called out rather than implied-away. Mitigations if it proves too risky in practice (a per-target expansion of the recognized cookie/key names via the recognizers table, an opt-in entropy heuristic, or falling back to names-only for storage values) are deliberately deferred, not silently assumed.
- **Context-level capture.** Unlike the per-page console/a11y/header collectors, storage state spans pages, so it is captured once at the context level after the crawl (before `context.close()`), guarded so a capture failure never fails the extraction it rode along with.

This is also the §2h "bring-your-own-session" input surface (a supplied storage state is how authenticated targets are run); capturing and safely summarizing it is the read side of that same mechanism. The **input side is now implemented** — see the bring-your-own-session decision note in §2h.

## 2d. Operational essentials (the gap between "extracts data" and "usable tool")

These didn't come up earlier because they're not about *finding* an element — they're what makes the difference between a tiered-cascade demo and something people actually run unattended. All of them lean on existing libraries, same as everywhere else in the plan:

- **Politeness & rate limiting.** A first-class `PolitenessPolicy` object (respects `robots.txt` crawl-delay, honors `Retry-After` headers, caps concurrent requests per domain) rather than just a README promise, since §6 already commits to respecting robots.txt by default. **Not Crawlee-sourced** (see the D1 decision in §9): concurrency caps, when they land, are a small in-house bounded worker pool over the existing sequential fetch path, not a dependency.
- **Change detection.** Use `ETag`/`Last-Modified` headers or a content hash to skip re-processing pages that haven't changed since last run. This pairs directly with the per-domain cache already in §2 and is what makes monitoring-style use cases (price tracking, content-change alerts) cheap to run on a schedule instead of re-scraping everything every time.
- **CAPTCHA / anti-bot detection — detection, not bypass.** Recognize known challenge-page fingerprints (a Cloudflare interstitial's title text, a reCAPTCHA iframe) and fail loudly with a clear "blocked" result rather than looping forever or silently returning a challenge page as if it were data. An honest "hit a wall here" is itself a useful, expected output.
- **Retry and error classification.** Separate transient failures (timeout, 5xx, 429) from permanent ones (404, 410, confirmed block) — transients retry with backoff (the `tenacity` library, MIT-licensed, is a clean fit rather than hand-rolling this), permanents land in a dead-letter log for human review instead of vanishing silently.
- **Output pipeline.** Pluggable sinks downstream of extraction — JSON Lines, CSV, SQLite as the practical defaults (all stdlib or near enough), Parquet as an optional extra — with the field schema from §2a validated (via `pydantic`, already in the stack) before anything is written. Extraction without a usable output path isn't a finished tool.
- **Run observability.** A structured run summary — items scraped, how often each tier had to escalate, error counts, which fields fell back to tier 3/4 — so a user can tell whether a run actually went well without reading raw logs. This is the project's own signals-catalog thinking (§2c) turned inward on itself.
- **Proxy support hook.** A pluggable interface for user-supplied proxy lists/rotation (Playwright and httpx both accept per-request proxy config natively) — the project doesn't provide or endorse any proxy source, just a clean extension point, since real-world scraping at any scale usually needs one. **Delivered** — see the bring-your-own-proxy decision note below.

**Decision — Phase-1 output-pipeline slice.** The output pipeline (`spoor/operational/output.py`) is a first-class stage between extraction and disk, not a CLI afterthought. Its Phase-1 pass ships the dependency-free text sinks — **JSON, JSON Lines, and CSV** (all stdlib); SQLite and Parquet remain named §2d sinks for later. The format is chosen by inference from the `-o` file extension (`.json`/`.jsonl`/`.csv`), overridable by an explicit `--format`; an unknown extension with no `--format` is a clear error raised *before anything is fetched or written*. Per §2d, records are validated against a pydantic model built from the §2a config fields (correct field set, declared types, `extra="forbid"`) before any file is opened, so a schema violation aborts the whole write rather than leaving a partial file — and CSV columns are exactly the config's fields, in declared order.

**Decision — SQLite and Parquet sinks (closes #108).** The two sinks §2d named as "for later" land as the same single-file, schema-validated-then-redacted write every other format already goes through — no separate pipeline. **SQLite** uses stdlib `sqlite3` (no new dependency): a fresh `records` table (TEXT/REAL columns matching the config's `string`/`number` field types, a missing value as SQL `NULL`), the target file deleted first so a re-run against the same path replaces it rather than erroring on a pre-existing table. **Parquet** needs the optional `pyarrow` extra (`pip install 'ht-spoor[parquet]'`, §7) — absent, `_write_parquet` raises a plain `ImportError` naming the install command *before* anything is written, the same "fail loudly before the fetch/write, not silently" posture the rest of this pipeline already takes for an unrecognized format. Both infer from their file extension (`.sqlite`, `.parquet`) exactly like the existing formats, and both are covered by `features/output.feature` alongside JSON/CSV/MD; the missing-`pyarrow` path is a plain unit test (`tests/test_output_parquet_missing.py`), not a `.feature` scenario, since the dev environment always has `pyarrow` installed (the feature scenarios need it too) and there is no way to drive a genuinely-missing-dependency path through Gherkin without actually uninstalling it.

**Decision — Phase-1 politeness slice and where the knobs live.** The politeness policy is a first-class object (`spoor/core/config.py:PolitenessPolicy` for the declarative knobs, `spoor/operational/politeness.py` for the runtime gate), configured per target via an optional `politeness:` block on the §2a extraction config — consistent with §2a's "a new site is a new config, never new code." Its Phase-1 scope is **respect `robots.txt` (default on, per §6) and honor the crawl-delay** (from robots.txt, or a `delay:` override in seconds). Fetching the `robots.txt` itself follows RFC 9309's conservative reading: an absent file (404) means no restrictions, but a server error (5xx) means the rules are unavailable and the origin is treated as disallowed rather than crawled as if open. Two parts of the §2d politeness bullet were deliberately deferred at the time and that was **not** a weakening of the commitment, only a phasing split: (1) *caps concurrent requests per domain* — the tier-1 crawl was sequential (one page, follow next-link), so a concurrency cap had nothing to bound until a request pool existed; it now does, and **has since landed** as `max_concurrent_per_domain` (see the #174 decision note below); (2) *honors `Retry-After` headers* — this only had meaning once a retry mechanism existed, and **has also landed** with the Phase-3.5 retry slice, living on `RetryPolicy` (`respect_retry_after`) rather than `PolitenessPolicy` since it is a retry concern (see the Phase-3.5 decision note below). Per §6, `respect_robots` may be set to `false`, but only as an explicit, deliberate opt-out — never the default.

**Decision — Phase-3.5 retry / error-classification slice (the deferred Retry-After lands here).** The retry mechanism the two notes above pointed forward to is a first-class object, split the same way as politeness: the declarative knobs live in `spoor/core/config.py:RetryPolicy` (an optional `retry:` block on the §2a config — `max_retries`, `backoff`, `respect_retry_after`), and the runtime lives in `spoor/operational/retry.py:RetryingFetcher`. Each tier-1 fetch outcome is classified by **generic HTTP category** (§0 — no site-specific wording): *transient* (timeouts, dropped connections, 5xx, 429) retries with exponential backoff (`backoff * 2ⁿ`); *permanent* (other 4xx) is taken at its word and not retried; success returns. This closes the item the politeness note deferred: a server-sent **`Retry-After`** (seconds or HTTP-date) is honored in place of the backoff when `respect_retry_after` is on, **capped at 120 s** so a hostile or mistaken header can't freeze a run (the *number* of waits is already bounded by `max_retries`; the cap bounds each wait's *duration*). A URL that exhausts its retries or fails permanently becomes a `FetchFailure` recorded in `RunResult.dead_letter` (URL + generic reason + attempt count) instead of crashing the run on the bare `raise_for_status()` it replaces; the run summary surfaces the dead-letter log and a transient-retry count (delivering the §2d run-observability "error counts"). A dead-lettered run is **not escalated** to a heavier tier — a classified fetch failure is a definite answer about the origin, not an empty page a browser might render differently, and re-fetching would only hit the same failing server (the same reasoning that already exempts a robots-`blocked` run). **Scope** of *this* slice is the sequential tier-1 httpx fetch path; **browser-tier navigation retry is a deliberate follow-on** that has since landed (see its decision note below), reusing this slice's classification and backoff. Two departures from the plan-as-written, recorded rather than hidden: (1) the retry loop is **hand-rolled, not `tenacity`** (§7 lists tenacity as a dependency) — the policy is ~30 lines of bounded loop + classification and needs to thread the injectable `sleep` seam the politeness gate already uses and to *return* a classified `FetchFailure` rather than raise-after-exhaust, both of which fit a small purpose-built loop more cleanly than tenacity's decorator/retry-state model; the §7 row is updated to reflect this. (2) The dead-letter URLs are surfaced on the summary like `blocked` URLs are; because a query string *can* embed a token, running both through the §2h redaction pipeline before shared output is a noted candidate for the redaction slice — not a weakening of the §2h non-negotiable, which governs *captured* secrets (HAR/storage), not the config's own request URLs.

**Decision — Phase-3.5 change detection (skip re-extracting unchanged pages).** The store lives in `spoor/operational/change_detection.py:ChangeDetector`, a per-domain, local-only JSON file (parallel to the tier-3 fingerprint cache) keyed by URL, remembering each successful fetch's `ETag`, `Last-Modified`, and a SHA-256 of the body. It is enabled by an opt-in top-level `change_detection: true` on the §2a config (a plain `bool`, not a sub-model — the mechanism needs no tuning knobs), and is **default off**: skipping extraction changes what a run produces (an unchanged page yields *no* records), which a one-shot scrape shouldn't get by surprise; a monitoring run turns it on. On a re-fetch the tier-1 path replays the remembered validators as a conditional `If-None-Match` / `If-Modified-Since` request; a **304 Not Modified** — or, when the server sends no validators at all, a body whose content hash matches — marks the page `unchanged` (recorded on `RunResult.unchanged`, surfaced on the summary), and its extraction is skipped. Four decisions worth recording: (1) **Store is the §0-sanctioned "runtime-learned" exception**, the same class as the fingerprint cache — a URL only ever appears as a runtime dict key, never a source literal, so `check_genericity.py` (which scans all of `spoor/`) stays clean; it is local-only (§2h) since a stored URL can embed a token. (2) **A first sighting always extracts** (nothing to compare), and a corrupt/malformed store loads empty rather than crashing — change detection degrades to "extract everything", never to a failed run. (3) **Pagination interaction is a natural consequence, not a hidden rule**: a 304 carries no body, so `_next_url` finds no next link and the crawl ends at the first unchanged page; a hash-matched 200 *does* still carry a body, so pagination continues past it. (4) **An unchanged run is not escalated** (`_should_escalate` now also excludes `unchanged`): zero records because the page matches a prior run is a definite "nothing changed" answer, not a selector miss a heavier tier would resolve — the same reasoning that already exempts `blocked` and `dead_letter`. **Scope** is the sequential tier-1 httpx fetch path; **browser-tier change detection is a deliberate follow-on** (a rendered navigation doesn't issue a conditional GET as cleanly), not covered here.

**Decision — Phase-3.5 browser-tier navigation retry (the retry note's follow-on lands).** The browser-tier navigation retry the retry slice named as a deliberate follow-on now lands, and it lands as **shared classification and backoff, not a second copy**. `spoor/operational/retry.py` was refactored so the HTTP-category classification (`_classify_status`), the `Retry-After` parsing (`_retry_after_seconds`, with the same 120 s cap), and the backoff (`_wait`) are transport-agnostic module functions; `RetryingFetcher` (tier-1 httpx) and the new `RetryingNavigator` (browser tier) are thin wrappers that differ only in *how* they attempt and *what* they catch. `Tier2Resolver._crawl` navigates through the navigator instead of a bare `page.goto(...)`: a transient navigation outcome — a 5xx/429 on the response, **or** a Playwright timeout / transport error (`PlaywrightTimeoutError` / `PlaywrightError`) raised by the navigation — is retried with backoff; a permanent 4xx or an exhausted transient one becomes the same `FetchFailure` recorded on `RunResult.dead_letter` and surfaced on the summary, and the browser tier's retries feed the same `retries` count. This replaces the previously-uncaught `page.goto` that would crash the whole run on the first navigation timeout. Three decisions worth recording. (1) **No Playwright import in `retry.py` (§0/layering).** The navigator takes the transient exception types from its caller (`transient_exceptions=(PlaywrightTimeoutError, PlaywrightError)`) and inspects only a small structural `_NavResponse` protocol (`status`, `header_value`) that Playwright's `Response` satisfies — so the operational retry module stays free of any browser dependency, and `navigate` is generic over the concrete response type so the caller keeps full access to its own `Response` (e.g. `.headers` for the §2c header signal). (2) **A dead-lettered navigation stops the crawl** exactly as a dead-lettered tier-1 fetch does — a page that never loaded has no next-link to follow — with `page.close()` still run via the existing `finally`; a `None` navigation response (a same-document navigation) is treated as a no-retry success with no status to classify. (3) **The transient-*exception* path is unit-tested, not BDD-tested**, because a live browser can't be made to raise a navigation timeout deterministically; the `.feature` drives a real browser against a scripted flaky loopback server for the HTTP-status paths (503-once-then-page, always-503, 404), and `RetryingNavigator`'s classification/backoff/exception handling is covered in `tests/test_retry_navigator.py`. Nothing here is site-specific (§0). This closes the browser-tier follow-on; **browser-tier change detection remains a separate deferred follow-on** (see the change-detection note).

**Decision — bring-your-own-proxy (docs/COMPETITIVE_PLAN.md §4/§6, Phase 1, closes #149).** The proxy hook named above ships as the same bring-your-own posture as `session:` (§2h): Spoor never discovers, provides, or rotates a proxy itself, only routes a run's traffic through one the operator already has. The declarative knob is `spoor/core/config.py:ProxyPolicy` (`server`, `username`, `password`, `bypass`), set via an optional `proxy:` block on the §2a config — omitted, a run connects directly, unchanged. Both resolvers wire it at client/browser construction, never per-request, since there's exactly one proxy for the whole run: `Tier1Resolver.run`'s `httpx.Client` and `Tier2Resolver.run`'s own separate `httpx.Client` (built for the `Politeness` gate) both pass `proxy=` built by `_httpx_proxy_url`, which folds `username`/`password` into the proxy URL as embedded credentials (httpx's single-URL `proxy=` shape, not the removed `proxies=` dict); `Tier2Resolver.run`'s `chromium.launch(proxy=...)` takes the same policy reshaped by `_playwright_proxy_settings` into Playwright's `ProxySettings` TypedDict (`server`/`username`/`password`/`bypass` as separate fields, its native shape). Set at browser *launch* rather than per-context so it applies uniformly without multiplying the existing HAR/session `new_context` branches. Two consequences worth recording: (1) because `Politeness` (robots.txt) and the sitemap seeding (§2d) both simply reuse whichever `httpx.Client` they're handed, a configured proxy transparently covers those fetches too — no changes needed in `politeness.py` itself. (2) a `client` injected directly into a resolver (the test seam) is used as-is and `config.proxy` has no effect on it, the same posture the `timeout`/`follow_redirects` construction already takes — `proxy:` only shapes a client/browser the resolver builds itself.

**Decision — Phase-3.5 anti-bot / CAPTCHA detection (detection, never bypass).** The recognizer lives in `spoor/operational/challenge.py:detect_challenge`, a pure scan of already-fetched HTML that returns a `ChallengeSignal` (vendor + the matched marker) or None. Both resolution tiers run it on the page they fetched/rendered (`RunResult.challenge`), and the run summary surfaces it **loudly and high** ("CHALLENGE DETECTED (Cloudflare) — not scraped as data"), delivering §2d's "recognize known challenge-page fingerprints and fail loudly rather than silently returning a challenge page as if it were data." Three decisions worth recording: (1) **Markers are structural, not vendor hostnames** — widget CSS classes (`g-recaptcha`, `h-captcha`, `cf-turnstile`), challenge-token params (`__cf_chl`), interstitial title text ("just a moment...") — both to keep false positives low and so nothing reads as a hardcoded target domain. This matters because `scripts/check_genericity.py` scans *all* of `spoor/` (not just `core/`), so a literal `hcaptcha.com` in `operational/` would fail it; the tokens are vendor-generic anti-bot infrastructure recognition, the same §0-allowed class as parsing `robots.txt`, not knowledge of a target site. It is a maintainable, PR-extendable table. (2) **A challenge is recognized whether it rides a 2xx *or* sits behind an error status.** A challenge in an otherwise-successful (2xx) response is caught at extraction; a challenge served *with* an error status (a 403/503 Cloudflare interstitial) is *also* recognized, because such a fetch is both a dead-letter failure and a challenge and the operator deserves to see both. The tier-1 fetcher scans the *failing* response body and carries the recognized vendor on the `FetchFailure` as a new `challenge` field (the generic `ChallengeSignal` only, never the raw body — §2h); `Tier1Resolver` promotes it to `RunResult.challenge` when it dead-letters. The browser tier scans the rendered `page.content()` on a dead-lettered navigation (the DOM is the right source there; the navigator itself stays body-free). Detection is reset per attempt, so a terminal transport failure (a timeout with no body) after an earlier challenged attempt reports None rather than a stale hit. This closes the "challenge bodies behind an error status" follow-on the first anti-bot slice named; the two slices now cooperate rather than partition — the retry slice still labels the HTTP error, and this adds the "…and it was a wall" line beside it. (3) **Escalation is deliberately unchanged**: a challenged page that yields zero records still escalates to the browser tier — a passive JS check can clear on render, turning a tier-1 "challenge" into real tier-2 data — and only a challenge that survives to the final attempted tier is surfaced. Detection never attempts to *solve or bypass* a challenge; naming the wall is the whole feature.

**Decision — request/response hooks, slice 1 of 2 (docs/COMPETITIVE_PLAN.md §4, Phase 2, part of #150).** #150 names two distinct extension-point gaps — Crawlee/Scrapy-style request/response middleware, and Crawljax-style exploration on-state/on-transition hooks — "tracked together" because they're the same underlying need, but the issue itself says the shape "needs its own design pass." This slice ships only the first: `spoor/operational/hooks.py:RunHooks` (`on_request`, `on_response`), plain Python callables supplied at the same injection seam `healer`/`sleep`/`client` already use (`extract.run_report(cfg, hooks=...)`) — not a config field, since a hook is code the operator brings, not declarative per-target config `load_config` could parse from YAML (§0). The Crawljax-style exploration half is deliberately deferred to its own issue, the same split pattern as the retry/change-detection Phase-3.5 slices above. Both resolution tiers call the hooks identically: `on_request(url)` may return extra headers merged into a URL the politeness gate has *already* decided to fetch (`gate.can_fetch` runs first; a blocked URL is never offered to either hook) — it cannot change or redirect that URL, so there is no path from a hook to routing around the robots.txt check. `on_response(url, html)` may return replacement HTML; it runs on tier 1 after the real fetch is recorded for change detection (`detector.record` uses the *actual* server response, never a hook's rewrite) but before challenge detection, extraction, and link discovery, and on tier 2 after `page.content()` but before the a11y snapshot and extraction — so a hook's rewrite is read by selectors and link-following the same way the real page would be, while every local-first-cache capture (a11y, the stored conditional-request validators) still reflects what the origin actually sent. Neither hook has a path to shared output that skips §2h redaction: `on_response` only ever touches pre-extraction HTML text, upstream of the output pipeline's schema validation and the redaction pass that always runs before a file is written. On the browser tier, `on_request` is applied via `page.set_extra_http_headers(...)` before each navigation (a per-page call, since `_crawl` opens one page per URL) rather than once on the context, so a hook that varies its headers per URL is honored per fetch, not fixed for the whole run.

**Decision — exploration hooks, slice 2 of 2 (closes #187).** The Crawljax-style half the slice-1 note above deferred: `spoor/exploration/hooks.py:ExplorationHooks` (`on_state_discovered`, `on_transition_taken`), supplied to `explore()` the same way its existing `progress` callback already is — an optional, keyword-only, default-`None` parameter, threaded through `resume_exploration()` identically. **Both safety properties #187 named hold by construction, not convention, confirmed by reading the explorer loop itself rather than assumed:** (1) a hook can never drive or influence a new interaction, because both fire only *after* the data they describe is already committed — `on_state_discovered` right after `graph.add_state(...)` (mirroring exactly where `progress()` already fires for a new state), `on_transition_taken` right after `graph.add_transition(...)` (after `driver.perform(action)` has already executed and `evaluate_action`'s gate decision, the single structural enforcement point before any destructive action fires, has already been fully consumed) — neither hook's return value is read, so there is no path from a hook back into which actions get attempted; a destructive action the gate skips outside a sandbox is recorded only as a skip, never reaches `add_transition`, so `on_transition_taken` is never even offered it. (2) a hook never sees a raw signal: `on_state_discovered`/`on_transition_taken` receive `ExploredState`/`ExploredTransition`, freshly built dataclasses carrying only counts (`SignalCounts`) and `redact()`-passed text (`ChangeSummary.console_added`/`storage_added`/`storage_removed`) — never the graph's own `StateSignals`/`TransitionSignals`, which hold raw console lines, storage keys, and network requests verbatim. This redaction-applying builder is deliberately **independent of `spoor/exploration/wiki.py`'s** own per-state/per-transition view builders, which already do the same `redact()`-based work but are shaped and tested for a single end-of-run bulk pass over a finished graph — reusing them would mean refactoring shipped, tested rendering code to be callable mid-walk for a safety property that a small amount of parallel logic gets for free, with zero regression risk to existing wiki output. Not exposed via CLI flag or config, the same posture as slice 1: a hook is code the operator brings, not declarative per-target config `load_config` could express (§0).

**Decision — sandbox registry gets a technical backstop (closes #102).** The §9 backlog item this resolves: `sandbox: true` was previously an unconditional bypass — `is_sandbox(target, declared=True)` returned `True` with zero host inspection, so a config or `--sandbox` flag pointed at a real, publicly-routable site (e.g. `https://staging.example/`) was silently treated as safe for destructive actions, a real gap against the non-negotiable even though it required an explicit operator action to trigger. `spoor/security/sandbox.py:is_sandbox` now checks the host either way: loopback (`localhost`/`127.0.0.0/8`/`::1`) still auto-qualifies with no declaration needed (unchanged); a *declared* sandbox additionally requires the host to be at least a private-range IP (`ipaddress.ip_address(host).is_private` — RFC1918, link-local, and the other IANA-reserved ranges, which already subsumes loopback) via a new `_is_private_host`. A declared sandbox whose host is a real public address, or an ordinary hostname that isn't an IP literal at all (`staging.example`, or an attacker-registerable lookalike like `10.0.0.5.evil.com`), now returns `False` — `declared` can only *confirm* a host that already looks private, never make an arbitrary real site count as one. **This changes behavior CLAUDE.md's non-negotiable wording previously allowed** ("registry match (`localhost`/`127.0.0.1`, or `sandbox: true`)" read as two independent, equally-sufficient conditions) — an explicit, recorded decision, not a silent tightening: declaring a real staging host by hostname (not a private IP literal) as a sandbox no longer works, a deliberate trade-off against #102's "no technical backstop" gap, confirmed with the maintainer before implementing (see the options considered: widen auto-detection to private ranges while leaving declared-only as an unchecked escape hatch, vs. this — requiring the host to check out either way; the latter was chosen). CLAUDE.md's non-negotiable bullet is updated accordingly. `features/exploration_safety.feature`'s "declared sandbox" scenarios were rewritten to match: a declared private-range host (`10.0.0.5`, `172.16.0.9`, `192.168.1.50`) is recognized as a sandbox and permits destructive actions; a declared real host (`staging.example`, `shop.example`) or declared spoofing attempt (`127.evil.com`, `localhost.evil.com`) is not, and destructive actions against it are still skipped.

**Decision — locale-aware destructive-action keyword guard (closes #101).** The §9 backlog item this resolves: the §2e destructive-action classifier matched an English-only keyword list, so a real-site UI labeled in another language could go unrecognized as destructive and get interacted with rather than skipped — narrowing the sandbox-only non-negotiable to English-labeled sites. The open question the backlog note left ("how locale is declared vs. detected... the exact safe-default when a target's language isn't covered") is resolved by sidestepping it: no locale is declared or detected at all. New `spoor/exploration/destructive_keywords_i18n.py` ships keyword sets, translated as common idiomatic UI verb forms (not literal glosses), for six more space-delimited languages (Spanish, German, French, Portuguese, Italian, Dutch); `spoor/exploration/safety.py`'s `_DESTRUCTIVE_RE` is now built from the union of English's existing `DESTRUCTIVE_KEYWORDS` and every one of those sets, unconditionally, every time — not gated behind a declared or detected locale. This directly satisfies the backlog note's "err safe" instruction *structurally*: a false positive here (treating a benign label as destructive) only ever costs a skipped, revisitable action, while a false negative costs a real destructive action firing on a real site, so there is no reason to ever match against less than the full union — over-matching across every covered language is strictly the safe direction, with no config complexity needed to get there. **Scope, explicitly bounded, not silently incomplete:** only space-delimited languages are covered — the matcher is a `\b`-bounded regex, which has no concept of a word boundary in a script written without spaces between words (Chinese, Japanese, Thai, …), so extending there needs a tokenizer, a materially different problem tracked separately rather than mismatched silently here. `evaluate_action`'s signature is untouched (no `locale` parameter was added), so the existing structural "no bypass parameter" BDD pin needed no change. `features/exploration_safety.feature` gained a new scenario outline pairing two destructive and one-to-two safe labels per covered language, confirmed to classify identically to their English equivalents.

**Decision — form-fill generator seam, v1 (closes #103).** The §9 sketch this resolves named three open questions: which generator library, how a value gets declared generated-vs-pinned, and how field semantics map onto a generator. All three confirmed with the maintainer before implementing (two explicit scope choices, not unilateral): **Faker only** for v1 (Mimesis/exrex deferred — see the §9 backlog note above), keyed purely by the existing `ActionableElement.input_type` signal (`pattern=`/`min`/`max`/`required`/`<select>`-options capture deferred, same note) — and **opt-in per field**, not generator-backed by default as the issue's own wording suggested: a new `generate: true` key on a scaffold field entry, read only when `value:` is blank, so every existing scaffold and every field that doesn't set it behaves byte-for-byte as before this shipped (the established "opt-in, no cost unless asked for" posture this codebase has used for every prior extension point — hooks, resume, proxy support). A non-blank `value:` always wins over `generate: true` on the same entry — an explicit pin is never second-guessed. `spoor/scaffold/interactive_config.py:generate_value(kind)` is the seam itself: a plain `kind -> Faker call` mapping for exactly the kinds `_GENERATOR_TABLE` already recognizes (email/phone/url/number/date/text), returning `None` — never a guess — for `choice`/`unknown`/anything else, the same "honest unknown over a silently wrong default" posture `infer_field` already held. `build_scaffold` now emits a commented `generate: false` toggle only for a field whose `kind` is actually generatable, so the scaffold never offers a dead toggle. `spoor/scaffold/apply.py:applicable_fields` resolves a blank-valued, `generate: true` field through that seam at apply time (a fresh value per call, never cached/seeded) and reports a field asking to generate for a non-generatable `kind` as a `FailedField` — an explicit ask that couldn't be honored is not the same "user chose not to fill this in" outcome a plain blank value already was, so it is never silently skipped. `evaluate_action`/`is_sandbox` gating is unchanged: a generated value is typed through the exact same sandbox-only path a pinned one already was, not a new interaction surface. New `faker>=24.0` runtime dependency.

**Decision — visual state identity, strand (b) (closes #104).** The §9 visual-capture note's strand (b): folding the perceptual screenshot hash already captured per state (`StateSignals.screenshot_hash`) into how a revisit is told apart from a genuinely new state, so a modal overlay or a color-only status change that normalizes to the same DOM/text no longer silently collapses with the screen it covers. Two scope choices confirmed with the maintainer before implementing, since this was explicitly "direction to spar, not yet designed": **(1)** `spoor/exploration/state.py:state_id(html)` itself is untouched — it stays DOM/text-only, a pure function of the HTML alone, so every existing `exploration_state.feature` scenario needed no change. The split happens one layer up, in `spoor/exploration/explorer.py`'s `capture()` closure: a new closure-local `dom_id -> [(dHash, final_state_id), ...]` map, scoped to one run's walk (not persisted — a resume starts it empty). An apparent revisit (the DOM hash already matches a graph state) is confirmed by comparing this visit's dHash, **by Hamming distance**, against every variant already recorded for that DOM id — not by exact equality; a distance beyond `_VISUAL_SPLIT_BITS` (3 of 64 bits) mints a new state instead of deduping into the wrong one, and its id is a fresh `sha256(dom_id + hash)` digest, not a literal `f"{dom_id}:{hash}"` string, so it can never share a string-prefix with `dom_id` itself. No hash (or no baseline hash ever recorded for that DOM id) falls back to the original DOM-only dedup, byte-for-byte. **Two real bugs, caught by CI on the opening PR and fixed before merge, not papered over:** the first implementation compared screenshot hashes by exact string equality — wrong, since a dHash is a *perceptual* hash meant to be compared by bit-distance; on a real page, clicking into a text field during exploration (an existing, unrelated behavior — `textbox` is in `ACTIONABLE_ROLES`) adds a focus ring to the screenshot, a trivial pixel difference that nonetheless produced a different hash string and wrongly minted a spurious second state for the identical screen. The fix reuses `spoor/exploration/screenshot_store.py:_NEAR_DUPLICATE_BITS`' exact threshold and reasoning (`hamming_distance`, `spoor/core/visual.py`) for this same dHash — already proven there to separate re-render noise from a genuine visual difference. The second, consequent bug: that spurious split's id (`f"{dom_id}:{hash}"`) shared a literal string-prefix with the plain `dom_id`, which broke `apply.py`'s `_resolve_state` (`str.startswith`-based prefix matching) — `spoor apply-scaffold`'s live browser-tier scenario failed exactly this way in CI (the state prefix resolved ambiguously, so no field was ever applied, and the live-page assertion then timed out waiting for a field the run never reached). Fixed by minting the new id as a content hash instead of a prefixed string. A third, smaller hardening added during the same review: the dHash string is now parsed through a helper that degrades to "no usable hash" on a `ValueError` instead of raising — the real driver can never produce a malformed value, but a crash on bad test/fixture data would otherwise abort the whole run, out of step with every other optional signal in this module. **(2)** Resume/replay verification (the three `state_id(driver.state_html())` recomputations in `explorer.py` that confirm a reset-and-replay landed where expected) deliberately stays DOM-only — a narrower first cut, not an oversight, tracked as a known gap rather than silently extended. The **cost model turned out better than scoped in the normal walk**: the confirmed design accepted "one extra signal capture on an apparent revisit, paid only where it might change the outcome" as the price; in practice it costs *nothing* extra for a fresh run, because every state but the root is reached by firing an action, which already captures a before/after bundle for the transition diff (`explorer.py`'s existing `capture(signals=after)` call) — that bundle is reused as the revisit probe, confirmed and pinned by a dedicated scenario asserting the exact signal-capture call count with and without a visual split. **One real exception, found and documented rather than glossed over:** a *resumed* run's root already lives in the loaded map, so its own `capture()` call (`signals=None`) does pay for one extra `capture_signals()`, once per resume — harmless (the visual-variant map starts empty every run, including a resume, so this call can never find a baseline to split against and always falls through to the original DOM-only dedup, identical to before #104) but a real, now-documented cost rather than a silently-claimed zero. New `features/exploration_visual_identity.feature` (5 scenarios, fast-tier) and a full existing exploration-suite regression run (243 scenarios, unaffected) confirm no behavior change for a driver that never produces a screenshot hash (every fake-driver-based scenario in the suite).

**Decision — screenshot-embedding redaction design, resolved (closes #106).** The §9 sketch's open question: what does "curated export" mean in practice — a manual review step, automated PII/text detection on the image before embedding, or both? Researched before deciding, not assumed: a screenshot, once captured, never automatically leaves the machine today — `spoor/serving/api.py` and `mcp_server.py` have no route or tool that serves a static file or image (both are 100% JSON/dict-returning, confirmed by reading each in full), `MapStore`/`map_view` never carry a screenshot path or bytes (only a `screenshot_changed` boolean), and the wiki directory is never read back by `spoor serve`/`serve-mcp` — they are two completely separate output paths. So the non-negotiable ("pixels reach a shared surface only via an explicit opt-in") is already structurally satisfied by the existing `--screenshots` flag: nothing transmits automatically, and the operator is the only one who can share the resulting directory, which they must do manually. Automated PII/text detection on images was considered and rejected for this slice — it's a materially different, much larger undertaking (a new CV/OCR dependency, real false-positive/negative risk, out of step with this codebase's "never guess, be honest about limitations" posture) more suited to the optional Phase 3 LLM-tier epic than a redaction-design fix. Confirmed with the maintainer before implementing: formalize that the opt-in itself is the "deliberate, explicit" gate the design called for, and add one small, concrete piece of transparency rather than new infrastructure — `spoor/exploration/wiki.py:build_pages` now computes `has_screenshots` (true when at least one real `ImageRef` exists anywhere: a full-page shot, an element clip, or an opened-contents capture — checked by actual reference, not just whether the caller-supplied mapping is non-empty, since a supplied sink can hold only failed `None` captures) and the index page shows a visible `.notice` banner when it's true: "this wiki includes screenshots... review the images yourself before sharing this directory with anyone else." `features/exploration_screenshots.feature` gained two scenarios (the notice appears when any screenshot is embedded; it's absent from a pixel-free wiki); self-review caught and fixed the all-`None`-captures false-positive case with a throwaway script before committing.

**Decision — bounded concurrent fetching, in-house (docs/COMPETITIVE_PLAN.md §4, closes #174).** Split out of the former crawl-engine mega-issue #148 after D1 (#146) decided against adopting Crawlee. `PolitenessPolicy` gains `max_concurrent_per_domain` (default **1**, reproducing the fully-sequential behavior every run had before this existed), enforced by a new `Politeness.acquire`/`release` pair (`before_fetch`/`after_fetch` are the same methods under their established call-site names) rather than the old single global "has this run fetched anything yet" flag. `acquire` does two independent things per domain (`netloc`, via `urlsplit`): blocks on a `threading.Semaphore` sized to the cap (bounding how many fetches to that domain may be *in flight* at once), then — under a separate per-domain `threading.Lock` held only for the check-and-sleep, never across the fetch itself — sleeps the crawl-delay if this isn't that domain's first-ever dispatch. The two compose rather than one superseding the other: the cap bounds concurrency, the delay still bounds how often a *new* fetch may start, so raising the cap doesn't mean raising the request rate past what `delay` already allows — it means more of an already-delay-paced sequence of dispatches can be in flight waiting on network I/O at once. **Not asyncio** — `Tier1Resolver.run` dispatches fetches through a stdlib `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor` (a fixed `_MAX_WORKERS = 8` ceiling, independent of the per-domain cap, just enough headroom that a multi-domain `crawl:` doesn't serialize on pool size itself) and processes each completed fetch — change detection, hooks, extraction, frontier growth — back on the single calling thread as it finishes, so none of that existing per-page logic needed its own locking; only the raw fetch itself runs concurrently. This is the same reasoning that decided D1: introducing an asyncio seam for one feature, in a codebase that is synchronous throughout (`httpx.Client`, Playwright's *sync* API), was the specific cost that tipped D1 against Crawlee's `AutoscaledPool`, and it applies identically to a hand-rolled asyncio pool. **Scope is tier 1 only** — the browser tier stays single-threaded (Playwright's sync API isn't safe to drive from more than one thread), so `Tier2Resolver._crawl` now pairs `gate.acquire`/`gate.release` around each page purely to avoid deadlocking the per-domain semaphore (an acquired-but-never-released slot would permanently stall that domain after the first page), not to gain any real concurrency there. Two other pieces of shared mutable state needed the same thread-safety treatment once tier 1 could call into them from multiple threads at once: `RetryingFetcher.retries` (incremented under a lock — the only mutable counter it keeps) and `Politeness`'s own robots.txt cache (a double-checked read outside the lock, so two threads racing on the same uncached origin do a harmless redundant fetch rather than blocking each other). One minor, deliberate behavior refinement over the old code, not a regression: the old single global "first fetch of the whole run" flag is now per-domain, so a multi-domain `crawl: {same_origin: false}` run no longer makes its first fetch to a *second* domain wait out a delay just because some *other* domain was fetched first — observable only in that multi-domain case, byte-for-byte unchanged for the single-domain crawl every existing scenario already covers.

**Decision — resumable, persisted crawl state (closes #175), storage model.** Split out of #148 alongside #174, and §175's own issue text is explicit this needed a design pass first: Spoor already has a resume mechanism, §2e's exploration-mode `--resume-from`, and that is the **wrong shape to reuse here**. `--resume-from` is a state-graph model (`spoor/exploration/persisted_map.py`/`resume.py`): it resolves a named anchor *state* inside a saved graph, then re-drives a live browser through a root→anchor replay path to re-establish position before continuing — machinery that exists because exploration's unit of progress is a *screen reached by a sequence of actions*, not a URL. A tier-1/tier-2 `crawl:` frontier has no actions, no states, no replay — just URLs and depths — so none of that applies. The right precedent instead is `spoor/operational/change_detection.py:ChangeDetector` and `spoor/core/fingerprint_cache.py:FingerprintCache`: a small per-domain JSON store under `.spoor-cache/`, loaded once at the start of a run and saved once at the end (in a `finally`, so it persists even on an aborted run) via a `_dirty`-flag-gated, never-raise-on-I/O-failure write, with `storage.CACHE_ROOT` read at call time (not import time) for test monkeypatching — the same contract every persistent store in this codebase already holds to. New `spoor/operational/crawl_state.py:CrawlStateStore` follows that contract exactly: `.spoor-cache/crawl_state/<sanitized-netloc>.json`, one file per domain, keyed inside by the full target URL (so one domain can hold several distinct crawl targets — `MapStore` keyed its records by URL the same way, before it moved to SQLite; see the #214 decision note) — persisting exactly the two structures both tiers' loops already carry in memory, `seen: list[str]` and `frontier: list[[url, depth]]`, nothing else.

**Decision — resume semantics.** Opt-in via a new top-level `resume: bool = False` on `ExtractionConfig` (§2a) — placed like `change_detection`, not nested under `crawl:`, since a plain `pagination.next` chain benefits too (a large paginated listing interrupted mid-run has the same "pick up where it stopped" need, with or without `crawl:` set). Three questions #175 named, answered: (1) **A resumed run never re-checks an already-visited URL** — the persisted `seen` set *is* the exclusion set, so "resume" means strictly "continue the frontier past where it stopped." Re-verifying old pages for changes is a different, already-existing feature (a fresh, non-resumed run with `change_detection: true`), not something resume additionally does — conflating the two would make "resume" mean two different things depending on another flag's state, which is worse than just composing the two orthogonally. (2) **`_MAX_PAGES` bounds new pages fetched *this invocation*, not a lifetime count across every resume** — each resumed run gets its own fresh budget; a very large crawl finishes over several bounded invocations rather than needing one unbounded one. `max_depth` needs no special handling at all: depth stays anchored to the original target root throughout, never re-rooted at a resume point the way exploration's "the anchor is depth 0 on a resume" model re-roots. (3) **Sitemap seeding (`crawl.sitemap`) only fires on a fresh start** — on a resume, those seed URLs are already captured in the persisted `seen`/`frontier` from the run that first enqueued them, so re-seeding would be pure duplicate work the existing `seen` dedup would just drop anyway, not a correctness issue but wasted lookups. **Scope is both tiers** — `Tier1Resolver.run` and `Tier2Resolver._crawl` carry the identical frontier/`seen` shape (per the #169/#174 decision notes above), and persistence only touches it before the loop starts and after it ends, so it has no interaction with #174's concurrency (load/save are single-threaded, outside the `ThreadPoolExecutor` block) or #150's hooks (orthogonal, per-fetch concerns). **Accepted tradeoff, stated rather than hidden:** persistence happens only in the `finally` at the end of a run, exactly like `ChangeDetector`/`FingerprintCache` — a hard kill (not a clean exception) mid-run loses that invocation's progress since its last save, the same risk profile those two stores already accept; incremental per-page checkpointing was considered and rejected as unneeded complexity for a first slice. When a run's frontier empties naturally (the crawl is complete), the persisted entry for that target is cleared, so a later run — resumed or not — starts fresh instead of finding an empty frontier forever. One sharper edge within that same accepted tradeoff, found in self-review: on tier 1's concurrent dispatch (#174), a URL is marked `seen` the moment it's *submitted* to a worker, not once its fetch completes — so if an exception propagates out of the dispatch loop (not a hard kill; `finally` still runs and still persists), any URL mid-flight at that moment is persisted as `seen` without ever having been fully processed, and a resume won't retry it. Accepted for the same reason as the rest of this note: a first slice, not incremental-checkpoint-grade durability: the alternative (only marking `seen` after a fetch fully completes) would let the same URL be submitted to two workers at once under concurrency, a worse bug than the one this accepts.

**Decision — link following with scope rules (`crawl:`), closing issue #169.** `docs/COMPETITIVE_PLAN.md` names this Spoor's single biggest concrete gap against Scrapy/Crawlee: `pagination.next` follows exactly one declared link in a straight chain; there was no way to discover and follow *multiple* links per page. Split out of the bigger crawl-engine issue (#148), which is blocked on the still-open D1 Crawlee decision (#146) — this piece isn't, because link discovery and scope filtering are Spoor-side generic logic no matter what (if anything) eventually sits underneath as a request-queue/concurrency layer (the tier-2 decision above already named Crawlee's request-queue/autoscaling machinery as something a *later* phase could still adopt; this phase didn't need it). New `CrawlScope` config (`spoor/core/config.py`): `include`/`exclude` glob patterns (`fnmatch.fnmatchcase`, matched against the URL's *path*, case-sensitive so there's no OS-dependent folding), `max_depth`, and `same_origin` (default true). Both tiers' page-following loops (`Tier1Resolver.run`, `Tier2Resolver._crawl`) were structurally identical — a single `url` variable, a `seen` set, the existing `_MAX_PAGES` cycle guard, `url = _next_url(html, url, config)` at the bottom — so this generalizes that single-URL chain into a `deque[tuple[str, int]]` frontier rather than inventing a parallel mechanism; every other per-page behavior (politeness, retry, change detection, challenge detection, signal capture, the `_MAX_PAGES` ceiling) is unchanged, since these are just more frontier items flowing through the identical loop body, not a new code path. Three decisions worth recording: (1) **A `pagination.next` hop never counts against `max_depth`** — it continues the same logical listing rather than branching to a new part of the site the way a `crawl`-discovered link does, so the two compose without surprise (pinned directly: a scenario crawls a paginated listing with `max_depth: 0` and confirms pagination still completes while the depth-1 crawl link is dropped). (2) **`break` became `continue` on a blocked/dead-lettered frontier item in both loops** — with a single chain this was never observable (nothing left to continue to either way), but with a frontier, one failed branch must not abort sibling branches still waiting their turn; a regression scenario (`crawl` omitted entirely) confirms single-chain behavior is still byte-for-byte unchanged. (3) **Extends, doesn't revisit, the change-detection interaction note above**: an unchanged (304/hash-matched) page still runs both `_next_url` *and* `_discover_links` exactly as that note already described for pagination alone — a 304's empty body naturally yields neither, a hash-matched 200's body still does. `features/extraction.feature` gained the fast-tier scope-rule matrix (include, exclude-wins-over-include, max_depth, same-origin default and opt-out, the pagination-composition case, and the no-`crawl`-at-all regression guard) plus one `@browser` scenario proving tier parity; a live multi-page proof against PrestaShop (the existing deeply-linked archetype) is a natural immediate follow-on, deferred the same way PrestaShop's own onboarding deferred its integration test ("stand the mechanism up and prove it fast-tier first"). Generic to every target (§0): nothing here reads a hostname or path from anywhere but the config.

**Decision — sitemap.xml and robots.txt as crawl seed sources (`crawl.sitemap`), closing issue #170.** The second of the two D1-independent pieces split out of #148 alongside #169, for the same reason: no dependency needed, since a sitemap is plain XML and robots.txt is already fetched and parsed by `Politeness`. `urllib.robotparser.RobotFileParser` — the parser `Politeness._parser` already builds and caches per origin for `can_fetch`/`crawl_delay` — turned out to already expose `site_maps()`, reading a robots.txt's `Sitemap:` directive(s) for free; no new robots.txt fetch or parsing needed, just a new public `Politeness.sitemap_seed_urls(target)` that fetches and parses whatever it names. A `<urlset>` yields its page URLs directly; a `<sitemapindex>`'s entries are child sitemaps, fetched and parsed the same way (breadth-first, bounded by a fetched-sitemaps cap and a total-URL cap against a cyclic or unbounded index — the same cycle-safety posture `_MAX_PAGES` already gives the crawl itself). `xml.etree.ElementTree` (stdlib, already proven sufficient elsewhere in this codebase's "no dependency where one isn't earned" discipline) parses tolerantly of a missing/non-sitemaps.org namespace by comparing tag *local names*, not full namespaced tags — real-world sitemaps are inconsistent about declaring it. A fetch failure, non-200, or XML that doesn't parse (or whose root is neither `urlset` nor `sitemapindex`) yields nothing from that source, the same tolerant degrade the rest of this module already takes for a missing or unreadable robots.txt — sitemap seeding never turns a run that would otherwise succeed into a crash. **Opt-in** (`crawl.sitemap: true`, default false): pulling in a whole site's sitemap in one step is a bigger behavior change than `crawl:` being set alone implies, the same reasoning `change_detection` and every `capture.*` flag already follow. Seeded URLs are filtered through the *same* `_in_scope` check #169 already built (same-origin/include/exclude) — turning sitemap seeding on never bypasses the rest of the config's scope rules — and added to the frontier at depth 0, the same tier as the target itself, since a sitemap names known entry points rather than links discovered *from* a page. `features/operational.feature` gained the scenario matrix (a sitemap seeds the crawl, seeds are still scope-filtered, no `Sitemap:` directive yields nothing extra, a sitemap index is followed to its children, malformed XML degrades rather than crashes, and the off-by-default regression guard) — reusing that file's existing per-scenario dynamic-routing-table pattern rather than a shared static fixture, since a shared `fixtures/static/robots.txt` would have silently changed every other test on `localhost:8000`. Generic to every target (§0): nothing here reads a hostname from anywhere but the config and the target's own declared robots.txt.

## 2e. Exploration mode: mapping the full interaction space (post-v1 — see §4)

Everything so far assumes a config (§2a) tells the tool which fields/actions matter. This is a second, distinct mode: point it at a target with no config at all, and it autonomously discovers every actionable element, interacts with each one, captures the full signal bundle (§2c/§2d) before and after, and builds a graph of `state --action--> state`. The end product is a generated wiki — one page per state, one page per transition, browsable — that answers "what happens, on every signal level, when I press this button" for the entire product, not just the fields a user thought to ask about.

**Prior art worth building on, not depending on directly.** Crawljax (now maintained under the OWASP ZAP project, `zaproxy/crawljax`) solved the core research problem here years ago: systematically firing every user-triggerable event on an AJAX-heavy app and building a state-flow graph. It's Selenium-based, not Playwright, so the right move is porting its *algorithm* onto our existing Playwright base rather than adding it as a dependency — specifically its **state abstraction function**: normalize the DOM (strip timestamps, session IDs, counters, anything volatile) and hash it, so revisiting an equivalent state is recognized as such instead of exploding into infinite near-duplicates. Actionable-element discovery reuses the accessibility tree signal already planned in §2c (it already labels roles like button/link/textbox generically, no new mechanism needed).

**Destructive-action safety (per your decision): sandbox-only, non-configurable.** The engine maintains a registry of declared local/self-hosted targets (matches on `localhost`/`127.0.0.1`, or a target explicitly marked `sandbox: true`). Only against a registry match will it interact with actions a heuristic flags as destructive/irreversible (keyword/ARIA matching on delete, remove, buy, purchase, pay, confirm, send, submit-payment, log out — a maintainable, PR-extendable list, not a fixed set). Against anything else — any real external site — those actions are always skipped and logged as skipped; there is deliberately no flag or config option that relaxes this for a non-sandbox target. This is a hard boundary, stated plainly in the README, not a tunable default: the worst case for exploring a real, unvetted site is "missed a state," never "placed a real order" or "deleted real data."

**Run-level controls are core, not optional hardening.** Every exploration run has a hard, user-set budget — max states discovered, max wall-clock time, max requests — that auto-stops the run when hit, plus a manual kill switch to stop a running exploration on command. Both ship in the same phase as the rest of §2e's safety system (Phase 5), never as a follow-up: a run that can't be bounded or stopped is itself a reliability/safety gap under §1's stance and the "core architecture, not later hardening" principle, not a nice-to-have.

This registry's `sandbox: true` declaration is checked against the host, not taken on faith (closes #102, see the decision note below) — a declaration only ever widens what counts as a sandbox to a host that is itself at least private-range, never to an arbitrary real, publicly-routable one.

**Signal capture during exploration reuses the existing cost tiers — and is request-driven, not judgment-driven.** Default to the free/near-free signals from §2c/§2d for every single transition (screenshot, accessibility snapshot, storage-state diff, HAR delta, console diff) — that's the bulk of the wiki's content. The expensive ones (full video, heap snapshots) are reserved for states matching an explicit rule the user configures (e.g. "always deep-capture states matching `/checkout/*`," or "deep-capture anything under this DOM subtree") — corrected from an earlier draft of this section, which described the system autonomously deciding a state looked "interesting." Per §1, Spoor doesn't make that judgment call itself: it records what it's told to, at the depth it's told to, nothing more.

**Wiki generation is comparatively cheap.** The graph (nodes = states with their captured signal bundle, edges = actions with their before/after diff) is just structured output; rendering it as a browsable site means reusing an existing static site generator (MkDocs or Docusaurus) for navigation/search, with a small templating layer turning each node/edge into a page, plus an embedded graph visualization (Mermaid works for smaller graphs; `vis.js`/`pyvis` for larger ones) for the overview. No custom wiki engine to build. *(Superseded in the build: the slice-6a decision note below chose a self-contained Jinja2 renderer over reusing MkDocs/Docusaurus, keeping Mermaid for the overview — see that note for the rationale.)*

**This is the real test of §0's no-tailoring rule.** The four archetype fixtures (§5) stop being just tier-cascade validation and become the proof that the *exploration algorithm* itself is generic: if it can autonomously map Juice Shop's vulnerability-laced flows, Sauce Demo's checkout, a self-hosted ERP's CRUD-heavy forms, and a Netflix-clone's media/auth state — with the same unmodified state-abstraction and safety logic — that's the real evidence this isn't tailored to any one of them.

**Honest scope note.** This is a substantial second project riding on top of the v1 stack, not a small addition — realistically another **6–9 weeks** (state-abstraction tuning, the safety system with its own dedicated test coverage, graph storage, and wiki templating each take real iteration), most likely landing as v2 rather than folded into the v1 estimate in §4. It's written up in full here because the design is worth locking in now, even though building it comes after v1 ships.

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e in shippable, gated slices.** Rather than one large drop, exploration mode is built in order: (1) **safety gate** — sandbox registry + destructive-action classifier + the always-skip-non-sandbox rule; (2) **state-abstraction function** — DOM normalize + hash; (3) **actionable-element discovery** — reusing the §2c accessibility tree; (4) **run-level controls** — budget + kill switch (ship *with* the safety system, per above, not after); (5) the **explorer loop** tying 1–4 into a `state --action--> state` graph; (6) **wiki generation**. Each is its own BDD-first PR.

**Decision — §2e first slice (safety gate), shipped.** Exploration begins with its safety foundation, built before anything that can fire an action exists — the non-negotiable, and the smallest self-contained, browser-free, in-process-testable piece (mirroring how §2f started with its thinnest testable slice). Two pure-logic pieces plus the gate combining them: `spoor/security/sandbox.py:is_sandbox(target, *, declared)` recognizes a sandbox only by loopback host (`localhost`/`127.*`/`::1`) or an explicit declaration; `spoor/exploration/safety.py` holds the PR-extendable `DESTRUCTIVE_KEYWORDS` set (the §2e list: delete, remove, buy, purchase, pay, confirm, send, submit-payment, log out), an `is_destructive(label, role)` whole-word matcher, and `evaluate_action(...)` returning a `GateDecision(allowed, reason)`. A non-destructive action is allowed anywhere; a destructive one only inside a sandbox, else skipped with a log-ready reason. **The non-negotiable is enforced structurally, not by discipline:** `evaluate_action`'s signature is exactly `(target, label, role, declared_sandbox)` — no bypass/force/override parameter exists — and `exploration_safety.feature` pins this with a scenario asserting that parameter set, alongside behavioral scenarios (destructive-on-real-target → skipped; destructive-in-sandbox → allowed; non-destructive → allowed anywhere). Emitting the skipped-log line and actually driving actions belong to the explorer loop (slice 5); this slice only *decides*. The registry stayed self-declared at this point (§9 gap, accepted) — tightened later, see the sandbox-registry-backstop decision note below.

**Decision — order-related destructive phrases added to `DESTRUCTIVE_KEYWORDS`.** Found by the maintainer testing a live run: the original §2e list caught no checkout/cancellation action at all — "Place order" and "Cancel order" matched nothing, a real false-negative against the sandbox-only non-negotiable, the more dangerous miss direction. The fix is **phrases, not the bare noun**: `"place order"`, `"cancel order"`, `"return order"` were added, the same pattern `"submit payment"` (not bare `"submit"`) already used — a bare `"order"` keyword would have immediately false-positived on ordinary navigation that happens to share the noun ("My Orders", "Order history", "Order state", "Track order", "Order confirmation"), exactly the failure mode the maintainer flagged pre-emptively before any code was written. `exploration_safety.feature`'s classification scenario outline gained the three new destructive rows *and* explicit regression rows for each of those order-containing-but-safe labels, pinning both directions in the same table rather than trusting the phrase-matching logic by inspection alone.

**Decision — §2e second slice (state abstraction), shipped.** The Crawljax state-abstraction function, still pure logic: `spoor/exploration/state.py:state_id(html)` normalizes a page's DOM and returns a stable SHA-256 hex digest, so revisiting an equivalent screen is one state, not an infinite spray of near-duplicates. Identity is built from (a) the tag **structure** in document order/nesting, dropping `script`/`style`/`noscript`/comments; (b) a small allowlist of stable, **state-bearing attributes** (`role`, `type`, and the flags `disabled`/`checked`/`selected`/`open` + ARIA equivalents) — every other attribute value (`id`/`class`/`href`/`value`/`nonce`/`data-*`) is ignored so a session token or random React id can't fork a state; and (c) **visible text with volatile spans masked** (timestamps, clock times, UUID/long-token strings, and digit runs → a constant placeholder), so a cart badge of 3 vs. 4 is one state while a "Maintenance" vs. "Orders" heading is two. Parses via `parsel` (already the core extraction parser — no new dependency). `exploration_state.feature` pins both directions (volatile-only edits collapse to one id; genuine structure/text/state-attribute differences separate) and determinism. **First, PR-tunable cut** — §2e's own note that state-abstraction tuning takes real iteration; the known trade-off is that two screens differing *only* by a meaningful number collapse (accepted, it's the abstraction's point), and attributes outside the allowlist don't distinguish states yet. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decision — §2e third slice (actionable-element discovery), shipped.** Still pure logic, and reusing an existing mechanism rather than inventing one: the §2c accessibility-tree signal (`Accessibility.getFullAXTree`, as `AccessibilityCollector` captures it) already labels every node with a generic ARIA role, so "what can the explorer act on here?" is filtering that node list. `spoor/exploration/discovery.py:discover_actions(ax_nodes)` keeps the nodes whose role is in the PR-extendable interactive set `ACTIONABLE_ROLES` (button/link/textbox/checkbox/radio/combobox/menuitem/tab/switch/…) and that aren't `ignored`, returning an `ActionableElement(role, name, backend_node_id)` per surviving node. The accessible **name** is precisely the label the safety gate (slice 1) classifies; the **backend node id** is the CDP handle the explorer loop (slice 5) will use to locate the element to act on it. Document order and every interactive occurrence are preserved — deduplication is the graph's job, not this slice's — and the reader is tolerant of missing fields (no name → empty label; no backend id → `None`), so an icon-only button is still a candidate. `exploration_discovery.feature` pins that only interactive, non-ignored roles survive, that name and DOM handle are preserved, that an unnamed element is still discovered, and that a page with no interactive roles yields nothing. This slice only *discovers*; driving actions is the explorer loop. Generic to every target (§0): the same role set for all.

**Decision — native `<video>`/`<audio>` control children are excluded from discovery, closing issue #166.** Exploring streaming-clone (#160-#165) with a session supplied reached every detail screen correctly but still logged 36 skips every run, all "could not be performed: ... not located after replay" on the same six elements: the native `<video controls>` play/mute/volume/scrubber/fullscreen/overflow-menu buttons. Investigated against a real Chromium build rather than guessed at: these elements resolve and box-model fine over CDP every time (ruling out a timing/auto-hide theory), but `document.elementFromPoint` — what `_ACTUATION_PROBE_JS`'s live click-verification (the 7a decision above) checks the click lands on — always retargets a hit anywhere inside a `<video>`/`<audio>` element's *closed* user-agent shadow DOM back to the host element itself, confirmed directly (`elementFromPoint` at a play button's coordinates returns the `<video>` tag, every time). That is a structural fact of the browser's shadow-DOM boundary, true on every site with a plain HTML5 media element (§0), not a fixture quirk or a flake — so no amount of retrying or hovering first would ever make the existing verification succeed for one. Rather than discover something that can never pass its own verification (and spend three wasted replay attempts finding that out, every single visit), `spoor/exploration/discovery.py:discover_actions` now excludes it at the source: `_has_media_ancestor` walks a candidate node's `parentId` chain in the *same* `Accessibility.getFullAXTree` snapshot discovery already has (confirmed live that the ancestor chain does carry a `role: "Video"`/`"Audio"` node — no extra CDP round-trip needed) and drops any node descending from one. Cycle-guarded for a malformed/fake node list, and tolerant of a node missing `parentId`/`nodeId` (every existing fake driver), which simply never matches — the same "include it" default the rest of discovery already takes for any other missing field. Verified live: the same streaming-clone crawl that logged 36 skips before now logs 0, discovering the same 7 states. `features/exploration_discovery.feature` gained three scenarios (a native media-control child excluded, an ordinary wrapped element still discovered, a cyclic ancestor chain doesn't hang).

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e fifth slice (explorer loop) in sub-slices.** The explorer loop is the largest §2e piece (graph + action-driving + a live browser + per-transition signal capture + a user-operable command), too big for one well-reviewed PR, so it is built logic-first in gated sub-slices: **5a** — the pure-logic `ExplorationGraph` model + the `explore` orchestrator (DFS with reset-and-replay, tying in the safety gate, state abstraction, discovery, and run controls) against an injected `BrowserDriver` protocol, fully in-process testable with a fake driver, no live browser; **5b** — the real Playwright `BrowserDriver`, live-browser BDD against a loopback fixture, **and** the user-operable `spoor explore <url>` command with the plain-language sandbox rule stated in the README (the first runnable exploration command lands here, per the "state the sandbox rule plainly in README" requirement); **5c** — per-transition signal capture (screenshot / accessibility / storage / HAR / console diffs, §2c/§2d). Each is its own BDD-first PR. Wiki generation stays slice 6.

**Decision — §2e fourth slice (run-level controls), shipped.** The budget + kill switch the "run-level controls are core, not optional hardening" note above requires, landed *with* the safety system rather than after — still pure logic. `spoor/exploration/control.py` holds two pieces: `RunBudget(max_states, max_requests, max_seconds)` — the user's hard bounds, each optional (`None` = that dimension unbounded), each set bound validated positive (a zero/negative bound is a config error, not a silent instant stop); and `RunController`, which tracks one run's consumption (`record_state`/`record_request`), reads wall-clock through an **injectable `clock`** (default `time.monotonic`; deterministic in tests, the same seam the retry module uses for sleep), and holds a **thread-safe kill switch** (`kill()` sets a `threading.Event`, so a stop-on-command can arrive from another thread). `check()` returns a `StopDecision(should_stop, reason)` with a log-ready reason; **the kill switch is checked before the budget**, so a manual stop is always reported as such, and budget dimensions are then checked in order (states, requests, time). The explorer loop (slice 5) records progress and calls `check()` before each step; this slice only *decides*. `exploration_control.feature` pins each bound stopping the run at its threshold (and not before), a within-bounds run continuing, positive-bound validation, and — pinning the §2e non-negotiable that a run is always stoppable — that the kill switch stops even an unbounded run and takes precedence over a reached budget bound. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decision — §2e fifth slice, sub-slice 5a (explorer loop, orchestrator), shipped.** The pure-logic core of the explorer, no live browser yet. `spoor/exploration/graph.py` holds the `ExplorationGraph` — nodes are abstract states keyed by the slice-2 `state_id` (each carrying the actions discovered there), edges are fired actions (`from --action--> to`), and gate-refused actions are recorded separately as skips, so the map honestly shows both what was explored and what was left alone; states dedupe by id (adding a known state is a no-op). `spoor/exploration/explorer.py:explore(driver, *, target, controller, declared_sandbox=False)` ties the four earlier slices together: it discovers each state's actions (slice 3), asks `evaluate_action` which may be fired (slice 1), recognises a revisited state by `state_id` so the walk is finite (slice 2), and calls `controller.check()` before every action so a budget or the kill switch always stops it (slice 4). The walk is **depth-first with reset-and-replay** navigation — to revisit a state it resets the driver and replays the path that first reached it, so it assumes no back button and works for any deterministic driver — and only recurses into a genuinely new state (a transition back to a known state is recorded but not re-explored). The browser sits behind a `BrowserDriver` `Protocol` (`reset`/`state_html`/`ax_nodes`/`perform`), so the whole loop runs in-process against a fake app; `exploration_loop.feature` pins mapping a two-state app, collapsing a revisited state to one node, the §2e non-negotiable both ways (a destructive action is skipped and its state never reached on a real target, but fired inside a sandbox), and stopping on a state budget or a pre-thrown kill switch. The real Playwright `BrowserDriver`, a live-browser run, and the `spoor explore` command are sub-slice 5b; per-transition signal capture is 5c. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decision — §2e fifth slice, sub-slice 5b (real driver + `spoor explore` command), shipped.** The orchestrator from 5a, now driving a real headless browser end to end. `spoor/exploration/driver.py:PlaywrightDriver` satisfies the `BrowserDriver` protocol against a headless Chromium page: it mirrors the tier-2 Playwright lifecycle (`spoor/core/extract.py`) but keeps **one page open for the whole run** (the explorer needs a persistent live page to drive), reads the accessibility tree over CDP (`Accessibility.getFullAXTree`, the same call the §2c a11y signal uses) for discovery, and returns `page.content()` as the state HTML. The one genuinely new piece is `perform`: reset-and-replay reloads the page, and a reload reassigns every DOM/accessibility node id, so the discovered `backend_node_id` is only meaningful inside the snapshot it came from and **cannot** be used to click after a replay — so `perform` re-locates the element in the *current* page by its accessibility **role and name** via Playwright's role locator (which also gives auto-waiting and actionability for free), acting on the first match in document order (deterministic; a known first-cut limitation for role+name collisions).
