Teardown is context-manager-guarded (page → context → browser → Playwright, each swallowing a close error). `spoor explore <url>` is the first runnable exploration command: it builds a `RunBudget` from `--max-states`/`--max-requests`/`--max-seconds` (a non-positive bound is a `BadParameter`, not a silent stop), installs a SIGINT handler that throws the kill switch (so **Ctrl-C stops gracefully at the next action**, not mid-click) and restores the prior handler in a `finally`, runs the driver, and prints a plain-language summary (states discovered / transitions / actions skipped, with a note when skips happened on a non-sandbox target). `--sandbox` is the only way to exercise destructive actions and its help states it is local/test-systems-only — the §2e non-negotiable holds unchanged (no bypass flag; the gate still decides). `exploration_browser.feature` drives real Chromium against a two-page loopback fixture (Home ⇄ Next) and asserts the summary reports two states, two transitions, zero skips — the one exploration scenario with a live socket and a real browser launch, since a browser can't use the in-process fake transport. The plain-language sandbox rule is stated in the README with this command (per the decomposition note's "state the sandbox rule plainly in README" requirement). Per-transition signal capture is sub-slice 5c. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e sub-slice 5c (per-transition signal capture) in two, logic-first.** 5c captures §2e's "free/near-free" signal bundle for every transition — screenshot, accessibility snapshot, storage-state diff, HAR delta, console diff — which is more than one cleanly-reviewable PR because some signals (a11y count, DOM, storage) are cheap reads already near the driver while others (screenshot hash, HAR delta, console events) need live-browser plumbing. So, mirroring the 5a→5b logic-first split: **5c-i** — the pure-logic signal model (`StateSignals` bundle + `diff_signals` → `TransitionSignals`) and its wiring into the graph (each state node carries its captured bundle; each transition carries the before/after diff) and the `explore` orchestrator, exercised in-process against the fake driver via a new `capture_signals()` seam on the `BrowserDriver` protocol — no live browser; **5c-ii** — the real `PlaywrightDriver.capture_signals()` that fills the bundle from a live page (screenshot content hash, CDP a11y node count, `localStorage`/`sessionStorage` keys, console-message and network/HAR deltas), surfaced through `spoor explore`. Each is its own BDD-first PR. Wiki generation (which renders these bundles/diffs per node and edge) stays slice 6.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 5c-i (signal model) then 5c-ii (live capture), shipped.** 5c-i landed the pure model: `spoor/exploration/capture.py` holds `StateSignals` (a state's free-signal bundle — accessibility node count, console messages, storage keys, network requests, screenshot hash) and `diff_signals(before, after) -> TransitionSignals` (what console/storage/network entries appeared, what storage was removed, the signed a11y-node delta, whether the screenshot changed; additions keep first-seen order and dedupe). The graph now carries a bundle on each `StateNode` and a diff on each `Transition` (both optional, default `None`), and `explore` captures the bundle either side of every fired action through a new `capture_signals()` seam on the `BrowserDriver` protocol — exercised in-process against the fake driver. 5c-ii then filled that seam on the real driver: `PlaywrightDriver.capture_signals()` reads all five signals from the live page — the CDP a11y node count, the console and network-request buffers accumulated by page listeners attached at launch (so a transition's diff is the slice between its before/after snapshots, correct under reset-and-replay), the current `localStorage`+`sessionStorage` keys, and a **perceptual (dHash) hash of a screenshot reusing the tier-3 visual hasher** (`spoor/core/visual.py`) — chosen over a byte hash so a transition is flagged as changing the screenshot only on a real visual change, not on anti-aliasing/cursor noise. Every signal is opportunistic (a read that fails is recorded empty, never failing the run — the §2c collector stance). `exploration_signals.feature` (5c-i) pins the diff model in-process; `exploration_signals_live.feature` (5c-ii) drives the real driver against a signal-rich loopback fixture and asserts genuine console/storage/network/a11y/screenshot capture and a real click's diff. The signals now attach to the graph on every `spoor explore` run; surfacing them to the operator/wiki is slice 6. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e sixth slice (wiki generation) in two, logic-first.** Slice 6 turns the `ExplorationGraph` (nodes = states with their captured bundle, edges = transitions with their before/after diff) into the browsable wiki that is §2e's end product. Mirroring the 5a→5b / 5c-i→5c-ii logic-first splits: **6a** — the pure renderer (`spoor/exploration/wiki.py`), a graph → a set of static HTML pages (an index with a graph overview + counts, one page per state showing its redacted signal bundle, one page per transition showing its redacted before/after diff), exercised in-process against a built graph with no browser and no disk; **6b** — wiring it to the CLI (`spoor explore --wiki <dir>` writes the site after a run) **and** a live-archetype integration test (per maintainer direction, "check the wikis are reliably created every time") that runs a real exploration against the §5.1 bench (Juice Shop / Sauce Demo) and asserts a complete, well-formed wiki is produced. Each is its own BDD-first PR.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6a (pure wiki renderer), shipped.** Two scoping decisions were taken with the maintainer before building (recorded here because both depart from an earlier draft of the §2e "wiki generation is cheap" note). (1) **Self-contained renderer, not a reused static-site generator.** The note above suggested reusing MkDocs/Docusaurus with a small templating layer; instead 6a ships a **self-contained Jinja2 renderer** producing plain static HTML directly, with the graph overview drawn by **Mermaid**. Rationale: MkDocs/Docusaurus each pull a heavy toolchain (a full site generator, for MkDocs a Python dep tree, for Docusaurus a Node build) to render what is a fixed, small page set; a direct Jinja2 template layer is fewer moving parts, no build step, and keeps the output honestly local-first. `jinja2` is added to the core dependencies (its first use in the project); Mermaid is loaded from a CDN by the index page (the pages themselves are static and readable offline — only the overview *diagram* needs the network to draw, an accepted first-cut limitation). (2) **Hash-only screenshots for now.** The captured screenshot signal is a perceptual hash (5c-ii), not stored image bytes; 6a therefore reports per transition whether the screenshot *changed* and shows the per-state hash, but embeds **no images** — capturing and embedding the actual screenshots is a deliberate follow-on. **§2h redaction is mandatory and structural here:** the wiki is a shared-output surface, so every captured value it renders — console messages, storage keys, network URLs, action labels — is passed through `spoor/security/redaction.py:redact` before rendering, and Jinja2 autoescaping (on for every template) independently neutralizes HTML/script injection from a captured value; `exploration_wiki.feature` pins that a bearer/vendor token logged to the console never appears raw in any page. The renderer is a pure `build_pages(graph, *, target) -> dict[str, str]` (filename → HTML, testable with no disk) plus a thin `render_wiki(graph, out_dir, *, target)` that writes them; state/transition page filenames are stable indices into the graph's insertion order. Nothing here is site-specific (§0): the same templates render every target's graph. The CLI `--wiki` flag and the live-archetype reliability test are sub-slice 6b. One §0-mechanism refinement rode along: `scripts/check_genericity.py` scans *all* of `spoor/`, so the wiki's literal Mermaid CDN host tripped it; rather than obfuscate the URL, the checker gained a small, explicit `ALLOWED_ASSET_HOSTS` allowlist (`cdn.jsdelivr.net`) for third-party **static assets Spoor's own output loads** — the same "generic infrastructure, not a *target* site" category as the existing `localhost`/loopback allowance, extended deliberately one entry at a time (never a wildcard). This does **not** weaken the §0 non-negotiable, which forbids baking in knowledge of a *target*; a CDN Spoor fetches a library from is not a target. The maintainer chose this over vendoring Mermaid's ~2.8 MB minified blob into the repo (heavier, hand-updated) or embedding un-rendered Mermaid source (no in-browser diagram).

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6b (`--wiki` CLI wiring + live-archetype reliability + graceful degradation), shipped.** The 6a renderer, now reachable from the command line and proven against real sites. (1) **CLI wiring.** `spoor explore <url> --wiki <dir>` renders the mapped graph to a browsable site after the crawl (via `render_wiki`) and echoes the written `index.html` path; the flag's help is plain-language (no §-refs). `exploration_browser.feature` gained a second live-Chromium scenario that reruns the Home⇄Next crawl with `--wiki` and asserts the command reports where the wiki was written and produces a complete site (index + a page per state and per transition) whose overview counts match the run. (2) **Live-archetype reliability test.** Per the maintainer's "check the wikis are reliably created every time" direction, `tests/test_integration_wiki.py` (marked `integration`, skips when the bench is down) crawls **both** archetypes — Juice Shop and Sauce Demo — under a small budget (`max_states=4, max_requests=8, max_seconds=120`), renders the wiki to a durable `test-output/` dir, and asserts a **structural-completeness contract** rather than golden counts (a live SPA's exact graph isn't a contract): exactly one page per state and per transition plus the index, every page well-formed HTML, the index's counts matching the graph, and every state/transition link resolving to a written file. It ran green twice against both sites. (3) **Graceful degradation — a real defect the reliability test surfaced.** The first live run *crashed*: `PlaywrightDriver.perform` let a click that couldn't land (Juice Shop's `Open Sidenav` under a modal overlay — a `Locator.click` timeout) propagate and abort the whole exploration, so **no** wiki was produced. On a live, dynamic target a discovered element being un-actuatable by reset-and-replay time (gone, hidden, covered) is *expected*, not exceptional. The fix keeps the explorer browser-agnostic: a new domain exception `spoor/exploration/explorer.py:ActionError` is part of the `BrowserDriver.perform` contract; `PlaywrightDriver.perform` catches `PlaywrightError` (which includes the click timeout) around the click and re-raises it as `ActionError` (terse, run-varying timeout value omitted for stable text; the Playwright detail preserved as the chained cause); and the `explore` loop catches `ActionError` around navigate+perform, records the action as a skip (`could not be performed: …`) and continues — the next iteration resets and replays afresh, so one dead element never aborts the map. `exploration_loop.feature` pins this in-process (a fake driver whose `perform` raises `ActionError`: the action is skipped and the rest of the map still built), and `tests/test_exploration_driver.py` pins the driver's timeout/detached → `ActionError` translation and the swallowed post-click load-state timeout without needing a browser. With the fix, Juice Shop yields a (degenerate but valid) 1-state wiki and Sauce Demo a rich multi-state/multi-transition one, both produced reliably. Making a *dynamic SPA's* home actions actually actuate (dismissing splash/cookie overlays before discovery so the graph is richer) is a separate exploration-productivity concern, backlogged (§9), not part of this slice. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6c (state-page readability), shipped.** A fresh wiki generated from the live PrestaShop crawl (after 7e) rendered, linked, and redacted correctly but read poorly, and the slice fixes the two concrete defects found — both generic (§0), neither touching the graph model or the safety gate. (1) **Human-readable state labels.** States were labelled only by their opaque 64-char `state_id` (shortened to 12 chars for display), so the index lists and the Mermaid overview were a wall of near-identical hashes. `StateSignals` gained a defaulted `title: str = ""` that `PlaywrightDriver.capture_signals` fills from the page's `document.title` (opportunistic like every other signal — a torn-down context or a titleless page yields `""`); the renderer's `_state_label(sid, signals)` uses the **redacted** title as the state's label on its own page, in the index state/transition lists, and in the graph nodes, falling back to the short id when there is no title, so every state stays identifiable. The full hash is still shown as the state id on its page. (2) **Collapsed repeats on state pages.** The console and network signals are whole-run running buffers (correct for a transition's before/after *diff*, which is the slice between two snapshots), so a state reached late in the run dumped the entire session's output — one real PrestaShop state page carried 105 identical `JQMIGRATE` console lines and thousands of repeated request URLs (~360 KB). A new `_tally(items)` redacts each line then collapses identical values into one row carrying an "× count", first-seen order; transition pages already show a first-seen-deduplicated diff (`capture.diff_signals`) and are unchanged. `features/exploration_wiki.feature` gained scenarios pinning the title label (and its short-id fallback) and the console/network collapse-with-count. **Screenshot embedding stays deferred for a §2h reason recorded here:** string redaction cannot scrub a secret that is *visible on the page* (a token shown in the DOM, PII), so pasting a screenshot into shared output would bypass the redaction every other signal passes through — safe embedding needs its own treatment, and until then the wiki reports the perceptual hash / changed-or-not only. Generic to every target (§0): the same label rule and collapse render every target's graph.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6d (network categorization + per-visit signal scoping), shipped.** Two follow-on refinements to the same "state pages read poorly" thread as 6c, both maintainer-requested after reviewing a live wiki, both generic (§0) and neither touching the graph model or the safety gate. (1) **Network requests grouped by kind.** A state page showed one flat list of every request URL; 6d collates them under a small fixed taxonomy — Documents, Scripts, Styles, Images, Fonts, Media, Data, Other — with a per-group count, so a reader sees at a glance what the page loaded. The kind is a **URL-only heuristic** (`spoor/exploration/wiki.py:_request_category`): the file extension of the last path segment maps through a fixed table, an extensionless path is a Document unless it names an `/api/` (→ Data), and an unrecognised extension is Other. This is deliberate — Spoor captures request URLs, not response content-types, so the label is best-effort, not a guarantee; but it needs no new capture and stays generic across every target. A timeline view was considered and **not** built: an ordered timeline needs per-request capture-layer timestamps the signal bundle does not carry, so categorization (which the captured URLs already support) shipped instead. (2) **Per-visit signal scoping (supersedes 6c's "whole-run buffer" framing).** 6c collapsed repeated console/network lines but left the underlying defect: the driver's console and network buffers accumulated for the *whole run*, so a state reached late still carried every earlier walk's output (collapse only hid the duplication). `PlaywrightDriver.reset` now **clears both buffers**, so each reset begins a fresh visit and a state's recorded console/network reflects only the walk that reached it — not the session's cumulative output. This is safe for transition diffs: `diff_signals` is computed from a before/after pair straddling a single `perform` with **no reset between them** (confirmed against `explorer.walk`), so clearing at the earlier reset changes only the absolute per-state contents, never a transition's delta. Collapse (6c) still earns its place — a chatty library or polled endpoint repeats within a single visit's reloads. `features/exploration_signals_live.feature` gained a scenario proving a capture after a reset drops a prior click's `/ping.txt` request and `going next` console line while still showing the fresh load's `home ready`; `tests/test_exploration_driver.py` pins the buffer-clear browser-free. `features/exploration_wiki.feature` gained the network-grouping scenario. Generic to every target (§0): the same taxonomy and the same reset scope every target's signals.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6e (state page leads with an Actions table), shipped.** A maintainer-requested presentation rearrangement of the state page, continuing the same "make the wiki read well" thread as 6c/6d; generic (§0), and touching only the renderer's state template — the graph model, the capture layer, and §2h redaction are all untouched. The state page's actionable elements were split across two lists — an "Actions here" bullet list of discovered elements and a separate "Outgoing transitions" list of fired edges — and both sat *below* the captured-signal sections. 6e replaces them with a single **Actions table** (`spoor/exploration/wiki.py:_elements`), one row per discovered element with columns **Label, Type, Screen capture, and Destination / target state**, placed **immediately after the state-identity block (label, id, settle) and ahead of the signal sections**, so the thing a reader acts on comes first. The Destination column folds in what the removed outgoing list carried: each element is joined (by redacted label + role) to the transition it fired, and the cell links to that transition page labelled by the target state, or reads "none" when the element was never fired; defensively, a fired transition whose element is somehow absent from the discovered list still gets a row so no recorded edge is dropped. The **Screen capture** column reads "none" for every element today — Spoor captures no per-element screenshot yet (the deferred visual-capture backlog item in §9), so the column is honest about there being nothing to show rather than implying one exists, and it is the natural slot for that image once the visual-capture work lands. `features/exploration_wiki.feature` gained scenarios pinning the table's presence and its position before the signals, a fired element's destination link, and an unfired element's empty destination. Generic to every target (§0): one table renders every target's elements.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6f (help/glossary page), shipped.** A maintainer-requested addition to the same "make the wiki read well" thread as 6c/6d/6e: a wiki reader who did not build Spoor had no way to learn what a term like "Accessibility nodes" or "Storage keys added" meant. 6f adds a fixed **help/glossary page** (`spoor/exploration/wiki.py:_HELP`, rendered to `help.html`) linked from **every page's nav** via the shared layout. It is written in plain, user-facing language (no internal §-references in the wording, per the audience rule) and defines every term the other pages use, grouped by where it appears — the map (state, state id, transition, overview, skipped actions, the `[REDACTED]` placeholder), a state page (actions and their Label/Type/Screen capture/Destination columns, accessibility nodes, screenshot hash, console messages, storage keys, network requests, the "did not settle" warning), and a transition page (accessibility node delta, screenshot changed, console/storage/network added or removed, "reached from behind a blocker"). The page is **fully static and target-independent**: it carries no captured values, so nothing on it needs redaction, and being identical for every target it holds nothing site-specific (§0). `features/exploration_wiki.feature` gained a scenario pinning that the page exists, defines its terms as glossary (`<dt>`) entries, and is reachable from every page. Presentation-only; the graph, capture layer, and redaction are untouched.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 6g (wiki layout: state and transition pages in their own subfolders), shipped.** The move 8c deferred — 8c tidied the *images* into `screenshots/` but left "transition pages keeping their own subfolder (the maintainer's tentative 'maybe')" backlogged, noting it "would mean rewriting every cross-page link." 6g does exactly that rewrite, now that the screenshot slices (8a–8g) are in, so the whole page set is grouped: state pages under `states/`, transition pages under `transitions/`, `index.html` and `help.html` staying at the root as entry points, and `screenshots/` unchanged. The link rewrite is handled by one uniform scheme rather than per-link special-casing: a page's stored filename is its **root-relative** path (`states/state-0.html`), and each page is rendered with a `root` prefix — `""` for a root page, `"../"` for one in a subfolder — that every nav link, cross-page link, and image `src` is written through, so a reference resolves from wherever its page sits (index links stay prefix-free; a state/transition page climbs one level with `../`). `render_wiki` creates each page's parent folder before writing. A Jinja gotcha handled along the way: a macro can't see the template's render context, so the shared `shot` macro takes `root` as an explicit argument. Purely a layout change — the graph model, capture path, §2h redaction posture, and the opt-in are all untouched, and it stays generic across every target (§0). `features/exploration_wiki.feature` gained two scenarios (every state page under `states/` and every transition page under `transitions/` while index/help stay at root; and the cross-subfolder links — index→state, transition→state, state→screenshot, state→transition, state→index/help — all resolve with the right `../` depth); the wiki unit tests, the six screenshot step files, and the live integration test were updated to the subfolder paths and the one-level-up `src`/`href` prefixes. Generic to every target (§0): one prefix scheme lays out every target's wiki.

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e slice 8 (visual capture): embed screenshots in the wiki, opt-in and staged.** The maintainer asked to finally surface the *pixels* the wiki has only ever hinted at (the hash and the honest "none" screen-capture column of 6e), the §9 "Visual capture" backlog item. Agreed scope, dictated by the maintainer as a four-stage plan so early value doesn't wait on the hard parts: **Stage 1** — one **full-page** screenshot per state, no blurring, captured with Playwright's `page.screenshot(full_page=True)` (agreed "good enough" for now; no hand-rolled scroll-and-stitch, and the acknowledged hard cases — scroll-driven content, videos, carousels, lazy-loaded content — are accepted as out of scope for this stage). **Stage 2** — neutralize animations (e.g. paint a black box over animated regions) for stable, deterministic captures. **Stage 3** — per-**action** capture: for each actionable element, capture what activating it produces (a hamburger control captures the whole menu it opens), filling 6e's Screen-capture column. **Stage 4** — place all captures into the wiki in an understandable layout. Two design forks the maintainer settled up front: **(a) opt-in, off by default** — screenshots are always captured to the **local-only** cache, but *embedding* them in the shared wiki requires an explicit opt-in flag; a default `spoor explore --wiki` run stays pixel-free and §2h-clean. This is the non-negotiable §2h boundary made concrete: a screenshot is **pixels**, and text redaction cannot scrub a secret that is visible on the page (a token in the DOM, PII), so pixels reach a shared surface only via the explicit opt-in export §2h already sanctions for the raw cache, with human curation standing in for redaction; the visual hash stays freely surfaceable (a 64-bit int). **(b)** delivery is **relative-path image files** written next to the static pages (the "image pack" direction in §9), not base64 embedded in the HTML. Built BDD-first in logic-first sub-slices: **8a (shipped)** — the pure renderer only. `build_pages(graph, *, target, screenshots=<set of state ids>)` embeds a state's full-page screenshot as an `<img class="screenshot" src="state-{index}.png">` (`_screenshot_filename`) leading the state page as its visual identity, ahead of the Actions table; `screenshots` defaults to none so a default wiki is pixel-free, and no image bytes, disk, browser, or flag are involved yet. The help glossary gained a "Screenshot" term stating the opt-in/can't-be-redacted rationale in plain language. **8b (shipped)** — the capture-and-write half. `PlaywrightDriver.screenshot()` returns a full-page PNG (`page.screenshot(full_page=True)`, `None` if it can't be captured); `explore(..., screenshots=<sink mapping>)` fills the sink with one image per discovered state, gated behind a separate `@runtime_checkable _ScreenshotCapable` protocol + `isinstance` check so capture is presence-of-sink opt-in and the core `BrowserDriver` contract (and every existing fake) is untouched; `render_wiki(..., screenshots=<state-id→bytes>)` writes each `state-{index}.png` beside the pages and passes the captured ids to `build_pages` to embed them. The `spoor explore --screenshots` flag ties both on (off by default) and is rejected without `--wiki` (there is nowhere to put the images). A live integration test proves real full-page PNG bytes and end-to-end embedding against the bench; a default run stays pixel-free. Stages 2–4 become sub-slices 8c+. Generic to every target (§0): the capture and the template are the same for every site.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 8c (wiki layout: screenshots in their own subfolder), shipped.** The first step of stage 4 ("place all captures into the wiki in an understandable layout"), per the maintainer's direction to stop the images sitting flat among the HTML. `_screenshot_filename(index)` now returns `screenshots/state-{index}.png` (a new `_SCREENSHOT_DIR` constant) instead of a flat `state-{index}.png`; `render_wiki` creates the subfolder before writing each image, and the state pages embed by that subfolder-relative `<img src>` (a page sits at the wiki root, so the relative path resolves). `exploration_screenshots.feature` gained a scenario pinning that a captured image lands under `screenshots/` and that nothing is left flat in the root; the 8a/8b unit and live integration tests assert the subfolder path and `src`. Purely a presentation/layout change — the opt-in, the §2h redaction posture, the capture path, and the graph are all untouched, and it stays generic across every target (§0). Transition *pages* keeping their own subfolder (the maintainer's tentative "maybe") is deferred: it would mean rewriting every cross-page link (index→transition, state→transition, transition→state) and is a larger move than this layout tidy — backlogged, not folded in here.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 8d (per-element screenshots fill the Actions table's Screen-capture column), shipped.** Stage 3 of the slice-8 plan ("per-**action** capture… filling 6e's Screen-capture column"), and the maintainer's "screenshots from every possible element — the currency dropdown, next, …" request. Since 6e the Actions table has reserved a **Screen capture** column that read an honest "none" for every element; 8d captures the image that fills it. A new opt-in `element_screenshots` sink on `explore` (a `MutableMapping[str, list[ElementShot]]`, keyed by state id, aligned by discovery position with the state's actions) is filled at state-discovery time by clipping each discovered element, gated behind a new `@runtime_checkable _ElementScreenshotCapable` protocol + `isinstance` check exactly like 8b's full-page sink — so the core `BrowserDriver` contract and every fake stay untouched and a default run clips nothing. `PlaywrightDriver.element_screenshot(action)` re-locates the element through the **same** `find_target` path discovery and actuation use (never Playwright's separate ARIA engine — the 7a divergence), reads its border box over CDP (`DOM.getBoxModel`), and crops with `Page.captureScreenshot` (`captureBeyondViewport`, device-scale 1 so CSS px = clip px); an element that can't be located/boxed yields `None`, kept in the list so it stays aligned. `render_wiki(..., element_screenshots=…)` writes each clip to `screenshots/state-{i}-el-{e}.png` (the 8c subfolder) and threads the filenames into the existing `_elements`/`_element_row` so the reserved column renders an `<img>` (or "none"). Same §2h posture as every screenshot — pixels reach the shared wiki only behind the explicit opt-in, because a picture can't be secret-redacted the way text is. `exploration_element_screenshots.feature` pins capture on/off, per-element write-and-embed aligned to each row, the `screenshots/` subfolder, and pixel-free-by-default; unit tests pin position-alignment with a `None` clip mid-list; a live integration test clips real element PNGs against the bench and asserts every written clip is embedded on a state page. Generic to every target (§0). **Followed by 8e** (below): capturing each element's *opened/revealed contents* (open a dropdown, screenshot what it reveals, restore) — the second column the maintainer sketched ("its contents… in a separate column") — shipped next on this same branch.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 8e (opened-contents screenshots fill a new "Opened contents" column), shipped.** The second half of the maintainer's "the currency dropdown *and its contents*… in a separate column" request, completing stage 3 of the slice-8 plan on the same `wiki-screenshots` branch as 8d. Where 8d clips each element as it sits *closed*, 8e captures what a **disclosure** element reveals when *opened*, and the Actions table gains a new **Opened contents** column beside the closed-clip column, one image per element row. `ElementShot` gained an `opened: bytes | None` field (the closed clip stays `clip`); a new `@runtime_checkable _OpenedScreenshotCapable` protocol (separate from 8d's `_ElementScreenshotCapable`, so a clip-only driver — every 8d fake — still matches and simply leaves `opened` None) exposes `opened_screenshot(action)`. `PlaywrightDriver.opened_screenshot` clicks the element via the same relocate-and-hit-test actuation path (ACTUATE only), settles, takes a full-page shot of the revealed overlay, then presses **Escape** to restore — best-effort, `None` on any failure. Two safety fences make this mutating capture sound: **(1)** the explorer fires it only for a role in `_DISCLOSURE_ROLES` (`combobox`/`listbox` — a subset of discovery's `ACTIONABLE_ROLES`, since a role never discovered could never be opened; `menu` was dropped as it is not an actionable role) that the **safety gate also permits**, so opening never actuates a destructive element on a non-sandbox target (§2e non-negotiable — verified by a scenario using a real, non-loopback target and a destructively-labelled dropdown); and **(2)** it runs **after** the clean-page state id, signals, and every closed clip are already recorded, and the next driver call is always a reset-and-replay, so the click never contaminates the mapped graph (confirmed against `explorer.capture`/`walk`: `capture` computes the id and signals from the clean page first, and every path out of `capture` resets before the next actuation). `render_wiki` writes each opened image to `screenshots/state-{i}-el-{e}-opened.png` (a shared `_write_element_image` helper handles both the 8d clip and the 8e opened pass identically), and threads the filenames through `build_pages`/`_elements`/`_element_row` into the new column; the help glossary describes it. Same §2h posture — pixels reach the shared wiki only behind the same single `--screenshots` opt-in, off by default. `exploration_opened_screenshots.feature` pins: an opened capture for a disclosure element, none for a non-disclosure element (a button), none for a destructive disclosure element outside a sandbox, none when capture is off, none from a clip-only driver, and per-row write-and-embed under the subfolder; a unit test pins that clip and opened images are tracked independently (one element can have both, another neither); the live path is proven two ways: a `@browser` scenario (`exploration_opened_screenshots_live.feature`) drives the real `PlaywrightDriver` against a loopback ARIA-combobox fixture — opening it, capturing the revealed in-DOM option list, and Escape-restoring, asserting a real PNG that differs from the closed page and a page left closed (deterministic, no dependence on bench content) — and an integration test opens a real disclosure element against the bench and asserts the opened PNG is embedded, skipping honestly if the small budget reaches none. Generic to every target (§0).

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 8f (stream each screenshot to disk as it is captured; hold only a reference), shipped.** A memory fix the maintainer required before comprehensive screenshotted crawls were possible: the 8b/8d/8e sinks held every captured image's **bytes** in memory until `render_wiki` wrote them all out at the end, so a deep run with `--screenshots` accumulated the whole run's pixels in RAM and OOM-killed a comprehensive crawl. 8f inverts the ownership: each capture is **written to disk the instant it is taken**, and the sinks hold only the subfolder-relative **filename reference**, never the bytes. `explore` gained a required `screenshot_dir: Path` (validated non-None whenever either screenshot sink is passed, and in the CLI set to the `--wiki` directory so images and the pages that embed them land together); a new `_stream_capture(data, screenshot_dir, name)` helper writes one capture under `screenshots/<name>` as it is produced (creating the subfolder on first write) and returns the stored name, or None for a capture the driver couldn't take — so the sink stays honest about a missing image. The sink types changed accordingly: `screenshots: MutableMapping[str, str]` (state id → filename) and `ElementShot.clip`/`.opened` are now `str | None` filename references. `render_wiki` **no longer writes any image bytes** — it writes only HTML that embeds the references the explorer already streamed to disk (which is why `screenshot_dir` must equal `render_wiki`'s `out_dir`); it therefore never holds a growing image buffer either. A circular import (wiki.py imports `ElementShot` from explorer.py) forced the screenshot-naming helpers (`_screenshot_filename`, `_element_screenshot_filename`, `_element_opened_filename`, `_SCREENSHOT_SUBDIR`) to move from wiki.py to explorer.py, where the only code that computes a filename (the capture path) now lives; wiki.py embeds whatever reference the shot carries. The capture-time state index is `len(graph.states) - 1` — the new state's insertion position, which equals the renderer's `state-{index}` enumeration (`graph.states` returns the states in insertion order), so the streamed filename and the embedded `<img src>` always agree. The §2h posture is unchanged (screenshots opt-in, off by default; pixels reach shared output only via the explicit `--screenshots` opt-in), and 8e's invariant holds (opened-capture still runs only after the clean state id/signals/clips are recorded, gate-permitted disclosure roles only). `exploration_screenshots.feature`, `exploration_element_screenshots.feature`, and their opened counterpart each gained a scenario pinning that a capture is a file reference on disk (not bytes in the sink) the moment it is taken; the 8a-era pure-renderer unit tests became reference-embedding tests (render writes no `.png`), and the three live integration tests pass `screenshot_dir` and assert the real PNG bytes round-trip **on disk** under the reference. Generic to every target (§0): the same stream-and-reference path runs for every site.

**Decision — §2e sub-slice 8g (deduplicate screenshots and store a clip as a crop of its page), shipped.** The waste 8f's streaming exposed next: a comprehensive live crawl wrote the **same picture** to disk many times — one run produced 2,409 element clips against just 40 full-page shots, nearly every clip a region of the page it came from, and states that render identically each wrote their own copy. 8g removes both redundancies so the wiki refers to one shared picture. **Two mechanisms, both deterministic and generic (§0 — pure image maths, nothing site-specific).** *(1) Duplicate detection.* A new `ScreenshotStore` (one per run, over the screenshot dir) routes every capture through `add(data, preferred_name) -> ImageRef`: a **byte-exact** duplicate (SHA-256 `content_key`) reuses the existing file; a **near-identical** capture (the same screen re-rendered with only anti-aliasing/cursor noise) is matched by the tier-3 dHash `perceptual_hash` within a tight **3-bit** Hamming distance (`_NEAR_DUPLICATE_BITS`) and reuses it too; otherwise the bytes are written and remembered. A **flat** image (dHash 0) is matched byte-exactly *only*, never perceptually, so distinct blank screens are never collapsed together. This never merges two **states** — only the image a state's page embeds is shared; the state pages stay separate (the graph identity is still the DOM `state_id`). *(2) Sub-image containment.* A clip that is a region of its state's full-page shot is stored as a **crop reference** into that picture — no file of its own. The element's on-page geometry is used first: a new opt-in `_ElementBoxCapable` protocol exposes `element_box(action) -> (x,y,w,h)|None` (the live `PlaywrightDriver` reuses its `DOM.getBoxModel` border-box path), and `_contained_box` **verifies** the hint by comparing the container region at that origin, sized to the clip's *own* decoded dimensions, pixel-for-pixel (robust to sub-pixel rounding); when the hint is absent or fails, the clip is located by exact pixel search (`find_subimage`, topmost-leftmost, candidate-bounded). Only a clip in no bigger picture is written (deduped) as its own file. **Type shape (supersedes 8f):** the bare `str` filename references become a frozen `ImageRef(src, box)` — `src` the subfolder-relative filename, `box` an optional `(x,y,w,h)` crop of it, `None` meaning the whole file; sinks are now `MutableMapping[str, ImageRef]` and `ElementShot.clip`/`.opened` are `ImageRef | None`. 8f's `_stream_capture` helper is replaced by the store's `add`. New pure module `spoor/exploration/dedup.py` holds the deterministic primitives (`content_key`, `decode`→`UndecodableImage`, `find_subimage`); `screenshot_store.py` holds the stateful writer; the explorer decodes the container once per state and resolves each clip via `_resolve_clip`/`_contained_box`. The wiki renders a crop reference as a CSS-cropped `<span class="screenshot-crop" … background-image:url('…') background-position:-x -y>` (a whole-file ref stays an `<img>`), via a shared `_image_view`/`shot` macro. **§2h posture unchanged:** dedup and containment only ever touch pixels a caller already opted into capturing, an `ImageRef` carries nothing but a filename and integers, and a default run still writes no images. Built BDD-first — `exploration_screenshot_dedup.feature` pins seven scenarios (identical screens share one file; near-identical share one file perceptually; clearly-different stay separate; a contained clip via geometry and via pixel search each become a crop reference with no own file; an uncontained clip gets its own file; a re-run writes the identical file set) with **real-PNG Pillow fakes** so decode/hash/search run for real; the fast, browser, and integration tiers stay green, and the live integration assertion was corrected to embed each state's *own* reference (dedup may point a later state at an earlier state's file) rather than assuming `state-{index}.png`. Generic to every target (§0): the same dedup-and-crop path runs for every site.

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e slice 9 (breadth-first traversal + depth budget + URL-path frontier): peel the site like an onion.** The maintainer observed that shallow, high-value pages — a shop's top-level categories (`/4-men`, `/9-art`), brand pages, the cart view — were missing from real maps, while deep product/variant tendrils were mapped. A throwaway diagnostic against a live PrestaShop bench (127.0.0.1:8080, not committed) corrected the assumed cause and grounded this slice: **(a)** the home page exposes **89 `link` nodes / 35 distinct first-layer paths** — every "missing" page (`/4-men`, `/9-art`, `/7-stationery`, `/brand/…`, `/cart?action=show`) is one click from home and *is* discovered as a gate-permitted `link`; **(b)** all six probed pages, including `/7-stationery` vs. its `?q=Availability-In+stock` filter, produced **six distinct `state_id`s** — so state-abstraction collapse is *not* why they were missed. The real cause is **traversal order**: slice 5a's walk is depth-first (it recurses into `first_seen` immediately), so it dives into the first product branch and spends the budget deep before unwinding to sibling categories. Confirmed: it is ordering + budget, not discovery and not collapse. Three agreed changes, one slice: **(1) Breadth-first traversal** — replace the recursive DFS walk with a FIFO frontier of `(state_id, path)` so the crawl peels layer 0 (home) fully, then everything one click away, then layer 2, maximizing coverage of shallow structural pages for any budget. Reset-and-replay, verification (7d), and layer recovery (7c) are unchanged — only the order states are dequeued for expansion changes. **(2) `--max-depth` budget** — a first-class "don't go past the Nth layer" knob (added to `RunBudget`, validated positive): a state at depth *d* is *expanded* (its actions fired) only when *d* < `max_depth`, so reached/screenshotted states go up to depth `max_depth` but the crawl doesn't descend further; unbounded when unset. Unlike the states/requests/seconds bounds (which *stop* the run via `controller.check()`), `max_depth` *prunes* the frontier and never stops the run — documented as such. **(3) URL path forks the frontier** — the frontier's already-expanded key becomes `(state_id, url_path)` where `url_path` is the URL **path only** (query and fragment dropped), read from a new `BrowserDriver.current_url()` (trivially `page.url` on the live driver; the diagnostic confirmed it is available and correct after navigation, but it was previously unexposed and uncaptured). **Graph identity stays the DOM `state_id`** (the maintainer's explicit choice): one node and one screenshot per DOM hash, so near-duplicate pages never multiply in the wiki. The fork only changes *what gets expanded*: a distinct URL path that renders an already-seen DOM is still walked outward, while `?q=…`/`#…` variants of a path already expanded are not re-expanded. Honest scope: the diagnostic showed real catalog pages already differ by DOM, so this fork is a **narrow safety net** for the "different URL, identical DOM" case (e.g. two empty listing pages), included now at the maintainer's direction rather than because those named pages needed it. Non-negotiables untouched: the safety gate still decides every fire (§2e), reset-and-replay stays verified (7d), and the mechanism is generic to every target (§0). Built BDD-first; in-process fake-driver scenarios pin BFS order (a budget that reaches shallow siblings under BFS where DFS would reach a deep descendant), the depth bound (expansion stops at `max_depth`, deeper states unreached), and the URL fork (a distinct path with an identical DOM is still expanded; a query-only variant is not).

**Decision — §2e slice 9b (destination-hint prioritisation: peel the onion in priority order), shipped.** Folded onto the slice-9 `explore-breadth-first` branch at the maintainer's direction, after the observation that a front-page link carries a real destination in its `href` (`ART` → `/9-art`) — a free signal already on the page. Slice 9 made the walk breadth-first, so the *layers* are peeled in order; but within a layer the actions still fired in discovery (document) order, so under a tight budget a deep-linking element discovered first (`/x/y/z`) could consume the budget before a shallow, top-level sibling (`/a`). 9b orders each state's actions by their **destination depth** — the number of non-empty path segments in the link's target URL — shallowest first, so structural, high-value pages are tried before deep tendrils *within* each BFS layer too. Three deliberate bounds keep it safe and generic: **(1)** the destination is a **hint, not a contract** — `ActionableElement.destination: str | None` (defaulted `None`, so every existing construction is unaffected); a button, a JS control, or an unresolved target has no destination and sorts **after** every known one (`_NO_DESTINATION_DEPTH`), falling back to plain discovery order via a **stable** sort, so nothing that used to be tried stops being tried. **(2)** It changes **reach order only** — the graph's stored action order is untouched, so the 8d/8e per-element and opened-contents screenshot alignment (indexed by discovery position) is preserved exactly. **(3)** It is site-agnostic (§0): depth is counted from the URL path structure, no host or path string is special-cased. The live driver reads the hint generically — `ax_nodes()` now issues one extra `DOM.getDocument` (pierced) to build a backend-node-id → `href` map, resolves each anchor's href against the live `page.url`, and reduces it to a URL path; empty hrefs, in-page fragments (`#…`), and non-navigational schemes (`javascript:`/`mailto:`/`tel:`) carry no destination. Built BDD-first: `exploration_discovery.feature` pins that an enriched node's destination is read (and a button's is `None`); `exploration_traversal.feature` pins that a shallow-destination link is tried before a deep one under a 2-state budget; and `exploration_traversal_live.feature` gained a `@browser` scenario proving the real `PlaywrightDriver` reads a link's `href` and reports it as a URL-path destination. Generic to every target (§0); no non-negotiable touched — the safety gate still decides every fire, ordering only changes which permitted action is tried first.

**Decision — user-configurable clickable-element rules (closes #152).** docs/COMPETITIVE_PLAN.md flagged Crawljax's clickable-element rules (incl. exclusions) as worth adapting: an explicit, operator-set allow/exclude list for which actionable elements exploration attempts, in the §1 "not an agent" spirit — a rule the operator wrote, never an inferred heuristic. `spoor/exploration/element_rules.py` adds `ElementRules(include: tuple[str, ...] | None, exclude: tuple[str, ...] | None)`, matched case-sensitively (`fnmatch.fnmatchcase`, the same choice the scoped-crawl `include`/`exclude` fields make, for the same reason: a pattern means exactly what it says) against `ActionableElement.name` — the visible accessible label, the one identifier every discovered element has regardless of role, rather than role or a DOM selector exploration has no equivalent of for AX-tree-discovered elements. `exclude` wins when both match (same composition rule as the scoped-crawl fields); unset (the default) leaves every element a candidate, today's behavior unchanged. Wired into `explorer.py`'s main firing loop only — the one site where a discovered action becomes (or doesn't become) a graph transition — via a new optional `element_rules` parameter on `explore`/`resume_exploration`, checked and skip-recorded (with a distinct reason) immediately before the existing `evaluate_action` safety-gate check, never instead of it. **Deliberately independent of, and unable to relax, the §2e sandbox-only destructive-action non-negotiable**: an `--include-element` pattern that happens to match a destructive label grants it nothing — both checks must separately allow an action before it fires, pinned by its own scenario (`features/exploration_element_rules.feature`). **Scope decision:** the rule applies only to the main firing loop, not to the opt-in "what a dropdown reveals" screenshot capture or to a layer-recovery candidate search — excluding an element from the mapped graph must not also stop it from being used internally to clear a layer blocking some *other*, included action (pinned by its own scenario). `spoor explore` gains repeatable `--include-element`/`--exclude-element` CLI flags (no YAML config object exists for exploration today, unlike the scoped-crawl fields, so this lives as CLI-only state built into an `ElementRules` right before the walk starts, the same shape `RunBudget` already takes). Generic to every target (§0): the pattern-matching mechanism is the same for every site; nothing site-specific is in the rule itself.

**Follow-up — `@<file>` pattern lists (maintainer-requested, same issue).** A real site's rule set can run to dozens of patterns, which repeated CLI flags handle poorly. Rather than adding a full exploration config file (a bigger surface, and exploration's whole pitch is "point it at a URL with no config"), `cli.py` adds a narrower `@<file>` convention to the *same* two flags: a value starting with `@` is read as a file of patterns, one per line (`#` comments and blank lines skipped), instead of being a literal pattern — `_read_element_patterns_file`/`_expand_element_patterns`. Literal and `@file` values mix freely across repeated uses of one flag. Both a missing file and a file that resolves to zero patterns (every line blank/a comment — almost certainly a mistake, not an intentional "match nothing") are refused up front with a `typer.BadParameter`, before any browser is launched, the same posture every other `spoor explore` input validation already takes (`--session`, `--resume-from`, `--screenshots`). `ElementRules` itself is unchanged — this is CLI-layer plumbing only, so no new `.feature` scenario; covered instead by plain `CliRunner` tests in `tests/test_cli.py`, matching how the existing `--session` file-threading and missing-file-rejection tests are done there rather than in Gherkin.

**Decomposition (agreed) — §2e slice 7 (exploration robustness): settle first, then recover.** Slice 6b closed by backlogging "make a dynamic SPA's home actions actually actuate (dismiss splash/cookie overlays before discovery)" as an exploration-productivity concern. Before building it, a live diagnostic against the §5.1 Juice Shop bench (throwaway, not committed) corrected the assumption behind that note. The dominant reason Juice Shop skips on `spoor explore` is **not** an overlay intercepting the click: the failing control (`Close Welcome Banner`) was present, visible, uncovered, and not inert every time it was inspected, and it clicked fine with the existing `get_by_role(name, exact=True)` locator *once the page had settled*. The real causes are (a) **unsettled, nondeterministic state** — discovered-element counts varied 4 → 6 → 7 → 22 across identical runs, because the Angular app renders the welcome dialog, the cookie bar, and a "language changed" snackbar asynchronously *after* `networkidle`, and some auto-dismiss — and (b) the resulting **relocation race**: reset-and-replay reads and acts against a half-rendered DOM, so an element found in one snapshot may be absent or not-yet-actionable by click time. This directly breaks the reset-and-replay determinism the whole explorer rests on. Genuine modal interception (a `cdk-overlay-backdrop` making underlying elements unclickable) is a real but *smaller and rarer* category — under a modal the background is usually `aria-hidden`/inert, so those elements are not even discovered.

So slice 7 is built in three BDD-first sub-slices, in order, each generic to every target (§0) and none weakening a non-negotiable — Juice Shop is only the *witness* that the generic mechanism works, never a special case in the code.

**7a — robust actuation (the deepest fix).** The `Close Welcome Banner` skip was a *relocation* failure, not timing: discovery reads the element from the **CDP accessibility tree**, but `perform` re-finds it through Playwright's *separate* ARIA name engine, and the two compute the accessible name differently, so `get_by_role(name, exact=True)` matched nothing on an element that was visible and uncovered. The fix keeps one engine end to end: at act time, re-read the **live CDP tree** (the same source discovery used, so names are consistent by construction), find the target node, scroll it into view, and actuate by **coordinates with a real mouse click** — trusted CDP `Input` events, which work identically headless and headed. Coordinates are never stored or guessed: the viewport is pinned (fixed size, device-scale-factor 1, so layout is reproducible), the box centre is read *live* from the current element, and `elementFromPoint(centre)` **verifies** the point resolves to the target before any click. If it resolves to something else, that is a precise, generic signal the element is genuinely covered (→ 7c) or mislocated (→ flagged) — a coordinate is computed, confirmed against the DOM, then used, never trusted blind.

*Shipped.* `spoor/exploration/actuation.py` holds the pure verdict (`classify` → ACTUATE / COVERED / NOT LOCATED) and the shared relocator (`find_target`, which runs `discover_actions` itself so act-time and discovery-time matching cannot diverge); `PlaywrightDriver.perform` re-reads the live CDP tree, resolves the node via `DOM.resolveNode`, and runs scroll-into-view + centre + `elementFromPoint` in one `Runtime.callFunctionOn` (so the point verified is the point clicked), then clicks it with Playwright's trusted, CDP-backed `page.mouse.click` — equivalent trusted input, headless or headed. COVERED raises `ElementCovered(role, text)` (carrying the layer, for 7c) and NOT LOCATED raises `ElementNotLocated`; both subclass `ActionError`, so today the explorer still records either as a skip until 7c catches `ElementCovered`. The context is pinned to a fixed 1280×800 viewport at device-scale 1. Pinned by `features/exploration_actuation.feature` (pure verdict) and `features/exploration_actuation_live.feature` (real browser: aria-label-only icon button clicked, below-the-fold element scrolled and clicked, covered element detected without activating the overlay, fixed viewport). First-cut limitations recorded honestly: duplicate role+name acts on the first in document order, and an element hosted in an iframe is not reached by the top document's `elementFromPoint`.

**7b — state settling (quiescence).** Before the explorer reads a state (for `state_id`, discovery, signal capture) or returns from an action, wait for the page to reach a stable rendering — DOM quiescence (no mutations for a quiet window) via a `MutationObserver`, i.e. wait for a *real signal that rendering stopped*, not a fixed sleep. A settle timeout is only a bounded safety net; if a page never settles the explorer **records that as a property of the state** rather than silently proceeding as if it had. The quiescence *policy* is pure logic behind an injectable clock (the `RunController` seam), testable in-process; the live glue (the observer + a bounded poll) is thin. This restores the reset-and-replay determinism the whole explorer rests on (discovered-element counts stop varying run to run).

*Shipped, with one scoping decision taken with the maintainer.* The live diagnostic exposed **two** coupled defects behind the shallow map, not one, so 7b delivers **settling + reset fidelity** together: (1) *Settling.* `spoor/exploration/settling.py` holds the pure quiescence decision — `wait_for_quiescence(*, observe, clock, sleep, quiet_window, timeout, poll_interval) -> SettleResult` settles once a monotonic mutation count holds steady for the quiet window and reports **unsettled** at a bounded timeout — driven by injectable `clock`/`sleep` (the `RunController` seam), so it is exercised deterministically with no browser (`features/exploration_settling.feature`). The live glue in `PlaywrightDriver` installs a `MutationObserver` mutation counter on every document (`add_init_script`) and a `_wait_for_settle()` polls it after each reset and each click; `state_html`/`ax_nodes`/`capture_signals` are called by the explorer right after those, so both reads and post-action returns see the settled page. A never-settling page is recorded — `StateSignals` gained a defaulted `settled: bool = True` that the driver flips to `False` on timeout, surfaced in the wiki as a "Did not settle" flag — and the run **proceeds on the last snapshot**; the earlier code path crashed (a reset that never reached `networkidle` raised straight through, aborting the run) or silently proceeded. (2) *Reset fidelity (the maintainer's decision).* A single browser context persists cookies and web storage across navigations, so a plain re-navigation lands on a *returning-visitor* render and reset-and-replay diverges from what discovery saw. `reset` now **clears cookies and both web-storage areas before navigating** (one context kept; only per-origin state wiped) and navigates with `wait_until="domcontentloaded"` + the settle wait rather than `networkidle` — so every reset is a true first visit and the `networkidle` crash is gone. Pinned live by `features/exploration_settling_live.feature` (reset clears cookies+storage → first-visit content; content rendered after the load event is discovered; a forever-mutating page is captured flagged unsettled, not fatal) and browser-free by `tests/test_exploration_driver.py` (post-click settle poll; reset clears cookies+storage then navigates, under a fake clock). Generic to every target (§0): one quiescence rule and one reset for every site; the fixtures merely stand in for persisted state, deferred render, and endless churn.

**7c — layer recovery.** For the genuine-interception category (7a's `elementFromPoint` check proved the target is covered), treat the blocking layer as its own state and interact past it toward making the originally-targeted action reachable — safety-gated (every engagement passes `evaluate_action`, so a layer whose only exit is a destructive action stays blocked outside a sandbox), bounded by *progress* (converge over steps, not a fixed retry), flagged with a real reason when it cannot be resolved (never a mute skip, never a loop), and with coordinate-level interaction available where DOM engagement can't clear it. `features/exploration_recovery.feature` already pins 7c's contract; 7a and 7b get their own features.

*Shipped.* The recovery is pure orchestration in `spoor/exploration/explorer.py`, browser-agnostic like the rest of the loop. A new `BrowserDriver.probe(action) -> ActuationVerdict` returns the actuation verdict (ACTUATE / COVERED / NOT_LOCATED) *without* clicking — `PlaywrightDriver.probe` and `.perform` now share one `_actuation` computation, so a probe can never disagree with the click that follows it. When `reach(action)` probes COVERED, it interacts past the layer with `next_layer_action`: the layer's *own* on-top actions are the discovered actions that themselves `probe` ACTUATE, gate-permitted and each fired at most once; after each it re-probes the target, converging when the layer clears (single-click), advances (multi-step), or the target uncovers, and giving up when no untried permitted on-top action remains (`_MAX_RECOVERY_STEPS` is a defensive cap, not the primary bound — the finite action set is). Because recovery runs inside `navigate`'s replay too, a covered state stays reachable on every reset-and-replay, not just the first visit. The §2e non-negotiable holds unchanged: a covering layer's destructive-only exit fails the gate, yields no candidate, and the covered action is flagged `blocked by an unresolved layer` (the destructive layer action itself is recorded as a skip); a genuinely missing element (NOT_LOCATED) is flagged `not located`, never mistaken for a blocker. Recovered edges are marked (`Transition.recovered_via`) and surfaced in the wiki ("Reached from behind a blocker"). Pinned browser-free by `features/exploration_recovery.feature` (blocker cleared with both actions behind it mapped; multi-step blocker worked through; destructive-only-exit stays blocked outside a sandbox; an unresolvable blocker flagged while exploration carries on elsewhere; a missing element flagged not-located) and live by its final scenario against `fixtures/static/explore_gated.html` (a full-viewport consent overlay intercepting every click → the site behind it is mapped and ≥1 action is recovered). Generic to every target (§0): the same discover + gate + probe recovers past every overlay; the fixture merely stands in for a launch dialog. *Coordinate-level interaction where DOM engagement can't clear a layer is deferred:* recovery drives the layer through its own discoverable actions, which cleared every overlay met so far; a layer with no discoverable clearing action is flagged blocked rather than brute-forced, and that limitation is recorded here honestly. Two further honest limitations: the clicks recovery fires to clear a layer are **not** counted against the run's request budget (the budget is checked once per walked action, and recovery is bounded per action by the finite layer-action set, so it adds only a constant factor — but the recorded request count understates the real click count on a heavily-overlayed target); and the kill switch is honoured at the next walked action, not mid-recovery (recovery is short and bounded, so this is at most a few extra clicks).

**7d — replay resilience (retry + fidelity check).** 7b restored reset-and-replay determinism for the *common* case (settling stopped discovered-element counts drifting run to run), but a follow-on diagnostic against the Juice Shop bench showed it did not eliminate the failure: under load the same reset still intermittently lands on a *degraded* render (Angular's "Force page reload" chunk-load fallback appears sporadically; the discovered action set flips 6 ↔ 7 even after settling). Reset-and-replay is all-or-nothing — one flaky step on the way to a leaf killed the whole subtree behind it — and the skip was mis-attributed to the leaf action rather than the replay step that actually failed. This slice makes replay resilient *and* honest.

*Shipped.* Purely in `spoor/exploration/explorer.py`, browser-agnostic like the rest of the loop; the `BrowserDriver` protocol is unchanged (it reuses 7c's `probe`/`reach` and `reset`). The replay path now carries the state id each step first reached (`_PathStep(action, to_state)`), so every reset-and-replay is **verified**: after `reset` the fresh page must equal the root state id, and each replayed step must land on the exact state id it first mapped to — otherwise the attempt raises an internal `_ReplayFailure`. `navigate_and_reach` **retries** a failed attempt up to `_MAX_REPLAY_ATTEMPTS` (3): a transient degraded render usually clears on the next reset, so coverage all-or-nothing replay used to lose is regained. When the attempts are spent the action is recorded as a skip with a reason that distinguishes a step that **stayed unreachable** (`replay could not reach …`) from a replay that **diverged** to a different state than first mapped (`replay diverged: …`) — never a mute skip, and attributed to the replay step that failed, not the leaf. A stably BLOCKED layer (7c's concern) is returned at once, not retried — there is nothing transient to wait out. Pinned browser-free by `features/exploration_replay.feature` (a transient miss retried so the state behind it is still mapped; a persistent miss flagged as an unreachable replay step; a replay landing on the wrong state flagged as divergence; a transient divergence retried) and live by its final scenario against a scripted loopback server whose entry page is served degraded on exactly one reset (retry meets a good render on the next reset and maps the page behind the flaky one). Generic to every target (§0): one verify-and-retry rule for every site; the fixtures merely stand in for a degraded render. Honest limitations recorded: (1) the fidelity check trades silent divergence for either retry-recovery or an explicit divergence-skip — on a genuinely jittery-render site where a step legitimately lands on different-but-valid states this could *reduce* recorded coverage versus the old ignore-divergence behaviour, so the effect on the Juice Shop bench is to be measured and reported, not assumed; (2) retry re-navigations are **not** counted against the run's request budget (same accounting gap as 7c recovery — the budget is checked once per walked action; retries add a bounded constant factor but the recorded request count understates the real navigation count on a flaky target); (3) the kill switch is honoured at the next walked action, not mid-retry (a retry loop is bounded by `_MAX_REPLAY_ATTEMPTS`, so at most a few extra navigations); (4) the *initial* reset at the start of the walk is not itself retried — if the very first render of the root is degraded the whole run starts from a poisoned root, which is out of scope here (a first-visit concern, not a replay one).

*Measured on the PrestaShop bench (7d limitation (1), reported not assumed).* Running `spoor explore` against the live PrestaShop storefront (§5.1) produced **6 states, 32 transitions, and 17 skips** — and **every one of the 17 skips is the 7d fidelity check** reporting `replay diverged: …` on the ever-present top-menu links (`Prices drop`, `HOME`). Root-caused with a normalized-DOM diff, not a guess: the target's *individual* pages are deterministic (the home page and the prices-drop page each hash to one stable `state_id` across every reset, direct navigation, and short click-path — normalization correctly absorbs PrestaShop's per-load randomness: random menu element ids, `static_token`, timestamps). The **listing pages are not**: `home → New products` lands on one `state_id` on most resets but intermittently on another. Diffing the two normalized structures (982 vs 1013 tokens) shows the difference is a JS-driven region that has not finished hydrating at capture time — one variant carries an extra `span[role=status]` (an ARIA live region / loading indicator), an extra `div[aria-disabled=false]` interactive block, and a different count of product `article` tiles. These are *genuinely different DOMs carrying state-bearing attributes* (`role`, `aria-disabled`), so state abstraction correctly refuses to merge them and the fidelity check correctly flags the replayed click as divergent. This is exactly the coverage-vs-fidelity tradeoff 7d predicted, now confirmed on a real multi-page site: the check behaved as designed (it converted a silent divergence into an honest skip) but capped the map at 6 states and cost 17 actions. The honest fix is **stronger capture-time quiescence detection** — wait for ARIA `role=status` live regions to clear and for the listing widget's DOM to stop mutating (a DOM-quiet + network-idle gate at capture) so both firings hash the fully-settled screen — *not* loosening state abstraction (ignoring `role=status`) or the fidelity check (accepting "any known state"), either of which would mask genuine divergence elsewhere. That is its own BDD-first §2e capture-settling slice (extends the 7b settling work); this note records the measurement, not a code change — the fix is **delivered as sub-slice 7e below**.

**Sub-slice 7e — settling also waits for the network to go idle.** *Shipped.* The fix the 7d PrestaShop measurement above pointed at, done at the source rather than by loosening state abstraction or the fidelity check. The pure quiescence decision (`spoor/exploration/settling.py`) gained an optional `busy: Callable[[], bool]` signal: the page counts as quiet only while `busy()` is false *and* DOM mutations have held steady for the quiet window, so a late network response that will still mutate the DOM cannot be settled past. Omitting `busy` is exactly 7b's DOM-only quiet (default `_never_busy`), so nothing that doesn't supply a network signal changes. `PlaywrightDriver` supplies it: it counts in-flight requests (up on `request`, down on `requestfinished`/`requestfailed`, floored at zero, and zeroed just before each awaited navigation and click so a leaked request can't wedge the count high) and passes `busy=lambda: self._inflight > 0` into the settle wait. The bound is unchanged and symmetric with 7b: a request that never completes (a long-poll, a hung fetch) makes the page *unsettled* at the safety timeout, exactly like a page that mutates forever — recorded on the state's `settled` flag, run proceeds on the last snapshot, never hangs. Pinned browser-free by `features/exploration_settling_network.feature` (an in-flight request holds the page unsettled until it completes, then settles under timeout; a request that never completes is reported unsettled at the timeout) and live by its third scenario (a loopback server whose entry page fetches a fragment the server delays past the quiet window — DOM-quiet alone would settle during the delay and miss it; waiting for the network to go idle means the fetched button is discovered). Generic to every target (§0): one network-and-DOM quiet rule for every site; the fixture merely stands in for a slow asynchronously-loaded page. *Measured effect on the bench:* re-running `spoor explore` against PrestaShop with 7e gave **7 states, 40 transitions, 0 skips** — against 7d's **6 states, 32 transitions, 17 skips** on the same target. All 17 divergence-skips are gone *and* coverage rose (more states and transitions mapped, since actions that were being skipped now fire), at a modest added settle wait (~0.75s per listing page, from the in-flight reproduction). The coverage 7d had been conceding to the capture-timing race is recovered. Honest limitations recorded: (1) the in-flight signal treats *all* requests alike — a site with a genuinely endless background request (analytics long-poll, a keep-alive stream, server-sent events) will now reach the settle timeout and be flagged unsettled where 7b's DOM-only quiet might have settled it, a deliberate trade of a slower, honestly-flagged capture for a correct one (the timeout bounds the cost); (2) the in-flight count is zeroed before each awaited action to prevent leaks, so a request still outstanding from *before* that action is not waited on — acceptable because each action's own settle waits out the requests it causes, which is what determines the state it lands on; (3) settle waits are still not charged against the run's request budget (the same accounting gap noted for 7c/7d).

**Sub-slice 7f — settling also waits out urgent live-region announcements.** *Shipped.* A third capture-timing race, found the same way 7d/7e were, this time on the Juice Shop bench (§5.1). The entry page renders, goes DOM-quiet *and* network-idle for several seconds, and only then removes a transient toast it showed on load — an `aria-live="assertive"` Material snackbar that auto-dismisses on a timer, with no network involved. So 7b's DOM quiet and 7e's network-idle both fire during that long lull: one capture includes the toast, a later one does not, the two land on different `state_id`s, and reset-and-replay (7d) flags a spurious "reset did not return to the start state" and skips the action. The measured symptom was a graph shape that reproduced across several main runs (`6/10/3/150`) and then drifted (`6/11/6/147`) on a run whose timing straddled the ~5.6s dismissal — a capture-timing race surfacing as apparent nondeterminism, not real drift, and it was the drift gate on the perf bench (§5.5) that caught it. The pure quiescence decision (`spoor/exploration/settling.py`) gained a second optional signal alongside 7e's `busy`: an `announcing: Callable[[], bool]` predicate. The page counts as quiet only while *neither* `busy()` nor `announcing()` is true and mutations have held steady for the window, so an urgent announcement still on screen holds the wait open exactly like an in-flight request does. `PlaywrightDriver` supplies it by querying for a non-empty `[aria-live="assertive"], [role="alert"]` element and passing `announcing=…` into the settle wait. The predicate is deliberately narrow (§0-clean — it keys on the ARIA standard, not on Juice Shop): `assertive`/`alert` mark an interrupting, by-nature transient announcement, whereas `polite`/`status` regions are commonly *durable* status — Juice Shop's own persistent cookie-consent banner is `aria-live="polite"`, and a broad "any live region" predicate was verified to hold the landing page unsettled forever (a regression, not a fix). The bound is unchanged and symmetric with 7b/7e: an assertive region that never clears makes the page *unsettled* at the safety timeout, recorded on the state's `settled` flag, run proceeds on the last snapshot, never hangs. Omitting `announcing` (the default) is exactly 7b/7e behaviour, so nothing that doesn't supply an announcement signal changes. Pinned browser-free by `features/exploration_settling_announcements.feature` (an assertive announcement showing until 600 ms holds the page unsettled past the quiet window then settles under timeout; one that never clears is reported unsettled at the timeout) and live by its `@browser` scenario (a loopback server whose entry page shows an assertive toast carrying a button and removes it on a timer past the quiet window — DOM-quiet alone would discover the button; waiting the announcement out means it is gone by the settled read). *Cost, recorded honestly:* the wait now sits through the full announcement lifetime, ~5.8s per reset on Juice Shop where the snackbar dismisses on a ~5.6s timer, taking the bench run from ~90s to ~7.3min — a deliberate trade of wall-clock for a shape that genuinely reproduces, made by explicit human decision when the alternative (loosening the exact-match drift gate to tolerate the race) was rejected. This also revises the §5.5 premise below: graph-shape counters *do* reproduce against a live SPA once the settle path waits out every source of post-load change, but only then — the gate is what proved they hadn't been. Known separate issue, not addressed here: the `/#/about` page runs a genuinely periodic CSS carousel (`slide-in-left`/`slide-out-right`) that is not a transient announcement and is out of 7f's scope.

**Design (not yet built) — §2e second-round interactive exploration: a map-generated config, subtractive permissions, sandbox-only destructive scope.** Exploration mode above (slices 6–8) is read-only: it discovers, observes, and maps, but never types into a field, logs in, or fires a destructive action outside a sandbox. This note captures the agreed shape of the *next* exploration capability — a **second, opt-in round** that goes further using a **config the first run generates for that specific site** — so the design and, above all, its safety boundary are locked in before any code. It is not built yet; when built it follows the BDD-first workflow like everything else, and it stays gated behind the read-only-mode-exit prerequisites already tracked in §9 (keyword-list localization, input-data provisioning). It pairs with the anchored depth-relative *resume* capability in the design note immediately below — the two are the intended shape of §2e "v2."

*The loop.* Round one is today's read-only autonomous crawl, producing the state-action map (§2e) and wiki. From that map Spoor **scaffolds a starter config matched to the site**: the actions it discovered, the input fields it saw (each with an inferred type/generator and an example, per the §9 input-data-provisioning seam), the points where a login is required, and every action its safety heuristic flagged destructive (and whether it was skipped for being off-sandbox). The user reviews and fills that config — supplying a session, pinning specific input values, and opting in per-action to what round two may touch — and round two re-runs, now able to authenticate, fill forms, and (only where the sandbox rule already allows it) exercise state-changing flows, complementing the map with the states only reachable past a login or a form.

**Decision — scaffold *generation* slice, shipped (issue #131).** The first, scoped piece of the loop above: `spoor explore --scaffold <dir>` writes the config-scaffold (`interactive.yaml`, a fixed filename inside the given directory — the flag originally took a file path directly, changed later to match `--wiki`/`--gen-tests`'s directory shape after that mismatch caused a real incident) as YAML, not JSON — this file is meant to be hand-edited, and YAML carries an inline comment next to each inferred value explaining why it guessed that generator, which a data-only format can't. **Consuming a filled-in scaffold to actually run round two is still not built** — this slice only generates the file. Two hard boundaries kept it from drifting: **(1)** discovery had no signal to tell a password field apart from any other text box (both surface identically as `role="textbox"` on the accessibility tree) — closed by a small, generic `input_type` enrichment on `ActionableElement` (`spoor/exploration/discovery.py`/`driver.py`), reading an `<input>`'s DOM `type` attribute the same way `destination` was added for links (slice 9b): one batched DOM read, not a per-element round trip, additive so nothing existing changes behaviour. **(2)** a `password`-typed field is never a generatable field — it becomes a `login_points` entry: a flag plus a blank `session:` key pointing at the same storage-state mechanism a `§2a` config already uses, never a username/password pair, per §2h's bring-your-own-session decision. `destructive_actions` entries are similarly informational only, matched against the safety gate's own recorded skip reason (`DESTRUCTIVE_SKIP_REASON`, `spoor/exploration/safety.py`) rather than re-derived from the action's label — re-deriving would misreport an action skipped for an unrelated reason (e.g. an overlay) as if it had been skipped for being destructive. `spoor/scaffold/interactive_config.py:build_scaffold`/`render_scaffold` mirror the pure-builder/thin-writer split `pytest_gen.py` and the wiki renderer already use. Nothing here is site-specific (§0): one generator for every target.

**Decision — a sandbox-only, typing-only round two may proceed ahead of the keyword-localization prerequisite (#101).** The design note above gates leaving read-only mode behind keyword-list localization (§9), because the destructive-action guard is English-only and round two raises the odds of actually reaching a destructive action on a real, authenticated session. Narrowed, explicit exception: a round that only **types a scaffold's pinned values into their fields on a registry-matched sandbox** (§2e: loopback or explicitly declared `sandbox: true`) may proceed ahead of #101; it must not issue submits, Enter presses, or clicks. **Typing is not read-only and can apply a value immediately:** [Playwright's `fill()` triggers an `input` event](https://playwright.dev/python/docs/input#text-input), and application handlers can autosave or otherwise change state without any subsequent submit or click. Those effects are permitted only within this exception's sandbox scope.
