The implementation must therefore check sandbox eligibility before every fill, independently of `evaluate_action`, and skip and record fills on non-sandbox targets even when their labels contain no destructive keyword. Every action must still pass the existing safety gate and the per-action permissions below; `evaluate_action` alone does not enforce this additional restriction, because it permits unmatched labels outside a sandbox. **Non-sandbox filling remains blocked:** completing #101 alone cannot establish that typing has no state-changing effects; any future expansion requires a separately recorded design decision addressing automatic application. Explicit submission, Enter presses, and clicks remain outside this exception and subject to the existing prerequisites, including #101 and resolution of how a filled field's value gets applied.

**Decision — typing-only scaffold *apply* slice, shipped, then corrected after review to match the decision above.** `spoor apply-scaffold <url> <file> --sandbox` implements the exception just recorded: the first, deliberately narrow piece of *consuming* a filled-in scaffold. It types each field's pinned value in and does nothing else — never Enter, never a submit control, never any action beyond typing.

The first cut of this slice shipped with real gaps a follow-up review caught, none of them cosmetic: it fired `fill` without ever checking `is_sandbox`, so typing could hit a production field's live autosave — the exact risk the decision above calls out; it reset and replayed the page for *every* field even when several fields shared a state, so an earlier field's typed value was reloaded away before the next one landed; a failed reset/replay aborted the whole run instead of just the fields on that page; an unquoted YAML number (`value: 42`) was silently dropped instead of reported; and `fill` returned success as soon as it had clicked and typed, with no check that a read-only or disabled field had actually changed. All five are fixed in `spoor/scaffold/apply.py:apply_scaffold` and `spoor/exploration/driver.py:PlaywrightDriver.fill`, described below as they now stand.

`apply_scaffold` checks `is_sandbox(target, declared=declared_sandbox)` first, before touching the driver at all — independently of `evaluate_action`, exactly as the decision above requires, since `evaluate_action` alone would allow any field whose label doesn't match a destructive keyword even outside a sandbox. `--sandbox` only *declares* the target, the same as `spoor explore --sandbox`; it is not a flag that relaxes the rule. Every field additionally passes `evaluate_action(target, field.name, role=..., declared_sandbox=...)` before it is filled — belt-and-suspenders with the sandbox check, so a field oddly labelled with a destructive keyword (e.g. "Delete") is refused here too, the same as during exploration; this closes the "every action must still pass the existing safety gate" requirement the decision above records, which the first cut did not actually do despite an earlier version of this note claiming it did. Applicable fields are grouped by their resolved state and share one `driver.reset()`, so multiple fields on the same page are typed into the same visit rather than being reloaded away by each other; a reset failure fails every field in that group without aborting other groups. A `value:` that parsed as something other than a string is reported (`"value must be a quoted YAML string"`), never silently skipped. This slice is further restricted to fields on the **root state only** — a field reached by replaying prior navigation clicks is refused with `"navigation replay is outside the typing-only scope"` rather than attempted, because a click that only exists to *reach* a field is still a click, and the exception this module lives inside is written against a session that clicks nothing but the field itself; `paths_from_root(graph)` is used only to detect that case, never to replay it (a narrowing from the first cut, which did replay it).

Actuation is `PlaywrightDriver.fill(action, value)`, sharing `perform`'s exact relocate-and-verify path (`_actuation` — the same `find_target` re-lookup, the same verified click point) rather than Playwright's own locator API, consistent with the relocation-bug precedent (7a) that first ruled that out; it clicks to focus, selects existing content, then types with real trusted keystrokes (`page.keyboard.type`), never a synthetic DOM write. After typing, it re-locates the field and reads its live value back over CDP; a value that doesn't match what was typed raises `ElementNotEditable` rather than reporting a fill that never landed — the fix for the read-only-field gap above. `spoor/scaffold/apply.py:apply_scaffold` never aborts on one field's failure (an unresolved state prefix, a field no longer present, a vanished/covered/non-editable element, a rejected navigation, a gate skip) — it records the failure and keeps going, the explorer's own skip-and-continue posture.

Verified against a real live crawl: `features/interactive_scaffold_apply.feature`'s `@browser` scenarios type into a real field and read the DOM value back, and a second scenario types into a read-only field and confirms both that nothing was reported applied and that the field's live value never changed. The original demonstration against the EcoEstate target predates this fix and is superseded by it — re-run against the real, corrected code, most of that target's fields now fail with `"navigation replay is outside the typing-only scope"` (they sit behind navigation) or `"target is not a declared sandbox"` (without `--sandbox`), which is the intended, safer outcome, not a regression. Nothing here is site-specific (§0): one applier for every target.

*§0 stays intact.* The generating and consuming *mechanism* is generic — it reads the map's own generic facts (roles, field semantics, ARIA) and emits a config the same way for every target; nothing about a specific site is baked into `spoor/`. The **filled config is site-specific data**, the identical sanctioned escape hatch a §2a extraction config or the per-domain cache already is — so this adds no site-tailoring to Spoor itself, exactly as intended.

*The safety boundary — unchanged, and the crux of this note.* The §2e destructive-action non-negotiable is **not** relaxed by any of this:

- **Permissions are subtractive only.** The per-action opt-in is an *allowlist that can only narrow* the built-in safety guard, never widen it. It cannot authorize an action the guard forbids; the guard stays the ceiling, the config only ever the floor.
- **Destructive actions stay sandbox-only and non-configurable (§2e).** On a registry-matched sandbox (`localhost`/`127.0.0.1`/`sandbox: true`) round two may fire flagged-destructive actions — the guard already permits this, so a permission config there needs no rule change. On any **non-sandbox** target those actions remain *always skipped and logged*, regardless of what the config says. Enabling destructive actions on a real production site would require an explicit, separately-recorded amendment to the non-negotiable — a maintainer decision this note deliberately does **not** make; it is called out here so it can never happen by the quiet accretion of a config field.
- **Auth is bring-your-own-session (§2h), not automated login.** Round two authenticates by consuming a supplied storage-state file (the shipped `session:` path), never by Spoor driving an SSO/MFA flow (declined in §2h as per-provider and §0-hostile). *Filling* a login form with user-pinned credentials is ordinary form-filling — interaction, gated by the same sandbox/keyword rules as any other input, not a special case.
- **Input-data and redaction rules carry over.** Values come from the §9 input-data seam (generic semantics-driven generation + user-pinned specifics); a config may hold real credentials/PII, so §2h redaction governs anything of it that reaches a shared surface, and pinned secrets stay local. The §2e run-level budget and kill switch apply to round two unchanged.
- **Keyword-list localization (§9) binds here too.** Because round two leaves read-only mode, the destructive-keyword guard must be locale-aware and err-safe first (treat an uncovered locale as "assume destructive / skip") — the same §9 prerequisite that gates Phase 5.

*What round two adds, concretely.* Three complementary modes, each opt-in and each inside the boundary above: an **authenticated deeper map** (a session reaches states behind a login the read-only round couldn't); **interactive form-driven exploration** (pinned/generated input data lets it fill search, filters, and multi-step forms, discovering states gated behind a submit); and, **sandbox-only, state-changing coverage** (against a sandbox target it can exercise cart/checkout/CRUD flows the guard already permits, mapping what the read-only round had to skip). The generated config is what makes all three reviewable up front: the user sees exactly which actions and fields a second round *could* touch, and every destructive one it flagged, before opting in to any of it.

**Decision — apply-scaffold observes and persists the state a fill reveals, shipped (issue #137), overriding this note's original "excluded from replay" proposal.** The typing-only apply command (`spoor apply-scaffold`, decision above) used to type a value in, report success or failure, and close the browser — the saved map and the wiki were left exactly as the original read-only crawl produced them, even when the typed value visibly changed the page. That gap was raised directly by a user running the shipped command: filling in the generated scaffold and re-running is, from the operator's point of view, *running Spoor again, now with that config* — the wiki should show what was behind the field, the same way it shows what was behind a click. This note's first draft proposed recording that state as observation-only and explicitly excluding it from path-based replay; a maintainer reviewing the draft overrode that specific part before implementation — see the two open questions below, both answered against that instruction, not the original proposal.

*What's built.* After `apply_scaffold` successfully fills a field (still root-state-only, unchanged from the decision above), the resulting page is read back with exactly the same read-only observation exploration already does at every step — `discover_actions` + `capture_signals`, no new mechanism, reused via a widened `ApplyDriver` Protocol (`state_html`/`ax_nodes`/`capture_signals`, the same three `BrowserDriver` methods the explorer's own after-action capture calls). When several fields share a state-group, each fill is observed individually and the walk chains through whatever it reveals — field two is matched against field one's *resulting* page, not the group's starting one, so a page that restructures between fields is still followed correctly. If a fill's resulting state id differs from where it started, it is added to the graph (a new state) and recorded as a transition (idempotent — re-running the same scaffold against an unchanged target adds nothing a second time); if the state is unchanged, nothing new is written, exactly as originally proposed. `spoor/cli.py`'s `apply-scaffold` command persists the enriched graph back to `MapStore` (preserving the entry's existing `records`/`tier`/`api_surface`/`config` — an update, never a silent full overwrite) whenever the graph actually grew, and refreshes the wiki through the existing `render_wiki` pipeline when `--wiki` is given.

*The two open questions this note originally left unanswered, now settled:*

- **How a fill is represented as a graph edge.** `ActionableElement` (`spoor/exploration/discovery.py`) gained one new optional field, `fill_value: str | None = None`. A transition whose action carries a `fill_value` is a typed edge; one that doesn't is a clicked edge exactly as before — no new transition kind, no branching data model, every existing `Transition`/`StateNode` consumer is unaffected by construction (the field defaults to `None` everywhere discovery itself still constructs an `ActionableElement`; only `apply.py` ever sets it). `_action_label` (`spoor/serving/store.py`) and `_action` (`spoor/exploration/persisted_map.py`) both carry `fill_value` through the local persisted-map round-trip, raw and unredacted like `name` already is — redaction happens only at the wiki/testgen render boundary (`_label`/`redact`), which is where a `fill_value` reaches a shared surface for the first time.
- **Whether a fill-revealed state is discoverable by `--resume-from`/testgen at all.** Yes, deliberately — this is the part that overrides the original proposal. A maintainer reviewing the draft wanted every config-derived state and transition to be a *real, replayable, deterministic* part of the map, clearly flagged by provenance rather than second-class or invisible to replay, because a consuming team's own test-automation treats the whole wiki as ground truth regardless of how a state was reached. `paths_from_root`/`spoor/testgen/pytest_gen.py` were extended, not bypassed: a generated test's `_PATH` and its own fired action are now `(role, name, fill_value)` triples — `fill_value is None` replays with the existing `fire()` (a click); a string replays with a new `fill()` helper in the emitted `_spoor_testkit.py`, mirroring `PlaywrightDriver.fill()`'s own click-focus/select-all/type sequence. Nothing here proposes continuing to *crawl* past a fill-revealed state (clicking whatever it exposes) — that remains the "interactive form-driven exploration" mode gated behind #101, unchanged from this note's original scope line.

*Flagged, everywhere it reaches a reader.* The wiki (`spoor/exploration/wiki.py`) marks both the transition page (what was typed) and the destination state page (that it was reached this way) with a plain-language badge — never silently blended in with a clicked transition. Verified end-to-end against the real EcoEstate demo target: filling "Search postcodes" revealed a new, real state with a genuine signal diff (a new network request, an accessibility-node delta), correctly persisted to the local map under its actual URL and rendered on the refreshed wiki with both badges. Nothing here is site-specific (§0): one observer for every target, and none of this touches the §2e destructive-action gate — a fill is still sandbox-checked and `evaluate_action`-gated exactly as the decision above already requires, before any of this runs.

**Design — §2e anchored, depth-relative resume: complement a map from a chosen state outward.** The companion to the second-round note above, and the other half of §2e "v2." Today an exploration run starts from the root and crawls to a global depth/state budget; a re-run starts over. This capability lets a subsequent run **begin from a state the first run already reached** and explore a bounded depth *outward from that anchor*, merging what it finds into the existing map — so mapping a large product becomes incremental and focused (deep-dive one area) rather than an all-or-nothing re-crawl. It reuses machinery that already exists — reset-and-replay drives to any recorded state (§2e 7d, by BFS over the recorded edges from root), depth-bounded traversal already caps the frontier, and the graph is persisted and servable (§2f) — so the new parts are anchor selection, re-origining depth at the anchor, and an additive merge. Built incrementally, BDD-first (see the shipped decision note below). Read-only-safe on its own (it discovers and maps, no interaction), so unlike the second-round note it carries no new safety-boundary exposure; the two compose (a resume run can also *be* an authenticated/interactive second round, each constraint applying exactly as written there).

*Anchor selection — configurable addressing, never an interactive picker.* An anchor is named in config/CLI and resolved against the persisted map by a small **state-selector** layer, generic to every target (§0). Four selector kinds, combinable: `title:` (the wiki's human-readable state title), `url:` (the route the state lives at), `id:` (a git-style state-id prefix), and `path:` (the action sequence that reaches it — the same replay path reset-and-replay drives). Resolution is **deterministic and never guesses**: a selector matching exactly one state anchors there; one matching several is an *error that lists the candidates* (id + title + url) to narrow — the same "never fabricate" stance the serving layer takes for an unmapped URL — and selectors may be combined to disambiguate. A GUI picker is explicitly *not* this (see the §2i local GUI decision, #112); the state-selector layer stays config/CLI-driven regardless of what a future GUI adds on top of it.

*What's resolvable today vs. what needs a persistence extension (recorded honestly).* The persisted map (§2f `shareable_exploration_map`) currently carries, per state, only the canonical **id** and the discovered **action inventory** (role + name) — **not** titles or URLs. State identity is deliberately DOM-only (a state carries no URL), and the wiki title is computed at *render* time from the captured `document.title`, never stored on the node or in the shareable projection. So `id:` and `path:` resolve against the persisted map as-is; **`title:`** needs the (already-redacted) title added to the shareable projection — cheap and §2h-safe, since the wiki already redacts it; and **`url:`** needs a prior decision, because no observed per-state URL is persisted today and introducing one touches the DOM-only identity model (the URL is currently transient walk state, used only in the frontier dedup key). The **pure selector resolves all four kinds** over an in-memory candidate set regardless; wiring each kind to the persisted map is gated on that field being available there — so the buildable-first slice is the pure resolver, then the projection extension for `title:`, then the `url:` design decision, then the resume traversal itself.

*Relative depth.* Depth is re-origined at the anchor: the anchor is depth 0 and the existing `--max-depth` bound is measured outward from it (explore N actions deep, backtrack, take the next branch, all capped at N from the anchor). No new traversal semantics — the same bounded frontier, its origin moved from root to the chosen state. The run-level budgets and kill switch (§2e) apply unchanged.

*Merge and staleness — fail loud, never fabricate.* Resuming replays the recorded path to the anchor (reset-and-replay), then explores outward and **merges additively** into the existing map: new states and transitions are added; states already present are left as they were. The honest failure mode is a **stale map** — the site changed since round one and the path no longer replays to the same state id. That is reported as a stale anchor and the run stops; it never silently re-explores from root, and never fabricates a match. Re-verifying or refreshing an already-mapped state is the §2f freshness concern (backlogged, §9), deliberately out of scope here — this capability *adds* coverage, it does not re-check what's already mapped.

**Decision — §2e resume, shipped (four slices, buildable-first order above).** The resume capability shipped in the four slices the design note ordered. **(1) Pure resolver** — `spoor/exploration/selector.py` resolves one or more constraints (`IdPrefix`/`TitleIs`/`UrlIs`/`PathIs`, combinable with AND) against a candidate set, returning resolved / unmatched / ambiguous-with-candidates; `parse_selector` reads the flat `kind:value` CLI form for the three single-value kinds (a `path:` is built structurally, since an action name can hold `:`/`=`/`>`); it never guesses (`exploration_state_selector.feature`). **(2) Graph candidate adapter** — `selector.graph_candidates(graph)` turns each in-memory state into a candidate (id, redacted title, root-relative `path` via the new `graph.paths_from_root`); `url` stays `None`, pinned honestly (`exploration_graph_candidates.feature`). **(3) Persisted-map loader** — `spoor/exploration/persisted_map.py:load_exploration_map` reconstructs an `ExplorationGraph` from the §2f shareable projection (ids in order, action inventory, topology, skips; no signals, so `title:` does not resolve against a saved map), validated by a real project-then-load round-trip (`exploration_persisted_map.feature`). **(4) Resume traversal** — `spoor/exploration/resume.py:resume_exploration` ties the three together (load → build candidates → resolve → crawl) and `explorer.explore` gained `resume_from`/`resume_anchor`: it starts from the loaded graph, seeds the breadth-first walk at the anchor with the root-relative reset-and-replay path (reconstructed by the new `graph.path_steps_from_root`, which keeps each step's landed state so replay can verify it), **re-origins depth at the anchor** (`len(path) - len(seed_path)`, so the anchor is depth 0 and `--max-depth` counts clicks from it), and **merges additively** — a transition-existence guard means a resume that re-fires an already-mapped edge does not duplicate it, and a fresh crawl (which fires each edge once) is unaffected. It **fails loud**: a selector matching no state or several raises `ResumeError` (the several case lists candidates); a start page whose id no longer matches the saved map's root, an anchor unreachable from that root, or a path that will not replay within the retry bound raises `ValueError` — never a silent remap. The CLI exposes it as `spoor explore <url> --resume-from <selector>` (loads the URL's saved map, refuses before launching a browser if none exists, and writes the merged map back). `exploration_resume_traversal.feature` pins the fast-tier chain (discovers states beyond the anchor, preserves the earlier map, measures depth from the anchor not the root, resumes from the root, and both fail-loud paths) and a `@browser` scenario proves the whole load→resolve→resume→discover cycle end to end in real Chromium over an A→B→C fixture. **Still deferred** (design note above): adding the redacted `title:` to the shareable projection so `title:` resolves against a saved map, and the `url:` per-state-URL decision; from a *saved* map the CLI therefore anchors by `id:` today. Generic to every target (§0): one resume path for all.

## 2f. Serving the map: MCP server & API (the hero-sentence promise)

Everything in §2a–§2e produces a map — extracted fields, the API surface, the signals catalog, the exploration-mode state graph. §2f is what turns that from "files on disk" into something an *agent* can actually consult: "give your agents a map of the web," not just "give a person a wiki to read."

Two consumption modes, both thin layers over data structures the rest of the plan already produces — no new capture/analysis logic here, purely serving what's already been built:

- **MCP server mode.** Spoor runs as an MCP server (using Anthropic's official `mcp` Python SDK) exposing tools like "get the known state/behavior for this URL," "list the actions available from this state," "what does the API surface look like for this domain," "has this product's checkout flow been mapped already." An agent — Claude or otherwise — can consult Spoor's existing map instead of burning its own tokens re-discovering a site's behavior from scratch every session. This is the direct payoff of §2b/§2c/§2e: the whole point of capturing all that signal was so it doesn't have to be rediscovered live each time.
- **Plain API mode.** The same underlying data served as a conventional REST API (FastAPI is the natural fit — async-native, pairs cleanly with the rest of the async stack, MIT-licensed) for consumers that aren't MCP clients: scripts, dashboards, CI checks against a previously-mapped site.

**Freshness, explained plainly.** Say Spoor mapped a site's checkout flow on Monday. On Friday, an agent asks Spoor "what happens when I click checkout" — but the site may have changed its checkout page since Monday, and Spoor wouldn't know unless it re-checked. "Too old" is just: at what point should Spoor say "let me re-verify that" instead of confidently handing back Monday's answer as if it were still true right now? The per-domain cache (§2) already tracks when something was last confirmed, so the mechanical part (attaching that timestamp to every served answer, and giving the caller a way to force a re-check) is easy. **The actual policy — how old is "too old" before Spoor should recheck on its own rather than wait to be asked — isn't decided, and doesn't need to be right now.** Backlogged (see §9); a reasonable placeholder is "always show the age, never auto-recheck in v1," deciding the smarter policy once there's real usage to learn from.

**Sequencing.** This depends on §2a (extraction) and §2b (API surface) existing to have anything worth serving, but not on §2e (exploration mode) — an MCP/API layer over "the fields and API surface from a configured run" is useful on its own, well before the full autonomous exploration graph exists. Reasonable to build a first pass alongside Phase 4 (packaging) as v1 scope, with the richer "serve the full exploration graph" version arriving once §2e (v2) ships.

**Decision — §2f first slice (read-only REST API over a captured map).** With Phases 1–3.5 stable, §2f begins with its plain-API mode — the thinnest useful serving layer — rather than the MCP mode, because it is straightforwardly testable in-process (a FastAPI `TestClient`) and shares the exact store/service the MCP mode will later sit over, so nothing is thrown away by starting here. Two pieces: (1) `spoor/serving/store.py:MapStore` — a persisted per-domain map (`maps/<domain>.json` under the git-ignored cache root, the same local-first home as the fingerprint/change-detection stores — **superseded by a SQLite database, see the #214 decision note; `MapStore`'s public interface is unchanged**). A run records its extracted records for the target URL there with a UTC capture time; a missing/corrupt database is "nothing mapped yet", never an error. It holds the **same records the output pipeline already writes to shared output** — not a raw local-only capture (HAR/storage state), which never enters it (§2h). The CLI's `run` populates it after each run; a new `spoor serve` command serves it. (2) `spoor/serving/api.py:create_app(store)` — a FastAPI app exposing **only** `GET /healthz`, `GET /domains`, and `GET /map?url=…`. **Read-only is structural, not disciplinary (§2f/§2h non-negotiable):** the app defines no non-GET route, and `serving.feature` pins this with a scenario asserting every route's methods are a subset of `{GET, HEAD}`. Every `/map` answer carries `captured_at` and a computed `age_seconds`, realising §2f's "always show the age" — the **v1 freshness policy is "never auto-recheck"**, and a **force-recheck** endpoint is a deliberate follow-on (§9), as is the **MCP server mode**. Because §2h names an API response as a shared surface, served record values pass through the secret-redaction primitive (`spoor/security/redaction.py`) on the way out — the local store may hold raw records, but the serving boundary is redacted, and `serving.feature` pins this with a scenario asserting a bearer token in a served record leaves as `[REDACTED]`. Serving dependencies (FastAPI, uvicorn) are an optional `serve` extra, kept out of the dependency-free core (§1) — only `spoor serve` needs them. **Deferred at this slice, built later:** the force-recheck seam (now shipped — see the force-recheck decision below).

**Decision — serve the observed API surface alongside records (§2f, §2h split preserved).** The `/map` answer now also carries the run's observed **API surface**, but only the §2h-shareable projection of it, mirroring the observability layer's own split: the published spec (`kind`/`version`/`url`) and GraphQL endpoint (`url`/`types`) are non-sensitive and served **whole**, while the *synthesized* spec and *action correlation* are reduced to **counts only** (`endpoint_count`/`request_count`, `action_count`/`request_count`) — their templated paths and local-only `doc_path` can embed an un-clustered secret segment, so they never enter the served surface at all. `spoor/serving/store.py:shareable_api_surface(...)` is the one place that projection is made (the CLI's `run` calls it, storing the projection, never the raw dataclasses), and the API runs it through the same redaction guard on the way out (counts untouched; a secret-shaped URL redacted like any shared string). `serving.feature` pins both halves: the spec/GraphQL/synthesized counts are served, and the served payload exposes no templated endpoint path.

**Decision — MCP server mode (the second §2f consumption mode, read-only always).** Beside the REST API, §2f now ships its MCP mode: `spoor/serving/mcp_server.py:create_mcp_server(store)` (using the `mcp` SDK's high-level `MCPServer`), and a `spoor serve-mcp` command running it over stdio for a local agent. It sits over the **same** `MapStore` and answers with the **same** view as the REST surface — the two modes were deliberately factored onto one shared `views.map_view(entry)`, so the freshness age and the §2h redaction of records + API surface are computed in exactly one place and neither mode can drift from the other. **Read-only is the non-negotiable, enforced two ways:** only read tools are registered (`list_mapped_domains`, `get_map` — no tool changes a target or the stored map), and each carries the MCP read-only / non-destructive annotations; `serving_mcp.feature` pins it with a scenario asserting the exposed tool set is exactly that allowlist and every tool is marked read-only and non-destructive. An unmapped URL is a tool error, never a fabricated answer (parity with the REST 404). The `mcp` SDK joins FastAPI/uvicorn in the optional `serve` extra — the core engine never imports it; only `spoor serve-mcp` and the serving tests do. **Now shipped (was deferred here):** the force-recheck seam — see the decision below.

**Decision — force-recheck seam (opt-in, read-only *with respect to the target*).** §2f said a caller may force a re-check; this ships it, on both surfaces. The subtlety is the §2f/§2h non-negotiable: the serving layer must never cause a *state-changing or destructive action on a target*. A recheck doesn't — it re-runs the URL's own extraction, which is the same **read/observation** a normal run performs — so the non-negotiable, which explicitly permits *triggering a new read/observation run*, is honored. The guarantee was therefore **reframed from "no non-GET route / read-only tools only" to "no route or tool changes a target"**: a plain reader server stays GET-only (REST) / read-tools-only (MCP), and enabling recheck adds *exactly one* seam — `POST /map/recheck?url=…` on REST, a `recheck_map` tool on MCP — whose sole effect on the target is to observe it. That MCP tool is **honestly annotated**: `read_only_hint=False` (it fetches and rewrites the local map) but `destructive_hint=False` — no tool on the server is ever destructive to a target. Mechanics: the store now persists the serialized `ExtractionConfig` each entry came from (**local-only — never projected into `map_view`, so it never reaches a served response**), and `spoor/serving/recheck.py:recheck_url(store, url)` reconstructs that config, re-runs `extract.run_report`, and re-records the fresh result; an unmapped URL (or one recorded before configs were persisted) is a 404 / tool error, never fabricated. Both surfaces gain a `--recheck` opt-in flag on `spoor serve` / `serve-mcp`, **off by default**. `serving.feature` and `serving_mcp.feature` pin both shapes (plain server unchanged; recheck-enabled server adds exactly the one seam, non-destructive, and honestly non-read-only on MCP). This is the *mechanical* force-recheck only; v1 still **never auto-rechecks** — the "how old is too old" policy stays backlogged (§9).

**Decision — serve the exploration graph (§2f, the "serve the full exploration graph" step §2f's sequencing named).** With §2e's exploration mode complete (state-action graph + wiki), §2f now serves that graph too, closing the hero-sentence promise for exploration mode: an agent can consult "what happens when I click X" as data, not just read a static wiki. Built the same way the API surface was: (1) `spoor/serving/store.py:shareable_exploration_map(graph)` — the one place the graph is projected to §2h-shareable facts, mirroring `shareable_api_surface`. It reduces to plain strings/ints/bools/lists so `map_view` redacts it wholesale: per state, the abstract id, the discovered action inventory (role + name), and the free-signal bundle **as counts only** (raw console/storage/network *values* captured at a state stay local-only, the same counts-only stance the synthesized API surface takes); per transition, the from/to ids, the action fired, and **what it changed** — the before/after signal diff, carried *with* its added values (console lines, storage keys, request URLs), because that diff is precisely the "what did clicking X change" answer §2f exists to serve, and each value is a plain string redacted on the way out; plus every safety-gate skip with its reason, and the three counts. Returns None for an empty graph (nothing to serve), the same no-shell stance as `shareable_api_surface`. (2) A new local-only-then-redacted `MapEntry.exploration` field with store round-trip, surfaced by adding one line to the shared `views.map_view` (`redact_value(entry.exploration)`) — so **both** the REST `/map` and the MCP `get_map` serve the exploration graph for free, redacted identically, neither able to drift. (3) `spoor explore` now records its graph into the map after each run (records empty, no tier — an exploration-only entry), so the capability is end-to-end: explore a site, then serve its graph. `serving.feature` and `serving_mcp.feature` each gain a scenario asserting the graph is served with the right counts/action label and that a secret captured in a signal leaves as `[REDACTED]`; `exploration_browser.feature` gains a scenario asserting a live `spoor explore` run persists a servable graph; and `tests/test_serving_store.py` pins the projection's shape, its counts-only per-state split, its actual-diff per-transition payload, and its empty-graph/None-signals contracts directly. Read-only w.r.t. the target is untouched (a graph is read back from the local map; no new route or tool, no target interaction) and §2h redaction is enforced at the one shared boundary.

**Design note (agreed, not yet built) — freshness by re-observation: record what changed on the mapped transitions, never judge it.** Agreed with the maintainer while sparring #128 (generated tests don't flag new/unexpected signals); tracked as **#211** (corrected citation — a later review found this design note had been mis-tracked under #107, which is actually the unrelated, narrower "should §2f auto-recheck stale served data on a timer" policy question; #211 is the real tracking number for the mechanism this note describes, #107 is left to that policy question alone). The motivating case is a **ghost call**: a backend change makes the front end fire an extra request (or log a new console line) on a transition whose markup and actions are untouched. State identity is DOM-based (§2e), so the graph is unchanged, the generated suite (§2g) stays green, and nothing in Spoor notices. This note sets how Spoor closes that gap *as a map*, and — deliberately — where it stops.

*The boundary, recorded so later slices don't drift into a testing tool.* Spoor observes and records; consumers judge. Every part of this capability must pass three questions: **(1) does it add a fact to the map, or hand down a verdict?** ("this transition now fires two request templates it didn't" is a fact; "high severity, fail the build" is a verdict); **(2) does it need to know what the site is *supposed* to do?** (a map records behaviour; a test encodes intent — once Spoor needs intent it has become a testing tool); **(3) could a consumer reach the verdict from the map alone?** (if so, the verdict belongs to the consumer — the generated suite, the team's CI, or an agent over MCP/REST). In Spoor: re-observing mapped transitions, normalizing what was seen, recording per transition what changed since the last observation, and marking a signal *volatile* when it can't be pinned down. Out of Spoor: pass/fail, severity levels, allowlists, count budgets, accept workflows, CI-dashboard reports. §2g's generated suite stays the one place that asserts, run by the consumer's pytest, not by Spoor.

*Shape.* A new re-observation run replays each **recorded** transition — the same verified reset-and-replay path (§2e 7d) and the same safety gate as exploration, so a destructive action is never fired outside a sandbox — captures the signal bundle, and records against the saved map what changed. It **discovers nothing new** (that remains a fresh explore) and it is **not `--resume-from`**: resume adds coverage, merges additively leaving mapped states as they were, and fails loud on a stale map (the §2e resume decision) — the opposite of what a re-check needs. The replay machinery is shared; the capability is separate. Per transition the recorded fact is one of: **unchanged**; **signal templates added / removed** (per kind: network, console, storage); **destination changed** (the action works but lands on a different state id); or **not reproducible** (the action is no longer found, or the path to it no longer replays). No severity is attached to any of them.

*Noise — measured in the run, not learned from history.* A single observation can't tell a real ghost call from a beacon that happened not to fire last time. Prior art: Diffy (Twitter; `opendiffy/diffy`) sends the same traffic to two instances of the known-good version and treats whatever differs between *those two* as noise. Here: **replay each transition twice within the re-observation run**; a signal template present in one replay and not the other is marked **volatile** — a recorded fact ("we can't pin this down"), not a filter that hides it — and differences from a compatible saved baseline are reported as **observed additions / removals agreed by both current samples**, never as proof of a lasting behavioural change. Agreement between two current samples does not establish baseline stability: a sporadic beacon seen once in the baseline but absent from both replays looks like a removal; one absent from the baseline but present in both looks like an addition. Record the baseline sample count and any observed baseline volatility alongside the current samples; a single-sample baseline has **unknown variability**, and that uncertainty remains visible with every comparison. Even agreement across two baseline samples and two current samples cannot rule out intermittent behaviour. This needs **no accumulated history**: retain the selected baseline samples and their variability metadata, not a log of "every origin ever seen." Before comparing, signals are normalized so values that vary by nature don't read as change: **request URLs** are reduced to method + origin + a path template (segments that are numeric, UUIDs, hex hashes, or high-entropy become placeholders — the same token-classification idea API inference uses, and `spoor/api_discovery/` clusters paths already, so reuse it rather than write a second clusterer; see #111) with query-parameter *names* only; **console lines** are reduced to templates with a log-template-mining algorithm (Drain; the `drain3` library is the candidate); and each request carries its **browser-reported resource type** (Playwright's `request.resource_type` — how the page initiated it), never a type guessed from the URL's extension. Hundreds of map-tile PNGs from one zoom-out therefore collapse into one template with a count range, instead of hundreds of individual signals.

*What exists and what doesn't.* The saved map already carries each transition's signal diff **with its added values** (console lines, storage keys, request URLs — local-only in the store, redacted on the way out; see the serve-the-exploration-graph note above), but these legacy diffs are **not a compatible baseline** for the comparison described here. Network values are URL strings without methods or resource types; the set-based diff discards duplicate requests and requests to URLs already seen before the action, so it cannot recover per-transition counts either. Reconstructing the saved diffs in `persisted_map.load_exploration_map` cannot restore that lost information. **Legacy-map policy:** replay the recorded transitions to capture a fresh baseline with the new signal schema; report baseline initialization, not added/removed signals or an unchanged verdict against the legacy data. Preserve the legacy map, and compare only subsequent observations against the newly captured baseline. The new capture must retain every request event within the action window, including repeated URLs, with its method and browser resource type, before normalization and counting. Persist a baseline schema/normalization version, sample count, and observed variability; an incompatible baseline requires explicit initialization rather than treating missing fields as empty observations. Still missing: that richer capture and persistence, normalization, double replay, and somewhere to record the observation's result. Everything recorded is shareable output, so §2h redaction applies unchanged; the comparison runs on raw, normalized values in the local-only store and only its redacted result is shared.

*Deliberately out of scope.* Interpreting a change as *harmful* — a new third-party script origin, same-URL script content changes, CSP/SRI checks — is a separate, later application (#130); re-observation reports "changed," never "dangerous." Response-content hashing (e.g. whether map tiles' pixels changed) is left out of the first version: against a live tile server the two replays would disagree and correctly mark the template volatile, and the pixel question is a consumer-side test concern. Whether the serving layer may *trigger* a re-observation (it fires non-destructive actions under the gate, unlike the extraction-only recheck seam above) is not decided here — CLI first.

*Open decisions before the feature file.* (a) **Baseline**: does a re-observation update the saved map (so "changed since" means "since the last observation"), or record beside it against a baseline that only an explicit re-explore replaces? (b) **Where the result lives**: in the saved map, surfaced by a "changed since" section in the wiki and in the served view, or a separate artifact? (c) **How #128 consumes it**: whether `--gen-tests` gains an opt-in reverse assertion ("and nothing unrecorded") that skips volatile templates. BDD-first per CLAUDE.md: the `.feature` file comes after these are settled.

**Decision — §2f map store moves to SQLite (closes #214, epic #216, Phase A of the sparred #184 design).** #184 (durable queryable store) was sparred into a real design with the maintainer: configs/sessions/exploration-state should move off the flat-file convention into a single SQLite database, read-write, replacing rather than sitting alongside the existing files; sessions become a first-class, named, multi-per-site concept (Phase B, #215, separate issue — depends on this one). This decision note covers Phase A only: swapping `MapStore`'s backing storage, with **zero change to its public interface**.

`spoor/serving/store.py:MapStore` previously held one JSON file per domain (`maps/<domain>.json`, a full read-modify-write of the whole file on every `record()`/`get()` call, overwrite-only — no history). It now holds a single SQLite database (`spoor.db` under the git-ignored cache root). `sqlite3` is stdlib — no new dependency — and the implementation matches the house style the existing SQLite *output* sink (`spoor/operational/output.py`, closes old #108) already set: plain `sqlite3`, explicit SQL, no ORM. Schema: `sites(id, domain UNIQUE)` and `runs(id, site_id, url, captured_at, tier, records_json, api_surface_json, exploration_json, config_json)`, migrated via SQLite's own `PRAGMA user_version` — a tiny, hand-rolled migration runner (a tuple of SQL scripts applied in order), not Alembic/SQLAlchemy, matching every other piece of persistence in this codebase.

**`record()` now inserts, never overwrites — real run history, not yet exposed.** The old per-domain-JSON approach could only overwrite (keeping history would have meant an unbounded full-file rewrite on every run); SQLite makes insert-only trivial. `get()` still answers with only the **latest** row for a URL (`ORDER BY captured_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 1`), so every existing caller's behavior — "this run's result replaces what's served" — is unchanged; the history sits in the table, unreachable through `MapStore`'s current interface. Exposing it (a served "history for this URL" view, or similar) is explicitly left to a later slice, not built here.

**Blast radius: zero call-site changes.** Confirmed every caller of `MapStore()` (`spoor/cli.py` ×5, `spoor/serving/recheck.py`, `spoor/serving/api.py`, `spoor/serving/mcp_server.py`, every BDD step-def fixture) only ever calls `.record()`/`.get()`/`.domains()`, never reaches into internals — so this is a pure internal swap. `MapEntry`'s shape (`url`, `domain`, `records`, `tier`, `captured_at`, `api_surface`, `config`, `exploration`) is unchanged.

**A genuine concurrency bug found and fixed by this slice's own test, not assumed away.** The first implementation issued `PRAGMA journal_mode=WAL` on every `_connect()` call, intending better concurrent-reader-during-write behavior for `spoor serve` fielding simultaneous requests. A new test spinning up 20 threads calling `record()` concurrently caught `sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked` intermittently (roughly 1 run in 3): switching journal mode itself requires an *exclusive* lock, so multiple connections racing to open a brand-new database file and all attempting that switch simultaneously could — and, empirically, did — collide. WAL was a premature optimization Spoor's actual write pattern (occasional CLI runs, rare recheck calls, not a high-throughput server) doesn't need; removed. The default rollback-journal mode plus `busy_timeout` (both the `sqlite3.connect(..., timeout=5.0)` parameter and an explicit `PRAGMA busy_timeout`) is correct and sufficient — confirmed by running the concurrency test ten consecutive times clean after the fix, not just once. A second, narrower race (two threads both seeing `user_version=0` and both running the schema-creation script, the second hitting `table already exists`) was fixed by making the migration DDL idempotent (`CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` / `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS`), which also makes the migration runner safe under concurrent first-connection races generally, not just the one this test happened to construct.

**Existing data: clean cutover, no migration importer.** Spoor is pre-alpha (`pyproject.toml`). Old `.spoor-cache/maps/*.json` files are simply orphaned — never read by the new store — rather than imported; every record in them is re-capturable by re-running. `tests/test_serving_store.py` gains direct `MapStore` tests (round-trip of every field, history retention verified by querying the database directly — not just that `get()` answers latest, which the existing BDD force-recheck scenario already covered — domain listing, migration idempotency, and the concurrency regression test above); the full existing `features/serving.feature`/`features/serving_mcp.feature` suite (24 scenarios) passed unmodified against the new backing store, serving as the regression guard that the public interface truly didn't change.

**Out of scope, deliberately.** Three other modules independently reimplement the same "per-domain JSON file, read-modify-write whole file" pattern this replaces for maps — `spoor/core/fingerprint_cache.py`, `spoor/operational/change_detection.py`, `spoor/operational/crawl_state.py` — found during this slice's design research. Not unified into the same SQLite database here: doing so would have meaningfully widened this slice's scope beyond what was asked, for a real but separate consistency improvement that deserves its own issue if picked up later. Phase B (#215, named/multiple stored sessions) is the next piece of epic #216 — **shipped**, see its own decision note under §2h.

## 2g. Test automation run generation (HT's own primary use case)

This is the concrete shape of "test automation run creation" from HT's own stated needs: a captured map (a configured §2a run, or a full §2e exploration graph) is a **golden-master record of known-good behavior** — state X, action Y, produced signal bundle Z. That's already 90% of what a regression test asserts; §2g is the last-mile step of exporting it as one.

Two output shapes, both generated, never hand-written:
- **Assertion-style regression tests**: for each captured transition, emit a test (pytest, or a Playwright Test file if the consuming team is JS-based) that replays the action and asserts the resulting signal bundle still matches — the DOM state, the API calls fired, the console being clean, whatever the capture included. Re-running these against the real product later is exactly how drift gets caught: a failing generated test means the product's actual behavior no longer matches its last-known-good map.
- **Human-readable test plans**: the same transitions rendered as a plain-language run sheet (ties into the §2e wiki output) for teams that want a reviewable test case, not just executable code.

This depends on §2a/§2e existing (nothing to generate from otherwise) and is a natural companion to §2f rather than a separate subsystem — both are "consume the captured map," just for different audiences (an agent asking a question live, vs. a generated artifact a CI pipeline runs). Sequencing: a first pass makes sense alongside Phase 5 (§2e, v2), since the richest version needs the full exploration graph — a thinner version scoped to just a configured §2a run's captured transitions could ship earlier if HT's own test-automation need turns out to be more urgent than the v2 timeline implies (worth revisiting once Phase 1–3.5 are stable and this becomes concrete rather than aspirational).

**Decision — §2g sub-slice 2g-i (pure pytest generator from the exploration graph), shipped.** The first §2g slice delivers the assertion-style output shape against the §2e exploration graph, built logic-first exactly as the wiki renderer was (6a): `spoor/testgen/pytest_gen.py:build_tests(graph, *, target) -> dict[str, str]` maps a graph to pytest test *sources* (filename → code) with **no browser and no disk** — the thin on-disk writer and a live "the emitted suite runs green against a fixture" proof are the follow-on sub-slice 2g-ii. Per mapped transition the generator emits a `test_transition_N.py` that, via a shared `_spoor_testkit.py` and a `conftest.py`, drives **Playwright** to replay the reset-and-replay path that reached the transition's from-state (reconstructed by a BFS over the recorded edges from the root state), fires the action, and asserts the additive signals the transition was recorded to add (console lines, storage keys, request URLs) still appear — a real regression test run against the live product, chosen over a data-only self-consistency check because catching product drift is the stated need. Two consequences recorded as deliberate, not gaps: **(1)** because a generated test is a **shared-output surface**, §2h redaction is mandatory on every captured value baked in, *and* — unique to testgen — the emitted suite must redact the live values it observes (`redact_all`, importing Spoor's own `redact`) before comparing, so both sides match like-for-like and no raw secret is ever the expected literal; this makes running the generated suite depend on `ht-spoor` + `playwright`, accepted because the §2h-safe alternative (baking the raw secret in so the assertion matches) is a non-negotiable violation. **(2)** Only the additive string signals are asserted; the a11y-node delta and screenshot hash are deferred (reproducing them in the generated runtime needs Spoor's CDP a11y read and visual hasher — the same staged deferral the wiki took for screenshot images), and an action whose accessible *name* is itself a secret gets redacted so its locator would not match live — accepted as rare (names are UI labels) over writing the raw name. Nothing is site-specific (§0): one emitter for every target. `features/testgen.feature` (6 scenarios) and `tests/step_defs/test_testgen_steps.py` pin the generated sources — valid Python, one test per transition, path replay, fire, per-signal assertions, the no-change smoke test, redaction, and the empty-graph→no-tests contract.

**Decision — §2g sub-slice 2g-ii (on-disk writer + CLI + live-run proof), shipped.** The follow-on the 2g-i note named, closing §2g's last mile from "generates test *sources*" to "writes a suite a team runs." Three pieces: **(1)** `spoor/testgen/pytest_gen.py:render_suite(graph, out_dir, *, target) -> list[Path]` — the thin on-disk counterpart, mirroring the wiki writer (6a): it calls `build_tests` (pure), creates `out_dir` and parents if absent, writes each `filename → source` as UTF-8, and returns the paths sorted; an empty graph writes nothing (no shell suite). It bakes in no new captured value, so it adds no redaction surface of its own — everything was already §2h-redacted by `build_tests`. **(2)** `spoor explore --gen-tests <dir>` wires it to the command beside `--wiki`, on both the fresh and the `--resume-from` paths, and reports the test count. **(3)** The live proof `features/testgen_writer.feature` names as its point: a `@browser` scenario crawls a real fixture (`gen_home.html` → `gen_deep.html`, whose deep page adds a console line and a localStorage key so the transition carries a real additive-signal diff), writes the suite, and runs `python -m pytest` on it in a **subprocess** against the same live fixture, asserting it exits green — end-to-end evidence the emitted tests replay, fire, and *assert* correctly against a running target, not merely that they are valid Python (2g-i's bar). The subprocess (over an in-process `pytest.main`) isolates the generated `conftest`'s own Playwright browser from the outer run. Fast-tier scenarios pin the writer (every file on disk, directory created, contents byte-for-byte the generator's, empty graph → nothing written). The CLI flag itself carries no dedicated test, matching the `--wiki` precedent (both are thin writers wired identically and covered through the feature scenarios). The richer configured-§2a-run and human-readable-test-plan shapes remain future work.

**Decision — §2g reverse assertion (`--assert-no-new-signals`), a scoped first piece of #128, shipped.** §9's #128 backlog item names the gap directly: the generated suite asserts only that recorded values still appear, never that nothing *beyond* them appeared, so a ghost call (an extra request or console line a backend change starts firing, with the markup and the discoverable actions untouched) produces an identical graph and a suite that stays green. That backlog entry's own "design pass done" note ties #128's consumption to the separate "freshness by re-observation" capability (#211) — specifically its open decision (c): whether `--gen-tests` gains an opt-in reverse assertion fed by #211's volatility-aware baseline. #211 itself (replay + double-replay noise filtering + normalization + persistent baseline) is not built yet. Rather than block #128 entirely on that larger capability, this slice ships the part of #128 that needs none of it: `spoor/testgen/pytest_gen.py:_closed_world_signals(graph, transition)` computes, from data a normal `spoor explore --gen-tests` run already has (no re-observation or baseline required), the union of every console/storage/network value recorded as *added* across the **whole replayed path** reaching a transition — not just that transition's own diff. The whole-path union is load-bearing, not an embellishment: the generated test's console/network listeners are attached for the entire replay (`conftest.py`'s `page` fixture), so a transition reached two hops in still sees the first hop's own recorded additions in `page.spoor_console`/`page.spoor_network` when its assertion runs; closing over only the leaf transition's diff would false-positive on every earlier hop's legitimate addition — confirmed by a dedicated scenario (`features/testgen.feature`, "closed set spans the whole replayed path") using a three-state fixture where a deeper transition's closed network set must include an earlier transition's own `/menu.js` addition, not just its own `/cart.js`. `build_tests`/`render_suite` gain `assert_no_new_signals: bool = False` (default off, so every existing call site and generated-suite shape is byte-for-byte unchanged); `spoor explore` gains `--assert-no-new-signals`, requiring `--gen-tests` (the same precondition shape as `--screenshots` requiring `--wiki`). **A signal kind is checked only when at least one value was recorded for it somewhere on the path** — a kind with nothing recorded gets no reverse check, because the initial `page.goto()` navigation's own console/network activity during `reach()` is never captured as any transition's "added" signal (added is a before/after diff *around firing an action*, not around page load), so asserting "nothing at all" for an untouched kind would false-positive on ordinary page-load noise with zero signal from Spoor about it. **Explicitly not what this slice is**: #211's normalization (URL/console-log templating) and double-replay volatility detection are not built, so a value that legitimately varies run to run (a timestamp query parameter, a random request id) is asserted literally — a known, documented source of noise for this flag, not a correctness bug, and the reason the flag stays opt-in rather than becoming the default. §9's #128 entry is updated to record this as shipped, not deferred, and #211 remains open for the re-observation capability itself (replay, baseline persistence, wiki/served surfacing) — a separate, larger piece of work, tracked on its own.

**Decision — §2g reverse-assertion follow-up: three review-found defects fixed (post-merge correction to the note above).** A full-epic adversarial review (all of #151–#154 together) found the first slice shipped broken in a way its own scenarios never exercised, because every testgen scenario inspects *generated source text*, never executes it. **(1) Guaranteed runtime crash.** `_assertion_body` emitted `assert set(console) <= {sorted(closed_console)!r}` — `sorted()` returns a `list`, so the rendered line was `assert set(console) <= ['a', 'b'], "..."`: comparing a `set` to a `list` with `<=` raises `TypeError` unconditionally in Python, on every execution. Every generated test that reached a console- or storage-touching transition under `--assert-no-new-signals` crashed instead of asserting anything — the feature was non-functional for its primary case the moment it touched a real signal, not merely noisier than documented. (The `network` branch was unaffected: it already used `all(any(...) for ...)`, never a set comparison.) Fixed by wrapping the literal in `set(...)` at render time: `assert set(console) <= set([...])`. **(2) Silent data loss + needless O(T·(S+T)) cost.** `_closed_world_signals` rebuilt a `{edge: Transition}` dict from the whole graph *and* re-ran `path_steps_from_root` (a full BFS) on every call, inside the per-transition loop in `build_tests` — repeated O(T) work T times for no reason. Worse: a single-value dict keyed by edge silently kept only the *last* transition seen for a given `(from, action, to)` signature; two distinct `Transition` objects sharing an edge (plausible after a `--resume-from` merge) meant the earlier one's recorded signals were dropped from the union entirely — a legitimately-seen value excluded from the closed set, producing a false-positive assertion failure with no diagnostic pointing at the cause. Fixed by hoisting both structures out of the loop (computed once in `build_tests`) and replacing the single-value dict with `_edge_index`, a `{edge: list[Transition]}` grouping that unions *every* transition sharing an edge rather than keeping one arbitrarily. **(3) The documented page-load-noise caveat was narrower than the real gap.** The original note correctly flagged that an *untouched* signal kind gets no check (page-load noise is never any transition's own diff); it did not flag that a *touched* kind has the identical problem — the landing page's own console/network/storage activity from the bare `page.goto()` at the start of every replay is in view for every generated test, including ones for touched kinds, and was never in the closed set either. On any real site that logs or requests anything on initial load — the common case, not an edge case — the very first generated test for a touched kind would false-positive on that landing-page noise. Fixed by seeding the root state's own full captured snapshot (`StateSignals`, not a diff) into every closed-world set unconditionally; `features/testgen.feature`'s "nothing ever recorded" scenario was rewritten to assert the new, correct behavior (root noise alone populates the check) rather than the old, now-inaccurate "asserts no signal changes." A new `tests/test_pytest_gen.py` closes the detection gap directly: it `exec()`s the generated closed-world assertion lines against stand-in `console`/`storage`/`network` values — proving they run, not just parse — and a dedicated test proves the duplicate-edge union keeps both transitions' signals. No non-negotiable was touched; §2h redaction is unchanged (every value redacted before reaching the rendered string, now including the root snapshot).

## 2h. Data handling, security & auth (core architecture, not later hardening)

This section exists directly because of the bottom-line critique: destructive-action safety was already treated as core (§2e), but everything about *data* sensitivity — where captured cookies/tokens/HAR/screenshots live, whether secrets get redacted, what an MCP-connected agent is allowed to do, how login-gated targets are handled at all — was drifting toward "backlog" by omission. These are now decided, folded into the base design rather than appended after the fact.

**Storage & redaction (decided): local-only, auto-redact.** Every artifact §2c/§2d capture produces — cookies, tokens, full storage state, HAR files, screenshots, console output — stays on the user's machine under a per-run directory, never transmitted anywhere by default; this is the literal, concrete meaning of "local-first," not just a slogan. Before anything is written to a *shared* surface — the §2d output pipeline (JSONL/CSV/SQLite), the §2e wiki, or a §2f MCP/API response — Spoor scans for known secret patterns (bearer tokens, common auth-cookie shapes, API keys matching common vendor formats) and redacts them. The raw, unredacted capture (full HAR, full storage state) lives in a separate local cache directory that's never included in anything shared, exported, or served by default — exporting that raw cache is a separate, explicit user action. The actual redaction pattern list is a real Phase 2.5 deliverable, not a vague promise — tracked in §9 below rather than assumed to already exist.

**MCP/API permission boundary (decided): read-only, always.** The §2f serving layer only ever answers questions about a previously-captured map, or triggers a new *read/observation* run (a §2a config or a §2e exploration) — it never exposes a tool letting an agent directly cause a destructive or state-changing action on the target. This is a hard boundary on the MCP/API surface specifically, in addition to (not instead of) §2e's sandbox-only rule for destructive actions during exploration itself: an agent consulting Spoor can look, and can ask Spoor to go look again, but can never use Spoor as a channel to change anything on the target.

**Auth, login, MFA, SSO, multi-role (decided, deliberately scoped narrow for v1): bring-your-own-session (implemented).** Spoor does not implement login flows, MFA handling, or SSO redirect automation itself — that's a large, fragile, per-provider surface that would violate §0's no-tailoring principle almost by definition, since every SSO provider ends up being its own integration. Instead, the user authenticates manually once (in their own browser, or Spoor's) and supplies the resulting storage state (`context.storage_state()` — the same shape the §2c capture writes) as an input to a run. Multi-role support is just running the same config once per supplied storage-state file, one per role. This keeps auth entirely outside Spoor's core surface while still making authenticated targets usable from day one.

Delivered as the `session:` config field (a path to a storage-state JSON), loaded by `spoor/security/session.py` — the §2h owner of this secret-bearing input, not `core/` (§0): `load_session` validates the file (a missing/malformed/wrong-typed file raises `SessionError` *before* any fetch, so a run fails loudly rather than silently proceeding anonymous), and the tiers consume it generically. The static tier (tier 1, no JS) sends the session's **cookies** via the httpx jar with their original domain/path scope; the browser tier (tier 2) hands the whole file to `browser.new_context(storage_state=...)` so Playwright applies **cookies + per-origin localStorage**. A target gated behind a localStorage token yields zero records at tier 1 and escalates to the browser, which reaches it — no config author choice, pure dispatcher escalation (§2). The supplied state is never echoed to shared output (it is loaded into the fetch, not captured from it), so redaction — which guards *captures* on their way out — is not in play; the discipline that applies is simply that `RunResult`/`RunSummary` carry nothing from it. Covered by `session.feature` (both tiers + the loud-failure path) and `tests/test_session.py` (loader boundaries).

**Decision — named, stored sessions per site (§2h Phase B, closes #215, epic #216).** The second half of the sparred #184 design (Phase A: #214's SQLite `MapStore` swap, shipped — see its decision note above). Today's `session:`/`--session` is purely a CLI-supplied file: never persisted, never named, no concept of "several for the same site." This slice adds a **second**, opt-in way to supply the same input — store a captured session once under a label, scoped to a site, and resolve it by name across runs — without changing the file-path behavior at all.

**Security posture, called out explicitly because it is a real change (§2h non-negotiable).** Storing session data makes Spoor a persistent holder of live login cookies for the first time — a new kind of state this project hadn't held before. It gets the identical treatment every other raw, secret-bearing capture already gets: the new `sessions` table lives in the same local-only, git-ignored database `MapStore` uses (`.spoor-cache/spoor.db`), never exported, never redacted-and-served (redaction guards things routed to a *shared* surface; this is never routed to one at all). `spoor/security/session_store.py:SessionMeta` — the only shape `SessionStore.list()` can return — carries a label and two timestamps and **structurally cannot** carry a cookie or storage value, so a bug in a future display path has nothing to leak. **Deliberately no MCP/REST surface for stored sessions in this slice** — confirmed with the maintainer rather than assumed, since #215's own text left it as an open question. Purely a CLI-side convenience; nothing here touches the §2f read-only non-negotiable, and nothing new was added to `spoor/serving/`.

**Shared database, shared connection code — not two SQLite implementations.** `spoor/security/db.py` (new) now owns the connection/migration machinery #214 built inside `spoor/serving/store.py`: `connect()`, the `PRAGMA user_version` migration runner, `get_or_create_site`. `MapStore` was refactored to call it rather than keep its own copy; `SessionStore` is built on the same functions from day one. One place opens the database, one busy_timeout/foreign-key posture, one ordered migration list (`_MIGRATIONS[0]` — `sites`/`runs`, #214's original schema, byte-identical — `_MIGRATIONS[1]` — new — `sessions(id, site_id, label, storage_state_json, created_at, last_used_at, UNIQUE(site_id, label))`). A session and a mapped URL for the same domain share one `sites` row.

**Resolution precedence: a real file always wins.** `load_session(path, *, domain=None)` — extended, not replaced — tries `path` as a file exactly as it always has; only when no file exists there, and a `domain` was given, does it try `path` as a stored label scoped to that domain, via `SessionStore.get`. A same-named stored label can never shadow an actual file a caller points at. `domain=` is a new keyword-only parameter, omitted by any call site that predates this slice — a bare `load_session(path)` with no domain never attempts a label lookup at all, so every existing caller's behavior (and every existing test) is unchanged; the three call sites that *do* want label resolution (`Tier1Resolver`/`Tier2Resolver` in `spoor/core/extract.py`, `PlaywrightDriver.__init__` in `spoor/exploration/driver.py`, and the CLI's eager pre-flight validation in `explore`/`apply-scaffold`) each now pass `domain=urlsplit(target).netloc`, deriving it from the URL they already have in scope — no new state threaded through. A session resolved from the store has `LoadedSession.path = None` (there was never a file); `storage_state_arg(session)` is the one new helper both Playwright-context call sites use to get either the path string or the raw storage-state dict, since Playwright's `new_context(storage_state=...)` accepts either.

**`spoor session add/list/remove`.** `add <file> <site> --label <name>` validates the file through `load_session` first (the same check `--session <file>` already applies) before storing it — a label can never point at something that was never a valid storage state. `site` accepts a bare domain or a full URL (normalized by `urlsplit(...).netloc or site`), whichever is easier to paste. Re-adding an existing label for the same site **upserts** — replaces what it points to and resets `last_used_at` — a deliberate choice for the common case of a session expiring and being re-captured under the same name, rather than requiring a remove-then-add round trip. `list [site]` prints only `SessionMeta` fields. `remove <site> <label>` reports whether anything was actually deleted, so "removed" and "there was nothing to remove" read differently rather than both printing a bare success.

**Clean cutover, not a migration — there is nothing to migrate.** There was no prior named-session concept at all, so unlike #214 (which had an old JSON format to orphan) there is no "before" shape to reconcile; this is additive from a blank slate.

**Test coverage.** `features/session_store.feature` (10 scenarios, pytest-bdd) drives `SessionStore`/`load_session`'s resolution precedence end to end: storing and listing, per-site scoping, metadata-only listing (asserted by checking the dataclass's own field set and that a raw cookie marker never appears in a rendered listing — not just "the test didn't print it"), upsert-on-re-add, remove-found vs. remove-nothing, label resolution, and the file-always-wins precedence (proven by chdir-ing into a temp directory so a bare relative name resolves against a real file, then showing the resolved content is the file's, not the store's). `tests/test_session.py` adds the boundary case the feature suite doesn't reach: that omitting `domain=` entirely reproduces the exact pre-#215 behavior, the guarantee every untouched call site depends on. `tests/test_cli.py` adds `spoor session add/list/remove` CLI-surface tests (full URL vs. bare domain for `site`, a malformed file rejected, remove-twice). The full existing `session.feature` suite (both tiers, browser included) passed unmodified against the refactored `load_session`, the regression guard that file-path resolution truly didn't change.

**Decision — Phase-1 run-observability slice (pulled forward).** §2d lists run observability among the operational essentials but the phased plan grouped it loosely with the Phase-3.5 items; it is pulled forward into Phase 1 by maintainer direction, because it builds directly on the escalation dispatcher just landed and closes the "did this run actually go well?" gap §2d names, with no new machinery. The slice ships a structured, operator-facing `RunSummary` produced for every dispatched run, reporting only what the current machinery can honestly derive: **items scraped, pages fetched, the tier that resolved the run, the escalation path (tiers attempted, so "escalated tier 1 → tier 2" vs. a capability-routed single tier is visible), and blocked URLs.** The §2d items that need absent machinery are deliberately **not** faked: error/retry classification waits on the Phase-3.5 retry mechanism, and "which fields fell back to tier 3/4" waits on those tiers existing. Placement follows the §0 layout: the raw per-run facts (`tier`, `pages_fetched`, `tiers_attempted`) are stamped on `RunResult` by the dispatcher in `spoor/core/extract.py`, and the operator-facing summary object plus its rendering live in `spoor/operational/observability.py` — core produces the signals, `operational` presents them. The CLI prints the rendered summary after each run; `RunSummary` stays a plain structured object so a later phase can serialize it (the §2d "signals-catalog thinking turned inward") without rework.

**Decision — Phase-2.5 HAR-capture first slice (pulled forward, local-only).** HAR recording — "wire in HAR recording on every Playwright context (near-free once Phase 1 exists)" (§2b/§2c) — is started early, by maintainer direction, as the concrete foundation the §2c signal catalog and the mitmproxy2swagger spike (§8.4) both build on. Scope of this slice, deliberately tight:

- **Opt-in this slice, via `capture.har: true` on the §2a config** (a new `Capture` model, `extra="forbid"` like the rest). §2c's eventual *default-on* capture is the full Phase-2.5 target and is **not** turned on here — an existing run's behavior is unchanged unless it asks for capture, so nothing starts silently writing files.
- **Browser-tier only.** Only tier 2 has a Playwright context to record; a run that resolves at tier 1 (no browser) produces no HAR, and the run summary (§2d) says so honestly rather than writing an empty file. A tier-1 static fetch's single request is not the target of network capture.
- **Raw, local-only — never the shared path (§2h non-negotiable).** The HAR is written to a per-run subdirectory of a local-only cache dir (`.spoor-cache/<run-id>/network.har`, git-ignored), exactly the "raw, unredacted capture lives in the local-only cache" surface §2h describes. It is **not** routed through the output pipeline, the wiki, or any MCP/API response, so the redaction-before-shared-output rule is not in play for it — and must never be bypassed by later routing a raw HAR to a shared surface without the explicit-export step. The per-run cache dir helper lives in `spoor/security/` (the §2h data-handling owner). Default-on capture is deferred to the full Phase 2.5. A configurable cache base was decided later, in its minimal form: the `SPOOR_CACHE_DIR` environment variable (see the gui-4 mechanics decision in §2i).

The captured HAR path is surfaced on the run summary so an operator can find it. This slice adds no new runtime dependency (Playwright already records HAR natively via `record_har_path`).

**Decision — secret-redaction pipeline (the §9 pattern-list deliverable, built as a reusable primitive first).** The §9 backlog names a "concrete secret-redaction pattern list" as a Phase-2.5 prerequisite before any capture surfaces raw values to shared output. It is built here as its own slice — `spoor/security/redaction.py`, a pure `redact(text) -> str` primitive over a maintained `SECRET_PATTERNS` list — *before* its first consumer, so the consumer (client-side storage state, the next slice) inherits a tested guard rather than growing an ad-hoc one. Scope and boundaries, deliberately explicit so the claim isn't oversold:

- **Default-on, no opt-out (§2h non-negotiable).** `redact` takes only the text; there is no parameter to disable it. A caller on the shared path cannot ship raw secrets through it. The only way raw values leave the machine stays the separate, explicit export of the local-only cache — which does not route through redaction.
- **Known shapes, not entropy.** The list redacts *recognized* formats: auth-scheme credentials (`Bearer …`, and `Basic …` when it rides an `Authorization`/`Proxy-Authorization` header — anchored there because "Basic" is also an ordinary English word, unlike "Bearer"), common auth/session-cookie *and* secret field names keeping the name and redacting the value (`sessionid`, `access_token`, `csrf_token`, `api_key`/`apikey`, `client_secret`, `api_secret`, `token`, `id_token`, `password`/`passwd`/`pwd`, …), common vendor API-key formats (OpenAI `sk-…`/`sk-proj-…`, Stripe secret/restricted `sk_live_…`/`sk_test_…`/`rk_live_…`, AWS `AKIA…`, Google `AIza…` and OAuth `GOCSPX-…`, GitHub `ghp_`/`github_pat_`, Slack `xox[baprs]-…`), JWTs, and PEM private-key blocks (`-----BEGIN … PRIVATE KEY-----`). It is **not** a general high-entropy detector — that trades false positives (mangling legitimate data) for a promise this layer does not make. A keyless high-entropy blob with no recognizable shape — the canonical example being an AWS *secret* access key (40 base64 chars, no prefix) — is deliberately **not** caught here; the local-only cache, not redaction, is what keeps such raw captures off shared output. This bounds the claim honestly, the same discipline §5/§6 apply to tier-3 accuracy and tier-4 stealth. The vendor forms above were widened after an adversarial false-negative probe found the earlier `sk-…`-only rule missed Stripe's underscore keys and OpenAI's `sk-proj-` form; the probe was distilled into `redaction.feature` scenarios so those gaps stay closed.
- **Structure-preserving and idempotent.** A non-sensitive prefix (auth scheme, cookie name) is kept; the `[REDACTED]` placeholder matches none of the patterns, so re-redacting is a no-op. The list is extended deliberately, with a fixture example in `redaction.feature` for every shape added — never a broad catch-all.

**Decision — the extracted-record path is wired to redaction (closing the last §2h shared-output gap).** The primitive above was first wired only to the storage-state signal; the primary **extracted records** — the thing the §2d output pipeline writes to a file and the §2f serving API returns — were still surfaced raw. That was the §2h non-negotiable's own listed surface ("before anything reaches the output pipeline … or an MCP/API response") left unenforced, so it is now closed. A shared record-level helper, `redaction.redact_records(records)` (walking every string value, including nested lists/dicts; numbers/`None`/booleans pass through; idempotent), runs **on by default, no opt-out** at both boundaries: `output.write_records` redacts after schema-validation and before any write, and the serving API redacts on the way out. The raw records still live only in the local-only cache and the per-domain map; only the redacted form reaches a file or an API response.
