This is the documented known-shapes/false-positive tradeoff applied to primary data: a scraped field that happens to hold a recognized secret shape is redacted (accepted), while a keyless high-entropy value is not (unchanged) — the same boundary the primitive draws everywhere. `output.feature` and `serving.feature` each pin a scenario proving a bearer token in a record leaves as `[REDACTED]`.

## 2i. Local GUI: operating Spoor without the command line

**Decision — a local web GUI (`spoor gui`), promoted from the §9 "possible GUI front-end" backlog item by maintainer direction.** The maintainer wants to use the CLI as little as possible. The surface to cover is everything the CLI does today: `explore` (~15 options), `run`, `apply-scaffold`, `session add/list/remove`, `serve`, `serve-mcp`, and the map browsing that today means reading the wiki or `curl`-ing `spoor serve`. The interactive `spoor wizard` already shows the demand for "don't make me remember flags"; the GUI is the full answer to it.

**Form: a local web app, not a desktop app or a Python-native UI framework.** `spoor gui` starts a server bound to loopback and opens the operator's default browser on it. This was chosen over (a) a desktop shell (Tauri/Electron), which would add a Node and/or Rust toolchain plus per-OS packaging to a pure-Python project for a cosmetic gain, and (b) NiceGUI/Streamlit, which are fastest to start but give little control over the result and pull in heavy runtimes. Stack: **FastAPI** (already the §2f choice) + **Jinja2** server-rendered pages (already a core dependency for the §2e wiki) + **Server-Sent Events** for live job output, with a few lines of plain JavaScript (htmx was considered and withdrawn; see the gui-2 mechanics decision below). No JavaScript build step and no Node toolchain: `pip install 'ht-spoor[gui]'` is the whole install. The `gui` extra holds FastAPI, uvicorn and `python-multipart` (form submissions, from gui-2); the core engine never imports any of it.

**The GUI is a separate local *control plane*, not part of the §2f serving layer.** This is the load-bearing distinction, and the reason this needed a recorded decision. The §2f/§2h non-negotiable makes the MCP/REST serving layer read-only *with respect to targets* and limits it to answering questions and triggering read/observation runs. The GUI must start exploration runs, and exploration can fire destructive actions against a sandbox (§2e). So the GUI can't be built by adding routes to `spoor/serving/api.py`; that would turn the agent-facing read-only surface into a control surface. Instead:

- It lives in its own package, **`spoor/gui/`**, with its own FastAPI app. `spoor/serving/` gains no route, tool or flag because of the GUI. The GUI may *import* serving's read helpers (`MapStore` reads, `views.map_view`) to display maps, so both surfaces show the same redacted view and can't drift apart.
- **The GUI drives Spoor only by running the real CLI as a subprocess** (`sys.executable -m spoor.cli <args>`), the same pattern `spoor wizard` already established. The GUI assembles an argument list, shows the exact equivalent command line, and runs it. It never reimplements a command's logic or calls engine internals to start a run. That gives three guarantees: GUI and CLI can't drift; every safety gate (the §2e destructive-action gate, the sandbox host backstop, §2h redaction, session pre-flight validation) applies exactly as it does from a terminal; and the synchronous Playwright engine runs in its own process, so a long run never blocks the GUI server and **Stop** can terminate it cleanly.
- The **sandbox declaration is a checkbox that maps to `--sandbox`**, with the same plain-language explanation as the CLI help. It can't do more than the flag can: `spoor/security/sandbox.py`'s backstop (a declared sandbox must also be loopback/private-range) still decides. The GUI adds no override, and no GUI setting relaxes the §2e rule.

**GUI security posture (decided, part of this capability's definition, not later hardening).** A local server that can start browser runs is a bigger target than a read-only API. These hold from the first slice:

1. **Loopback-only, non-configurable.** `spoor gui` binds `127.0.0.1` only. Unlike `spoor serve --host`, there's no host option; refusing a non-loopback bind is pinned by a scenario. Remote operation is out of scope; it would need real authentication and is a separate decision.
2. **Per-launch access token.** At startup the server generates a random token and opens the browser at a URL carrying it once; the server then sets it as an `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict` cookie. Every request without a valid token is rejected. The token is valid only for that GUI process, so its one appearance in the launch URL (and so in browser history) is worthless once the GUI exits. This stops other local users, and any web page the operator visits, from driving the GUI.
3. **DNS-rebinding and CSRF guards.** Requests whose `Host` header isn't the loopback address/port are rejected, and state-changing requests (POST/DELETE) also require a matching `Origin`. This blocks the standard "malicious page rebinds a hostname to 127.0.0.1" attack against local dev servers.
4. **Redacted display, even locally.** Maps are shown through the same `views.map_view` the serving layer uses, so secret-shaped values appear as `[REDACTED]` in the browser too (screen-shares and screenshots are a real leak path). Run logs streamed to the page pass through the `redact` primitive line by line. Raw captures (HAR, storage state) are never rendered. The GUI may show their *local path* and offer "open folder", consistent with §2h's explicit-export rule.
5. **Stored sessions: metadata only.** The sessions page lists only `SessionMeta` fields (label, site, timestamps), the same structural guarantee `spoor session list` has. Adding a session is a file upload validated by the same `load_session` check; contents are never echoed back.
6. **No new non-loopback surface.** Starting `spoor serve` from the GUI launches the same command with its existing defaults (loopback, read-only, recheck off unless ticked). The GUI doesn't widen `serve`'s options.

**Scope — the four areas, built as staged slices, each BDD-first:**

- **gui-1, shell + browse maps (read-only).** `spoor gui`, the security posture above, a home page listing mapped domains (`MapStore.domains()`), and a per-URL map page showing the redacted map view: captured time and age, records, the API-surface projection, and the exploration graph (states, transitions, safety-gate skips with reasons). It doesn't link to a generated wiki: the map store doesn't record where a wiki was written, so that link belongs to gui-2, where the job that wrote it knows the path. This slice can't affect any target, which is why it goes first.
- **gui-2, run jobs: explore / run / apply-scaffold.** Forms covering every option of the three commands, grouped as in their `--help` (target, limits, outputs, element rules, session). Each form shows the resolved command line live and validates paths before launch. A **job manager** tracks subprocess jobs: live log over SSE (redacted), status, exit code, **Stop**, and on completion the output paths written as clickable "open folder" links, plus a link to the generated wiki (served as static files from that output directory under the same token check). This puts the CLAUDE.md "always state where output landed" rule into the UI. Job history is held in memory for the GUI process's lifetime in this slice; persisting it is a follow-on.
- **gui-3, sessions + configs.** Sessions: list / add (upload) / remove, mirroring `spoor session`. Configs: open a §2a YAML config, edit it in a text area, **validate** it against the same Pydantic model `spoor run` loads (errors shown inline with their field path), save, and "run this config" (hands off to gui-2's `run` job). A structured form editor for configs is a follow-on; validation-backed text editing comes first because it covers every field from day one.
- **gui-4, server controls.** Start/stop a managed `spoor serve` (port, recheck checkbox) as a gui-2-style job with its URL shown when it's up. For `serve-mcp`, which is stdio-only and therefore not something the GUI can usefully host, the GUI shows the **ready-to-paste MCP client configuration** (command + args for the installed Python) instead of launching it.

**Delivered — gui-1 (shell + browse maps).** `spoor gui [--port N] [--no-browser]` (`spoor/gui/`: `app.py` guard + pages, `launch.py` loopback-only launcher, `templates.py` inline Jinja2 like the §2e wiki's). As built:
- The launcher binds the socket itself before building the app, so the default is an OS-assigned free port (no clashes) and the Host allowlist knows the real port. `127.0.0.1:<port>`, `localhost:<port>` and `[::1]:<port>` are accepted.
- The token cookie is named per port (`spoor_gui_token_<port>`). Cookies aren't port-scoped, so a fixed name would let a second running GUI overwrite the first one's token; the gate's self-review found this.
- FastAPI's `/docs`/`/openapi.json` are switched off, and the Origin check is already enforced on every non-GET request even though gui-1 has no POST routes.
- Browsing a site needed one new **read** on the store, `MapStore.urls(domain)` (each mapped URL with its latest capture time). It is a store method, not a serving route or tool; `spoor/serving/api.py` and `mcp_server.py` are untouched, and `gui.feature` pins the serving app's routes.
- Server-Sent Events aren't needed for static pages, so they arrive with gui-2.

`features/gui.feature` (16 scenarios, one `@browser` end to end) plus `tests/test_gui.py` cover it. Before the PR, each guard (Host, Origin, cookie token, HttpOnly, redaction, unmapped-404) was deliberately broken in turn to confirm a scenario fails.

**Decision — gui-2 mechanics (run jobs), settled before its feature file.** Five choices the gui-2 outline above left open, decided as follows:
- **Live progress needs an opt-in plain-line mode in `spoor explore`.** Its progress indicator deliberately prints nothing when stdout isn't a terminal, so a supervised run would show nothing until it finished. With `SPOOR_PROGRESS=lines` in its environment, `explore` instead prints one plain progress line (states, actions, elapsed seconds) at most every two seconds. Without the variable, piped output is unchanged.
- **Graceful Stop uses a stop file, not a signal.** `explore` already treats Ctrl-C as "stop after the current action, then save the map and write the requested outputs". A supervisor can't send that portably. On Windows the only console signal a child can receive is delivered to its whole process group, which includes Playwright's driver and browser, so it would crash the run instead of stopping it. Instead, with `SPOOR_STOP_FILE=<path>` in its environment, `explore` checks for that file on each progress tick (once per state found and per action fired) and throws the same kill switch Ctrl-C does. **Force stop** kills the job's whole process tree (`taskkill /T /F` on Windows; on POSIX the child runs in its own session and the group is killed), so no orphaned Chromium is left. `run` and `apply-scaffold` have no graceful stop, so their Stop is Force stop, and the page says what that means: nothing from the run is saved. Closing the GUI force-stops any job still running rather than leaving an unsupervised browser behind.
- **Validation stays the CLI's.** The form layer only parses what it must to build an argument list: required fields present, numbers that are numbers. Everything else (budget bounds, output paths that exist as files, `--screenshots` without `--wiki`, an unknown session label) is left to the command itself, whose error appears in the job's log at once. Re-implementing those checks in the GUI would be the drift §2i exists to prevent. A **command preview** endpoint runs the same argument builder the launch uses, so the previewed line is exactly what runs.
- **No htmx.** Static pages, one form-preview fetch and a log that appends lines over Server-Sent Events need about forty lines of plain JavaScript using the browser's native `EventSource`. Vendoring a third-party library for that isn't worth it, so the htmx part of the outline above is withdrawn.
- **Outputs are owned by their job.** Output paths are resolved to absolute paths before launch (the child runs in the GUI's own working directory, the same one the local cache lives under), so "where it landed" is unambiguous. The job page lists each requested output that now exists. "Open folder" opens only a path the job itself recorded, never one a request supplies. A generated wiki is served under `/jobs/<id>/files/wiki/…`, confined to that directory and behind the same token check. Default output folders are pre-filled per form as `spoor-output/<timestamp>/…` under the working directory, so runs don't overwrite each other. Job history stays in memory, and the log keeps its last 10,000 lines, every one redacted.

**Delivered — gui-2 (run jobs).** Explore / Extract / Apply scaffold forms (`/new/<kind>`) covering every option of the three commands, with a live command preview; a job page with the redacted live log (Server-Sent Events), Stop / Force stop, and "Where the output landed" (absolute paths, "Open folder", "Open wiki", and a link to the map); and a session-long Jobs list. Each map page links to "Explore this page again" with the address pre-filled. As built (`spoor/gui/`: `commands.py` form → argument list, `jobs.py` supervisor + runners, `job_routes.py` pages and endpoints):
- `spoor explore` gained the two supervisor hooks from the mechanics decision above: `SPOOR_PROGRESS=lines` and `SPOOR_STOP_FILE`. With neither variable set, its behaviour is unchanged.
- Both stops were checked against real processes on Windows before the PR. Stop ended a live exploration about two seconds after it was pressed, with exit code 0 and its wiki written. Force stop took down the job's whole tree (Python, Playwright's driver and Chromium; six processes) at once, leaving nothing running.
- Form fields for paths are made absolute against the working directory. Session labels and `--resume-from` selectors are passed through untouched, because the command decides what they mean.
- `start_gui` takes a `workdir` (the jobs' working directory, which is also where relative form paths resolve and where the child's local cache lives). The real-browser scenario uses it to run in a temp folder.

`features/gui_jobs.feature` (26 scenarios, one `@browser` end to end that explores a static fixture from the form and opens its wiki), plus `tests/test_cli.py` unit tests for the two hooks, cover it.

**Decision — the GUI wears the wiki's theme (one stylesheet, two surfaces).** By maintainer direction, the GUI's own light/dark palette is retired for uniformity with the §2e wiki, which already had a considered design (the slice 6h/6i dark, token-driven theme). The wiki's CSS moved verbatim into `spoor/exploration/theme.py:STYLESHEET`. The wiki embeds it exactly as before; its rendered output was checked byte-identical against a pre-move snapshot. The GUI embeds the same string and adds only GUI-specific rules built from the same tokens: forms, buttons, job status colours, and the log. It also reuses the wiki's own classes (`table-wrap`, `index-list`, `stats`/`stat`, `notice`) instead of parallel ones, so a token change restyles both, and a GUI page and the wiki it links to read as one product. The GUI was therefore dark-only, like the wiki, until the day/night decision below gave both a day mode. While checking the result in a real browser, the review found and fixed:
- Tables squeezed short columns until words split (`overflow-wrap: anywhere` on every cell). Now only code and paths break anywhere.
- A transition's "what changed" was raw JSON. It is now short plain-language lines (`app.describe_changes`).
- A failed job's status line repeated itself.

**Decision — gui-3 mechanics (saved logins and config files), settled before its feature file.**
- **Saved logins change only through the CLI; listing reads the store.** Adding and removing run the real `spoor session add` / `spoor session remove` through the same job runner gui-2 uses (so they appear in Jobs too). The GUI waits up to 30 seconds for that short command and then shows its result on the sessions page, rather than sending the operator to a job page. Listing calls `SessionStore.list()` directly, as the map pages read `MapStore`: it can only return `SessionMeta` (label, site, timestamps), so a stored cookie has no path to the page.
- **An uploaded login file lives only as long as the command.** The upload (capped at 5 MB) is written to a fresh private temporary folder, passed to `spoor session add`, and deleted with its folder as soon as the command ends, whether it succeeded, failed, or was killed for overrunning the wait. Its contents are never shown back. The CLI's own `load_session` check decides whether it is a valid storage state.
- **The explore and apply-scaffold forms suggest stored login names** for their "Saved login" field. Only labels are offered, never contents.
- **Config files: confined to the working folder, validated by the same model `spoor run` uses.** The Configs page lists `.yaml`/`.yml` files under the GUI's working folder. It skips hidden folders, the local cache, `spoor-output`, and virtualenv/`node_modules` folders, stops at four levels deep and 200 files, and says whether each file validates. The editor opens and saves only a `.yaml`/`.yml` file whose resolved path is inside that folder (and not in the local cache). A path that climbs out, or points elsewhere, is refused, so the GUI can't be used as a general file editor. **Check** parses with `load_config`, the exact function `spoor run` uses, and lists each problem with its field path ("fields.price.type: …") or its YAML line, without saving. **Save** writes the file even when it doesn't validate yet, so work in progress isn't lost, and says plainly that it isn't valid. **Run this config** opens gui-2's Extract form with the config pre-filled. **New config** starts from the README's quickstart example and refuses to overwrite an existing file. Writing a config is editing the operator's own input file, not running Spoor, so it doesn't go through the CLI.
- **The editor shows a config exactly as it is on disk; it doesn't redact it.** §2h redaction guards *captured* output on its way to a shared surface. A config is the operator's own input, and an editor that blanked a token would write `[REDACTED]` back into the file on save. The page says so, so nobody is surprised by a header token on screen.
- **No sandbox-registry editor.** The outline above anticipated one, but there is no registry file: a sandbox is the `--sandbox` flag (a checkbox in gui-2's forms) plus the host check in `spoor/security/sandbox.py`, which deliberately can't be configured.

**Delivered — gui-3 (saved logins and config files).** A Logins page (list, add by upload, remove) and a Configs page (list with validity, new from the example, editor with Check / Save / Run this config), as decided in the gui-3 mechanics note above. The explore and apply-scaffold forms suggest stored login names, and the Extract form can be pre-filled with a config. As built (`spoor/gui/`: `manage_routes.py` pages and endpoints, `configs.py` the confined config-file logic; `JobManager.run_and_wait` for the short session commands):
- Session add/remove run through the job manager, so they also appear in Jobs. Their outcome (the command's last line, or its full error) is shown on the Logins page.
- Both session commands put the site, label and upload path after a `--` separator, so a name or site that starts with "-" is still read as a value, never as an option. This was checked against the real CLI. gui-2 refuses such an address instead, because `explore`'s options follow its address and so can't sit after a `--`. Refusing here would have left a login saved from the command line under such a name impossible to remove from the GUI.
- A session command that overruns the 30-second wait is force-stopped, and the page says to check the list, because it may already have saved the login.
- Two problems found in review are fixed. A config the editor can't safely round-trip (over 1 MB, or not UTF-8) gets no editor at all, because an empty editor with a working Save button would let one click wipe or corrupt the file. Symlinks pointing outside the folder aren't listed.
- A browser always submits the editor's text with CRLF line breaks. Saving writes them back in the file's existing style (CRLF if it had any, LF otherwise and for new files). This was found when a merely opened-and-saved fixture showed up as a whole-file line-ending change in git.
- The fake runner the GUI scenarios share moved to `tests/step_defs/_gui_fakes.py`.

`features/gui_sessions_configs.feature` (23 scenarios, 25 test cases with the path outline's examples; two `@browser`: a login saved through the real `spoor session add`, and a config fixed in the editor) covers it.

**Decision — gui-4 mechanics (server controls), and a minimal configurable cache location.**
- **The local cache location can be set with `SPOOR_CACHE_DIR` (maintainer decision; previously deferred, see §2h).** The deferral note under the HAR-capture decision now points here. When the variable is set and non-empty, `storage.CACHE_ROOT` is that path (`~` expanded; a relative value is taken from the working directory). Unset, it stays `.spoor-cache` in the working directory, so nothing changes for anyone who doesn't set it. It is read once, when the process starts, and applies to every command (maps, sessions, raw captures, fingerprints), because they all share the one root. The cache stays local-only, so §2h is unchanged: this only says *where* on the operator's machine it lives. It was needed because an AI client launches `spoor serve-mcp` from a working directory of its own choosing, and with a relative cache root the server would read an empty map. The alternatives were weighed with the maintainer and rejected: a client `cwd` field (not every client supports one; Claude Desktop, for example, doesn't) and a `python -c` wrapper that changes directory first (works, but leaves an unreadable config).
- **The map API server is a supervised `spoor serve` job.** The Servers page starts the real `spoor serve --port N [--recheck]` through gui-2's job manager, so it also appears in Jobs and closing the GUI stops it. There is no host field: it stays on `serve`'s loopback default (§2i posture item 6). At most one managed server runs at a time; a second start is refused with a pointer to the running one. Readiness is the server's own `GET /healthz`, polled from the GUI's backend (the page refreshes itself while starting), so "running at http://127.0.0.1:N" is shown only once the server actually answers. Stop is Force stop: a read-only server has nothing to save.
- **MCP: a ready-to-paste config, not a hosted server.** `serve-mcp` talks over stdio to the client that launched it, so the GUI can't usefully host it. The page shows the JSON `mcpServers` entry (`command`: this Python, `args`: `-m spoor.cli serve-mcp`, `env`: `SPOOR_CACHE_DIR` set to the GUI's own cache as an absolute path) and the equivalent `claude mcp add` command line. Each has a copy button and a note on adding `--recheck`. A test runs the exact generated config from an unrelated folder and checks that the server lists the GUI's mapped sites.

**Delivered — gui-4 (server controls), completing the four §2i slices.** A Servers page that starts and stops the map API server (`spoor serve --port N [--recheck]`, no host field, one at a time, "running" only once `/healthz` answers, and the page refreshes itself while it starts) and shows the MCP setup (JSON `mcpServers` entry and `claude mcp add` line, each with a copy button), as decided in the gui-4 mechanics note above. `SPOOR_CACHE_DIR` lives in `spoor/security/storage.py:cache_root_from`, read once at start-up. As built (`spoor/gui/server_routes.py`):
- The health check is injectable, like the job runner, so readiness is tested without a real server.
- Stopping waits briefly for the server to exit, so the page doesn't still show it running.
- A stopped server job's status line says "Stopped." rather than gui-2's "nothing more was saved", which doesn't apply to a read-only server.

`features/gui_servers.feature` (15 scenarios, 18 test cases) covers it. Its MCP scenario runs the *exact* configuration the page shows, as a real MCP client (`mcp` stdio client), from an unrelated folder, and checks that it lists the GUI's mapped site. The `@browser` scenario starts a real `spoor serve` from the page, queries it, and stops it.

**Decision — Spoor's logo, and where it lives.** The maintainer supplied the Spoor mark (a hoof print on a hexagon) in three colourways. They live in `docs/assets/` as the canonical brand files (SVG and PNG each; `docs/assets/README.md` says which to use where), and each SVG gained a `viewBox` so it scales cleanly to icon size without changing how it looks. The GUI uses the orange-hexagon, black-print variant (`Spoor_O_B`) in its top bar and as its browser-tab icon. Compared side by side on the GUI's dark theme, the black-hexagon variants lose their hexagon edge and read as a floating print, and the orange one also works on both light and dark tab bars. `docs/` isn't packaged, so the GUI ships a copy at `spoor/gui/assets/spoor-logo.svg` (confirmed in a built wheel; renamed `spoor-logo-night.svg`, with a `spoor-logo-day.svg` beside it, by the day/night decision below). That copy is inlined as a `data:` URI, so no new route is needed, and a test fails if it ever drifts from `docs/assets/Spoor_O_B.svg`. The logos' orange was then matched to the theme rather than the other way round, at the maintainer's wish: their original `#E97132` sat visibly off the theme's coral accent in the top bar, and the maintainer couldn't hit the CSS colour when drawing them. The two orange variants were recoloured to `#F85840`. In the SVGs this is a fill swap. In the PNGs every visible pixel was verified to be an exact orange/black blend, and that blend was reapplied to the new colour, so edges and transparency are unchanged. The only exception was a few near-transparent edge pixels (alpha ≤ 31) carrying invisible rounding noise, which were clamped to the pure colour. The black/white variant has no orange and is untouched.

**Decision — day and night modes for the GUI and the wiki (maintainer request).** The shared theme gains a day palette beside the existing night one, which is unchanged. Both surfaces switch together: they share the stylesheet, so they keep looking like one product. Settled with the maintainer:
- **Choosing a mode.** The page follows the system's light/dark setting. A sun/moon button at the right of the top bar overrides it, and the choice is remembered. It is saved as a cookie (cookies aren't tied to a port, so the choice survives the GUI's per-launch port) and in localStorage (which works for a wiki opened as a local file, where cookies don't). The switch script (`theme.py:THEME_SCRIPT`) runs in `<head>` before the page is drawn, so a saved choice never flashes the wrong mode.
- **The palette.** Day colours share the night palette's token names, so every rule follows. They are defined once (`theme._DAY_TOKENS`) and applied both for an explicit choice (`data-theme="light"` on `<html>`) and for a system preference with no explicit dark choice. Coral, teal and the yellow code colour are deepened for white. `tests/test_theme.py` pins WCAG contrast for the text colours in both palettes (AA 4.5:1 for text, 3:1 for the large teal headings) and that both palettes define the same tokens. It found nothing to fix in night mode at those pairs. One known, pre-existing, minor night case remains outside them and unchanged: coral link text over the table-row hover colour is about 4.1:1.
- **Logos.** Each mode uses the logo made for its background, through `--night-only` / `--day-only` display tokens: the orange hexagon by night, the black hexagon with coral print by day. The browser-tab icon stays the orange hexagon in both, since it reads on any tab bar. The GUI ships both as copies of the brand files, and the drift test covers both.
- **The wiki's overview diagram** used to have its night colours hard-coded. It now reads the page's colour tokens when it draws, using exactly the same values as before at night, and redraws on the switch's `spoor-theme-change` event.

The scenarios added to `gui.feature` and `exploration_wiki.feature` cover it. They include a real-browser scenario that starts in day mode under a light system setting, switches to night, and finds night still chosen after the GUI is restarted on another port. The result was also checked visually in both modes: GUI map, form and job pages, and the wiki with its diagram redrawing on the switch.

**Decision — the GUI opens on an introduction page (maintainer request).** `/` is now an introduction to Spoor rather than the mapped-sites list, which moves to `/maps`. The "Spoor" logo in the top bar returns to the introduction; "Maps" goes to the list. The page is written for an operator, in plain language with no section references, and is consistent with the README's own framing. It covers:
- the hero sentence and a short description;
- what Spoor does (map a site's screens and actions, extract data that survives redesigns, show the API behind a page, hand the map to scripts and agents);
- who it's for (test automation, AI-agent builders, data collection, understanding an application);
- how it stays safe by default (local storage, sandbox-only destructive actions, redaction, `robots.txt` and rate limits, never logging in on its own);
- where to start, with live counts of mapped sites and running jobs;
- links to the repository, its README and its issues, read from the package's own metadata (`[project.urls]` in `pyproject.toml`, via `app.project_urls()`), not written in code. `scripts/check_genericity.py` scans `spoor/gui/` too and rightly flagged a hardcoded `github.com` constant; metadata, the standard place for project links, keeps the check strict instead of adding an allowlist exception;
- the installed version and the README's short legal note.

The copy was reviewed against what Spoor actually does, and two claims were softened before merge. "Nothing is sent anywhere" became "stored on this computer and never uploaded": Spoor does fetch the sites it visits, and the wiki loads its diagram library from a CDN. "Links each action to the requests it triggered" became "can tie an action to the requests it triggered".

**Testing.** Fast tier: the GUI app via FastAPI `TestClient` (token/host/origin rejection, loopback-only bind refusal, pages render the redacted view, metadata-only sessions, config validation errors), and the job manager against a **fake runner** (an injected callable standing in for the subprocess) so argument assembly, status transitions, Stop and log redaction are tested without launching Spoor. `@browser` tier: one end-to-end scenario per slice that launches the real GUI and drives it with Playwright, Spoor dogfooding itself. For gui-2, that means starting an exploration of a static fixture through the form and seeing the output path appear. `scripts/check_genericity.py` already covers it: nothing here is site-specific (§0).

**Deliberately out of scope here, each its own future decision:** remote/multi-user access and real authentication; capturing a login session from inside the GUI (open a headed Spoor browser, the operator logs in by hand, save its storage state as a named session; §2h's bring-your-own-session explicitly permits "in Spoor's [browser]", so this is likely the next high-value slice after gui-4, but it is a new capture path and gets its own note); a structured config form editor; persisted job history; and retiring `spoor wizard` (kept until the GUI covers every command it does).

## 3. License

**Decided: MIT.** Maximizes adoption ("for the masses"), and is compatible with Crawlee (Apache-2.0) and Playwright (Apache-2.0) as dependencies. Copyleft options (GPL, and especially AGPL) were rejected deliberately: AGPL in particular would bind HT's own future use, since it triggers on running a modified version as a network service — exactly what §2f's MCP/API serving layer is. Apache-2.0 was also considered (its explicit patent grant matters more for projects with heavier original patent-eligible contributions); MIT is the simpler, more universally recognized choice given Spoor's genuinely novel pieces are integration/porting work, not something that needs that extra protection. Avoid depending on anything AGPL/commercial-only; specifically, use Healenium's OSS core algorithm/approach rather than Healenium Pro (commercial, adds SaaS features this project doesn't need).

## 4. Phased roadmap

Scope is all four tiers for v1 per your call, but "all four tiers" doesn't have to mean "all four polished." Sequencing them so something real ships early is what keeps this from becoming a months-long black box:

**Phase 0 — Setup (few days)**
Repo scaffold, license, `pyproject.toml` (distribution name **`ht-spoor`** — the plain `spoor` name is taken on PyPI by an unrelated package; project/brand/CLI/repo name all stay **Spoor**, only the `pip install` name carries the prefix), CI (lint + test), contribution guidelines, issue templates.

**Phase 1 — Tiers 1–2, config format, and basic output (1.5–2.5 weeks)**
Wire up Crawlee's adaptive crawler almost as-is — highest-value, lowest-risk work, a thin wrapper around a mature library. Add the §2a declarative config parser and a first pass at the §2d output pipeline (JSON/CSV) and politeness policy in the same phase, since without these the tool has no real user-facing surface yet. Ship this alone as an early v0.1 if you want real feedback fast.

**Phase 2 — Tier 4, human interaction (3–5 days)**
Playwright's primitives plus the jitter/easing wrapper. No ML, no fragile heuristics — quick to build and test, and it's useful on its own (e.g. defeating simple bot checks) even before tier 3 exists.

**Phase 2.5 — API surface discovery + default signal capture (1–2 weeks, can run in parallel with Phase 2/3)**
Spec discovery + GraphQL introspection probes (days), wire in HAR recording on every Playwright context (near-free once Phase 1 exists), then the mitmproxy2swagger spike and, if it pans out, the action-correlation log. Bundle in the free/near-free signals from §2c (storage state, console/errors, accessibility tree, response headers) as default capture at the same time, since they piggyback on the same context. This only depends on Phase 1 existing, not on tiers 3–4, so it can be built alongside them rather than after.

**Phase 2.6 — Opt-in deep signals (3–5 days, only if/when needed)**
Video recording, heap snapshots, and media-manifest capture as explicit flags, not defaults — build these on demand rather than up front, since they're the least likely to be used on every run.

**Phase 3 — Tier 3, local self-healing (2–3 weeks, do not compress this phase — see §1)**
The most genuinely novel engineering here, and per §1 the single highest-priority phase in the whole plan: fingerprint capture on first success, scored matching on failure, a tunable confidence threshold, built alongside the §5.3 mutation-testing harness from the start rather than bolted on after. Expect this phase to need the most iteration and real-world testing against sites that change their markup — that's expected, not a sign something's wrong.

**Phase 3.5 — Reliability essentials (1–1.5 weeks)**
The rest of §2d. **Delivered:** retry/error classification with a dead-letter log (the tier-1 fetch path — see the Phase-3.5 decision note in §2d), browser-tier navigation retry (the same classification/backoff wrapping `page.goto`, so a transient navigation failure no longer crashes the run — see its decision note), anti-bot/CAPTCHA detection (fail loudly, detection-not-bypass — including challenges served *behind* an error status, carried on the `FetchFailure` and surfaced beside the dead-letter line — see its decision note), change detection (ETag/`Last-Modified`/content-hash skip of unchanged pages, opt-in — see its decision note), and the run-summary observability output (Phase 1, now also carrying the dead-letter log, retry count, challenge, and unchanged-page count). This completes §2d's reliability essentials. Sat naturally after Phase 3 since it's the same "handle things going wrong gracefully" mindset as self-healing, just at the request/run level instead of the element level.

**Phase 4 — "For the masses" packaging + v1 surface (1–2 weeks)**
PyPI publish as **`ht-spoor`** (`pip install ht-spoor`, imports/CLI still `spoor`), CLI polish, docs site fronted by the hero sentence, 3–5 worked example configs (§2a) against public scraping-practice sites, a short "why not just use Crawl4AI" positioning note in the README, GitHub Discussions or Discord for community, and a first-pass §2f MCP/API server exposing whatever §2a/§2b have already captured (the fuller version, serving the §2e exploration graph, follows once v2 ships). Two extension points are **documented, not built — just the interface**: the proxy hook, and the optional AI fallback (`resolver.set_ai_fallback(fn)`, tier 3.5) so a power user can wire in Claude/GPT/a local Ollama VLM with no default provider shipped and the core staying dependency-free. (This absorbs what an earlier draft split out as a standalone "Phase 4 — AI fallback"; see the decision note below.)

Realistic total: **9–13 weeks** of focused work for a genuinely usable v1 (four UI tiers, API surface discovery, the declarative config interface, and the operational essentials that make it a real tool rather than a library fragment). Phase 3 (tier-3 healing accuracy) is still the likeliest to run long; Phase 3.5 is the next-most-likely, since "graceful failure" only gets exercised properly against real, messy, occasionally-hostile sites.

**Decision — the optional AI fallback is folded into packaging, not a standalone phase.** An earlier draft sequenced the tier-3.5 AI fallback as its own "Phase 4" ahead of packaging. It is deliberately the smallest, lowest-stakes item in the plan — off by default, no provider shipped, a thin `set_ai_fallback(fn)` seam whose whole purpose is to keep AI *out* of the dependency-free core (§1). Given that, standing it up as its own phase over-weighted it: AI is explicitly not the product's differentiator. It is therefore folded into the packaging phase as a **documented extension point built to the same "interface only, not implemented" bar as the proxy hook** — the seam exists and is documented at v1 launch, but no phase is spent building a provider around it. A concrete provider integration lands only if a real user need pulls it in. This changes *sequencing only*: the tier-3.5 capability itself (§2 tier table, §7 dependency table) is unchanged.

**Phase 5 — Exploration mode + wiki generation (v2, ~6–9 weeks, starts after v1 ships)**
The full §2e system: state-abstraction tuning, the sandbox-only destructive-action safety system (with its own dedicated test suite proving it can't be bypassed against a non-sandbox target), graph storage, and wiki templating. Deliberately sequenced after v1 rather than folded into it — it depends on the tiers, signals, and action-correlation groundwork from Phases 1–3.5 already existing and being stable, and it's substantial enough on its own that bundling it into the v1 estimate would just make that number meaningless.

## 4a. Repository setup & development approach

**Feature development is BDD-first, layered on top of the existing test pyramid (§5), not a replacement for it.** Every named capability in this plan (§2a extraction, §2d operational essentials, §2e exploration safety, §2f MCP/API serving, §2h data handling) gets a `.feature` file written in Gherkin (Given/When/Then), using `pytest-bdd` so it plugs directly into the same `pytest`/`hypothesis`/`pytest-cov` stack already committed to in §7, rather than adding a second test runner. A feature's scenarios define what "done" means for that capability in plain, reviewable language; they sit above — never instead of — the property-based mutation testing (§5.3) and golden-master diffing (§5.4) that validate tier 3's internals, since "generate hundreds of variants and check a statistical property" isn't a shape Gherkin scenarios are suited for. Workflow for adding or changing a feature: extend/add the `.feature` file first, then step definitions, then implementation, then the full local gate (below) — in that order, not implementation-first with tests bolted on after.

**`CLAUDE.md` at the repo root is the agent-facing instruction file** — read automatically by any Claude Code session working in the repo, the counterpart to this document (which stays the source of truth for *what* and *why*; `CLAUDE.md` is *how to work here*). It points at this file rather than restating decisions, states §0 as a self-check with the genericity CI script named as the enforcement mechanism, spells out the BDD workflow above, and names the non-negotiables — sandbox-only destructive actions (§2e), the MCP/API read-only boundary (§2h), default secret redaction (§2h), and the ≥95% tier-3 mutation-corpus bar (§5.3) — as things no session may weaken without an explicit human decision recorded here.

**Initial document set (Phase 0):**
- `README.md` — hero sentence, elevator pitch, quickstart (human-facing front door)
- `CLAUDE.md` — agent-facing instructions, as above
- `docs/ROADMAP.md` — this document, moved into the repo as the living plan
- `LICENSE` — MIT (§3)
- `CONTRIBUTING.md`
- `pyproject.toml`
- `.github/workflows/` — CI: lint, test, the §0 genericity-check script, the §5.1 nightly drift job
- issue templates
- `features/` — one `.feature` file per named capability, as above

## 5. Testing strategy & test bench design

**Honest framing first, since reliability is now the top priority (§1): "100% accuracy" isn't a real target for a system that must generalize to arbitrary, unknown websites — no adaptive, heuristic-driven mechanism can be proven correct against input it's never seen.** What's achievable, and what this section actually designs for, is a *measured, tracked* accuracy standard: a known success rate against a large, growing corpus of realistic mutations, regressions caught automatically before they reach a user, and an honest published number rather than a marketing claim. §6 already says not to oversell tier 4's stealth in marketing copy — the same discipline applies here to tier 3's reliability.

### 5.1 The fixture layer

- **Static local fixture pages** (checked into the repo) for deterministic per-tier unit tests — hand-built HTML/JS pages that isolate one mechanism each (one page whose element class name changes every reload, to drive tier-3 healing tests deterministically; one that requires JS to render a field, to test tier-2 escalation; one with WebSocket traffic, to test §2c capture) — these exist because the archetype apps below are realistic but not fully controllable, and deterministic unit tests need fully controllable inputs.
- **Public scraping-practice sandboxes** (`books.toscrape.com`, `quotes.toscrape.com`) for basic integration tests and demos — legal, stable, and exactly what "the masses" will try the tool against first.
- **The archetype matrix, purely as validation fixtures (see §0 — never as integrations the core code knows about):** OWASP Juice Shop (security-flavored webshop + API, self-hosted via Docker), Sauce Demo (clean login-gated webshop: login/cart/checkout, self-hosted via Docker — never the live `saucedemo.com`, per the out-of-scope note below), a self-hosted ERPNext or Odoo instance (auth-gated internal app: heavy tables, forms, workflows), and a self-hosted open-source Netflix-clone (heavy client-rendered SPA + media state). If one archetype needs a special case to pass, that's a bug in the general mechanism, not a fixture to special-case around.
- **A `docker-compose.yml` test bench** bringing up all four fixtures at once, each declared `sandbox: true` (so §2e's exploration mode can exercise destructive actions safely against them) — what CI and local exploration-mode testing runs against.
- A small **nightly job** against a handful of real-world sites (permissive robots.txt) to catch drift before users hit it.

**Explicitly out of scope as a test target:** a real company's live production site (e.g. Netflix's actual service) — it teaches the suite nothing the self-hosted clone doesn't already cover, and ties CI health to a target nobody controls.

**Decision — Phase-1 in-vivo bench (Juice Shop only).** The `docker-compose.yml` bench (`fixtures/docker-compose.yml`) is introduced in Phase 1 with **only OWASP Juice Shop** stood up, not the full four-app matrix. Juice Shop is an Angular SPA whose product listing renders client-side, so it exercises exactly what Phase 1 built end-to-end against a real, uncontrolled app: tier 1 finds nothing, the dispatcher escalates, tier 2 renders in a browser and extracts. The other three archetypes gate on capabilities that do not exist yet — Sauce Demo and ERPNext/Odoo need auth (Phase 2+), Juice Shop's API surface needs §2b discovery (Phase 2.5), and destructive-action exploration needs §2e (Phase 5) — so standing them up now would be scaffolding, not validation; they join the same bench as their phases arrive. Per §5.2 the docker E2E smoke is a **pre-merge gate**, so CI boots the container and runs it on every PR; locally the integration test **skips gracefully** when the container isn't reachable, keeping the fast unit gate network- and Docker-free. Juice Shop is pinned to a specific image tag for determinism. The `sandbox: true` declaration §5.1 calls for is recorded as a compose label until the §2e registry consumes it. Because the pinned image is deterministic, the run's extracted records are both written to a durable path and uploaded as a CI artifact **and** diffed against a committed golden master (`tests/golden/`) — the first concrete seed of §5.4 dogfooding, so a silent change in Spoor *or* the fixture is surfaced. CI caches the pip download, the Playwright browser, and the pinned image tarball to keep the gate fast (~30s) as heavier archetypes join the bench later.

**Decision — Sauce Demo joins the bench (second archetype, first login-gated).** With bring-your-own-session (§2h) shipped, the capability that can actually exercise a login-gated app now exists, so Sauce Demo joins the bench. It earns its place on two counts: it is a structurally different SPA from Juice Shop (a second §0 no-tailoring data point — the same unmodified tiers resolve both), and its inventory is reachable only behind a login, making it the first *end-to-end* proof of BYO-session against a real app rather than the synthetic loopback server `session.feature` uses. No official or trustworthy prebuilt image exists, so it is built from a **pinned source commit** of `saucelabs/sample-app-web` (a static React/Vite build served by nginx; `fixtures/sauce-demo/`) — pinned for the same determinism reason Juice Shop pins an image tag. The bench host port defaults to 3001 and is overridable (`SAUCE_DEMO_PORT`) for machines where 3001 is taken; the integration test reads the matching base from `SPOOR_SAUCE_DEMO_BASE`. Two integration tests (`tests/test_integration_sauce_demo.py`) capture the guarantee: a browser-tier run **without** a session is redirected to the login page and extracts nothing (the gate is real), and a run **with** a session the test captured the way a user would (Spoor never logs in — that would be a per-site flow violating §0) escalates from the empty static shell to the browser tier and extracts the gated inventory, diffed against a committed golden master (§5.4). The `session-username` login cookie is short-lived, so the session is captured fresh per run rather than committed (which also keeps a secret out of the repo).

**Decision — PrestaShop joins the bench (third archetype, first server-rendered storefront).** The two existing archetypes are both SPAs whose whole app effectively lives at one URL — which under-exercises exactly the part of §2e the 7-series has been hardening: mapping a *large, deeply-linked, stateful, multi-page* site with real navigation, forms, and per-page URLs. PrestaShop fills that gap: a mainstream PHP/MySQL e-commerce platform whose catalog, category pages, product pages, cart, and checkout are ordinary server-rendered pages — the structural opposite of Juice Shop and Sauce Demo, and a much richer graph for reset-and-replay, state abstraction, and coverage measurement to chew on. It is stood up from the **official `prestashop/prestashop:8.1` image**, pinned by tag for the same determinism reason the other two pin (Juice Shop's image tag, Sauce Demo's source commit). Unlike the distroless SPAs it needs a datastore, so it ships as **two services** — `prestashop` plus a `mariadb:11` `prestashop-db` (infrastructure, *not* itself a scrape target, so no `sandbox` label and no host port) — and **auto-installs on first boot** (`PS_INSTALL_AUTO=1`): the first `up` takes a few minutes to build the schema and demo catalog, then persists in a Docker volume so later boots are fast. `PS_DOMAIN` is pinned to the published `127.0.0.1:<port>` so generated links resolve to the served host. The bench host port defaults to **8080** (PrestaShop's conventional dev port; the 300x range is taken) and is overridable via `PRESTASHOP_PORT`. Like the others it carries the `spoor.sandbox: "true"` label so §2e exploration may exercise it freely. *Deliberately not shipped in this change:* an integration test and golden master — a full storefront's exact graph is a poor golden (its demo catalog and session state vary), so a PrestaShop integration test wants a structural-completeness contract like `tests/test_integration_wiki.py`'s rather than a record diff, and that is its own BDD-first slice. This change stands the target up and documents it; wiring it into the automated bench (the CI boot matrix and an exploration reliability test against it) follows.

**Decision — streaming-clone joins the bench (fourth archetype, purpose-built rather than adopted).** The planned "self-hosted Netflix-clone" archetype turned out to have no clean adopt-as-is candidate. Every third-party project vetted (`devopsinsiders/netflix-clone`, `Apestein/nextflix`, `zoriya/Kyoo`, an `aiworklabs` clone, `ridhwaans/homehost`, and the LocalFlix/`netflix-local` family) was either a production media server needing real video files and a transcoding pipeline to show anything, or a thin frontend hard-wired to a mandatory external SaaS (Clerk, Stripe, Neon, TMDB, Spotify, in various combinations) — neither matches the bench's pattern of a self-contained app with its own data and no external accounts (Juice Shop/PrestaShop's pinned images, Sauce Demo's pinned-source-commit build). So this archetype is authored by us: a small Express app (`fixtures/streaming-clone/`) with a same-origin JSON API, a server-set-session login gate (a second, differently-shaped bring-your-own-session §2h proof point alongside Sauce Demo's SPA-token pattern), and a per-title detail view with a `<video>` element — the first fixture to exercise the media/streaming-capture signal named in §2c but not yet triggered by any archetype. No database, so like the two SPAs it's a single service, built from our own checked-in source (no upstream commit to pin, unlike Sauce Demo). The catalog, posters (`seed/catalog.json`, generated placeholder SVGs), and one short, low-bitrate seed video clip per title (`seed/videos/`) are all checked in — `seed/videos/README.md` documents the filename contract for adding a new title later, and the app degrades gracefully ("Preview unavailable") should a clip ever be missing. The bench host port defaults to **3002** and is overridable via `STREAMING_CLONE_PORT`, carrying the same `spoor.sandbox: "true"` label as the rest of the bench. `tests/test_integration_streaming_clone.py` covers login, the catalog API, and the unauthenticated-rejection gate, following the skip-when-Docker-down pattern. Kept deliberately minimal (no search, no recommendations, no watch-history state, no published prebuilt image) — those are backlogged as this archetype's own future enhancements, not built now.

**Decision — streaming-clone wired into Spoor's extraction pipeline (bring-your-own-session, second data point).** The follow-on the decision above deferred. `fixtures/configs/streaming-clone-catalog.yaml` extracts the title catalog via a new item-mode config (`item: ".tile"`, fields for title/category/poster); the frontend gained a hidden per-tile `.tile-category` span (`public/app.js`) so category is readable without reaching outside the tile's own row, since a field selector resolves *within* its matched element (`root.css(...)`, a descendant query) rather than against it. This is streaming-clone's proof of bring-your-own-session (§2h) end to end — a second, structurally different one from Sauce Demo's: Sauce Demo's session comes from an SPA login-route redirect, streaming-clone's from a server-set session cookie behind a same-page login form (the view toggles via JS, no URL change on success). `tests/test_integration_streaming_clone_extraction.py` mirrors Sauce Demo's test shape: a session-less run escalates to the browser tier (tier 1 sees the empty shell) but extracts nothing (no `.tile` ever attaches without the fetch a login gates), and an authenticated run (session captured by driving a real Chromium through the login form, exactly as a user would — Spoor performs no login itself, §2h/§0) extracts the catalog and matches a committed golden master (§5.4) — an exact match fits here, unlike PrestaShop's deferred golden master, because the catalog is static checked-in seed data, not a variable demo catalog. No core code changed (§0): both the config and the frontend tweak are fixture-side.

**Decision — `spoor explore` and `apply-scaffold` gain bring-your-own-session, closing issue #164.** `spoor run` (config-based extraction) had `session:` from the start; exploration mode never did — `PlaywrightDriver(url)` in `spoor/cli.py` was always constructed anonymously, a deliberate scope narrowing recorded above ("Sauce Demo dropped from the *exploration* bench") rather than an oversight, but one that left streaming-clone's whole reason for existing (a rich, login-gated catalog/detail surface, explored into a wiki with screenshots) unreachable: `spoor explore` against it mapped exactly one state, the login screen. The real fork wasn't "add a flag" — it was that `PlaywrightDriver.reset()` deliberately `clear_cookies()`s and clears web storage before every navigation, so reset-and-replay always starts from a true first visit (§2e, 7b); naively loading a session only via `new_context(storage_state=...)` at `__enter__` would get silently wiped by the *very first* `reset()`, and exploration would still never get past login. Resolution: a supplied session redefines "true first visit" as "as this session," not anonymous. Cookies (no init-script equivalent) are re-added via `context.add_cookies(...)` right after every `clear_cookies()` in `reset()`; localStorage is restored by one `context.add_init_script(...)` registered at `__enter__` (alongside the existing mutation-observer script) that runs on *every* document the context ever loads, so it needs no per-reset action at all — proven together live against a loopback fixture whose second page is cookie-gated and third page is localStorage-gated: an anonymous crawl maps 1 state, a cookie-only session reaches 2, and a full session reaches all 3, across the crawl's several `reset()` calls, not just the first. `spoor/security/session.py:LoadedSession` gained a `raw` field (the parsed storage-state object itself) so the driver can re-apply the exact cookie/origin shapes Playwright wrote, rather than lossily round-tripping through `SessionCookie` (built only for the static tier's `httpx` jar). `--session` lands on both `explore` and `apply-scaffold` (the latter's help text already gestured at `session:` without consuming one), validated up front via `load_session` so a bad file fails before any browser launches, matching every other validation this command already does; it composes freely with `--resume-from` (orthogonal — one picks where a crawl starts, the other whether it's authenticated). Spoor still performs no login itself (§2h, §0) — this only replays an already-captured session, the same boundary `run` already respects. No change to the `BrowserDriver` Protocol (session is a driver *construction* detail, not a new method), so every existing fake driver is untouched.

Manually verifying this against the actual fixture that motivated it (streaming-clone) surfaced a second, real gap, fixed in the same change: an authenticated crawl reached the real catalog (confirmed by screenshot — the login screen was gone) but still mapped only 1 state, because each tile was a plain `<div>` with a JS click handler and no ARIA role — invisible to the accessibility tree Spoor's discovery (and a keyboard/screen-reader user) reads from. `public/app.js` now renders each tile as a real `<button type="button">` (restyled in `style.css` to look identical); the same session then reaches all 6 detail screens, 7 states and 15 transitions total, each with a real screenshot including the seeded `<video>` preview. Session support and element discoverability are two separate concerns — this fixture just happened to need both before it was actually explorable end to end.

**Decision — the bench also validates the §2f serving round-trip end-to-end.** The read-only serving layer (§2f REST + MCP, with the §2h redaction and API-surface projection) was built and tested in isolation against a synthetic in-memory `MapStore`; it had no proof it holds against a *real* capture. `tests/test_integration_serving.py` closes that: it runs the dispatcher against live Juice Shop, records the result the exact way the `spoor run` command does (`shareable_api_surface` → `MapStore.record`), reads it back through a **fresh** store (so the on-disk JSON round-trip is real, not an in-memory shortcut), and serves it over **both** surfaces — the REST API via Starlette's `TestClient` and the MCP server in-process. It asserts each surface returns the same records the run produced (post-redaction, §2h), carries a non-negative freshness age (§2f), agrees with the other, and refuses an unmapped URL rather than fabricating one; the MCP surface's read-only/non-destructive tool annotations (the §2f non-negotiable) are asserted against a real capture, not just a fixture. This reuses the same skip-when-Docker-down pattern as the other archetype tests, so the fast unit gate stays Docker-free. No serving code changed — the layer already held; this is the missing archetype-bench proof that it does.

**Decision — test tiering: a seconds-fast local gate, the full suite on CI.** The suite had grown to the point where a plain local `pytest` took ~10 minutes, because the browser-launching scenarios (real headless Chromium) and the docker-archetype integration tests ran alongside the in-process unit and fake-driver BDD tests. That is the wrong default for the edit-run loop: local should be fast, CI can be thorough. The split is by pytest marker. Two slow tiers are tagged — `browser` (anything that launches a real Chromium; applied per *scenario* via Gherkin `@browser` tags on the browser-tier scenarios, not per file, because most step files are mixed: a browser scenario beside network-free `mock_client`/tier-1 scenarios) and the existing `integration` (needs a live docker archetype). `pyproject.toml`'s `addopts` deselects both by default (`-m 'not browser and not integration'`), so a bare local `pytest` runs only the in-process tier in seconds; the PR-blocking CI gate overrides it with `pytest -m "not integration"` (§5.1's amending decision below moved `integration` off the PR gate entirely) so the `browser` tier still gates every PR, while the live-docker-archetype `integration` tier runs nightly instead. On demand, a developer runs `pytest -m browser`, `pytest -m integration`, or `pytest -m ""` locally. One test-isolation defect surfaced doing this: the `test_escalation.py` walk tests called `run_report()` with no client, so the §2b companion API probe opened a real `httpx.Client` and hit `http://localhost/x` — a multi-second timeout per test; they now take the `mock_client` fixture purely to keep that probe off the network.

**Decision — Sauce Demo dropped from the *exploration* bench, kept for extraction/session.** The live-archetype wiki-reliability test (`tests/test_integration_wiki.py`) previously crawled both Juice Shop and Sauce Demo; the Sauce Demo parametrization is removed. Sauce Demo's whole app sits behind a login wall, so an *unauthenticated* exploration crawl (which is what that test runs — it does not inject a session) reaches only the login page and maps nothing worth asserting a completeness contract over. Sauce Demo keeps its place in the bench where it earns it: `tests/test_integration_sauce_demo.py` (the bring-your-own-session §2h end-to-end and the §0 second-data-point extraction proof), its container, configs, and golden master are all retained. This narrows one test's scope; it does not retire the archetype.

**Decision — the live-docker-archetype `integration` tier moves off the PR gate to a nightly run.** The fast-local/full-CI split above did not stay balanced as the bench grew: by the time Sauce Demo (login-gated, built from source on every run — no prebuilt image exists) and PrestaShop (a full PHP/MySQL server that auto-installs on first boot) joined Juice Shop on the bench, `docker compose -f fixtures/docker-compose.yml up -d` in `ci.yml` was booting all three **unscoped** on every PR — including PrestaShop, which has no integration test consuming it yet, so that boot cost was pure waste. Sauce Demo alone (no image cache, a real build step) made the PR gate "terribly long," in a maintainer's own words, undermining the point of the seconds-fast/thorough-CI split the decision above exists to protect: a slow mandatory gate is a gate people start working around, not a safety net.

The `integration` marker (any test needing a live docker archetype — `test_integration_juiceshop.py`, `test_integration_sauce_demo.py`, and any future PrestaShop/ERP/Netflix-clone counterpart) is removed from the PR-blocking gate and runs instead in `.github/workflows/nightly.yml`, on the same daily schedule as the drift job, plus `workflow_dispatch` for an on-demand run. `ci.yml` no longer touches Docker at all: `pytest -m "not integration"` runs the fast in-process tier plus the `browser` tier (still real Chromium, but no live-app boot — comparatively fast and, unlike `integration`, has no external service to skip against). The nightly job boots the *whole* bench (`docker compose up -d`, unscoped — appropriate there since nothing is racing a PR) and runs `pytest -m integration`; a failure there is a genuine regression to fix promptly, not a PR blocker to wait out, since §0's genericity guarantee is still being checked daily, just not synchronously with every push.

This is a narrowing of *when* the live-archetype proof runs, never of *whether* it runs or what it's allowed to find: a fixture that only passes with a special case is still a bug in the general mechanism (§5 preamble) regardless of which workflow catches it. Local `pytest -m integration` remains available on demand for anyone touching exploration/tier-escalation code who wants the live-archetype signal before pushing, same as today. The §5.2 load/concurrency and chaos-injection tiers, and booting further archetypes (ERP/Netflix-clone) as their phases arrive, remain a genuinely open design fork — this decision only resolves *where the existing three archetypes' `integration` tests run*, not the shape of a future performance/chaos matrix, which still wants its own decision on runner cost and matrix shape when it's actually built.

### 5.2 Test types, mapped to what each actually validates

| Test type | Tool | What it catches |
|---|---|---|
| Unit tests | `pytest` | Each tier's logic in isolation against static fixtures — fast, deterministic, run on every commit. |
| Property-based / mutation testing (tier 3 specifically) | `hypothesis` (MIT) | See §5.3 — this is the one that actually matters most given §1's reliability priority. |
| Golden-master regression | `pytest` + Spoor's own captured output | See §5.4 — using Spoor's own maps as the "did anything silently change" baseline, including §2g's generated assertions. |
| Chaos / fault injection | `toxiproxy` (Apache-2.0) | Network latency, dropped connections, slow responses, injected between Spoor and the fixtures — proves §2d's retry/backoff/error-classification actually handles real-world messiness instead of just the happy path. |
| Load / concurrency | `locust` (MIT) | Confirms the §2d politeness policy actually caps concurrency and backs off under load, not just in a single-request test. |
| Safety / sandbox-bypass (adversarial) | `pytest` | Deliberately tries to trick the §2e destructive-action guard (malformed sandbox declarations, edge-case URLs) and asserts it never fires against a non-sandbox target. Given the weight put on that guarantee, this needs its own dedicated suite, not incidental coverage. |
| MCP/API contract tests | `pytest` + schema validation | §2f's responses match their declared schema, and the freshness/staleness signal (§2f) is actually present and accurate on every response. |
| Genericity enforcement (§0) | A small custom script, in CI | Scans core source for hardcoded hostnames/domain strings outside the recognizers/fixtures config, fails the build if found — turns §0's "no site-tailoring" design principle into an automated, unbypassable check rather than a code-review hope. |
| End-to-end smoke | CLI run against each fixture | Cheap, fast pre-merge gate: did the whole pipeline actually run and produce non-empty, valid output. |
| Coverage tracking | `pytest-cov` | Baseline metric, tracked over time — not a target to game, but a signal if a whole code path has zero test contact. |

### 5.3 Tier 3 gets the most rigorous testing in the plan, on purpose

Given §1: this is the piece that can't be flaky. The design: a **mutation harness** takes a known-good fixture (one where tier 1/2 correctly finds an element) and applies a battery of realistic DOM mutations — class renames, attribute shuffles, element reordering, added wrapper elements, text changes, combinations of the above at increasing severity — then asserts tier 3 still resolves to the *correct* element above a defined confidence threshold. Using `hypothesis` to generate mutation variants (rather than hand-writing a fixed list) means the corpus grows and explores far more of the space automatically than example-based tests alone. The tracked output is a **success rate against this corpus**, published and monitored for regression on every change to tier 3 — that number, not a one-time claim, is what "reliable" actually means here.

**Concrete v1 target (decided): ≥95% correct-element resolution on the mutation corpus**, tracked per release and gating a Phase 3 "done" call — not just an open-ended measured number with no bar to clear. Anything below that threshold on a given mutation class is a known gap to fix before shipping, not a footnote.

### 5.4 Dogfooding: Spoor's own captured maps as regression tests

Once Spoor maps a fixture's behavior (via §2a or §2e), that map is a golden master: state X, action Y, signal bundle Z. Re-running Spoor against the same fixture later should reproduce a matching bundle — a diff-based test asserts nothing silently changed, in either the fixture *or* Spoor's own behavior, and this doubles as a concrete demonstration of exactly what §2g (generated test automation) produces for an end user. This is testing the tool with the tool, run on every CI pass against the docker-compose fixtures in §5.1.

**On exact reproducibility:** Spoor does not promise byte-identical output run-to-run against a real, live target — the target itself can change, tier 3 can legitimately heal to a different-but-equally-correct element, and async network timing varies. The golden-master/diff approach above *is* the reproducibility model: not "identical every time," but "any change gets surfaced and reviewed," which is the honest version of reproducibility a system like this can actually deliver.

### 5.5 Performance bench & trend dashboard (decided; small set shipped)

A continuous performance bench runs the real exploration pipeline against the pinned §5.1 archetypes in CI and trends the numbers over time, so a performance regression (or a coverage regression) is caught as drift rather than discovered by a user. Three sets, sized for how often they can afford to run:

- **Small** — a tight, count-bounded Juice Shop crawl. Cheap enough to run **in parallel with the CI gate** on every push/PR (its own job and container, so it never gates or is gated by the suite).
- **Mid** — a larger-budget Juice Shop crawl. Nightly.
- **Large** — a PrestaShop crawl (server-rendered, deeply linked, DB-backed; needs the image/volume cached like CI caches the Juice Shop tarball). Nightly or weekly.

**The metrics split in two, and this split is the whole design.** For a *pinned* target and a **count-based** budget (`max_states`/`max_depth` — never `max_seconds`, whose stop point would depend on runner speed), the *map* the crawl builds is deterministic (§0), so the **graph-shape counters** reproduce exactly run to run and are **gated** against a committed baseline (fail the run on any drift, the golden-master model of §5.4 applied to the crawl shape): states, transitions (actuated actions), skips, and the total actions *discovered* (coverage — read against actuated/skipped it shows how much of what was found was driven). These held identical across every CI run.

What is *not* reproducible — and seeding the baseline proved this empirically, which is exactly why §5.5 requires a CI-seeded baseline to be inspected before the gate is trusted — is anything downstream of the **walk**: the **per-operation call counts** (`reset`/`state_html`/`ax_nodes`/`probe`/`perform` …), the **skip-reason message**, *and even the capture counts* (`image_files`/`image_refs`). The walk's replay/recovery loop reacts to the live target's runtime nondeterminism (Juice Shop is an SPA whose reset does not always land in the same place), so the number of replay retries — and occasionally an extra element capture — differs run to run even when the map is identical: a check run caught `image_refs` moving 151→152 with states/transitions/skips/discovered unchanged. So all of these are **reported as advisory only, never gated** (dedup stays observable as files-vs-refs; the counts feed triage). This corrects the earlier assumption that the per-operation call counts and the dedup counts were deterministic — only the graph shape is. Dedup *correctness* is still protected, by slice 8g's own unit and browser tests, not by this gate.

Wall-clock timing, by contrast, swings on GitHub's shared runners, so it is an **advisory trend only** (charted via `benchmark-action/github-action-benchmark` on a `gh-pages` branch, alert-on-regression comment, never a hard failure). Timing is taken **per browser operation, not as one whole-run blob**: each driver call is one "transaction", and the bench reports each operation's median, p95 and total across the run. This is deliberate and is what makes the timing usable — a regression *localises* ("`screenshot` got 2× slower", not "the run got slower"), and each operation yields many samples per run, so its **median** is far more stable against a noisy neighbour than a single end-to-end total. Two further **localisation aids** (advisory, never gating) turn "the run is slow" into "slow *here, because of this*": a **triage verdict** that reads the deterministic result against the timing (counters unchanged ⇒ a slowdown is perf-code or runner noise; counters changed ⇒ the run did different work that likely explains it), and the **slowest individual transactions** — the specific calls, with the action each touched, that consumed the most wall-clock. This keeps runner noise out of the gate while still pointing at a real slowdown's cause.

Beyond aggregate timing, the bench collects an **event-anchored resource trace** so a regression can be located not just by *which* operation but *where in the run* it happened. Every transaction records an absolute start offset (seconds from run start) and **resident memory sampled immediately before and after the call** — bracketing each browser round-trip — using `psutil` to sum the Python process and its children so Chromium's memory is counted, not just Spoor's. Sampling on event boundaries rather than a fixed clock is deliberate: each point already knows the operation and action it belongs to, so a memory step attributes directly to the call that caused it (a fixed-cadence sampler is blind to *what* it sampled and would have to be correlated back against the transaction log anyway). The trade-off is honest — a transient that rises and falls entirely *inside* one call is invisible to boundary sampling; if that ever proves to matter, a background sampler can be layered on the same absolute-time axis later. Like the walk-dependent counters, the trace is nondeterministic run to run and is therefore a **within-run diagnostic, never gated**: it is emitted as a per-run JSON artifact (ordered `t_start`/`duration`/`op`/`detail`/`rss_before`/`rss_after` rows, ready to stretch onto a timeline), and its one scalar reduction — **peak resident memory** — rides the advisory dashboard as a smaller-is-better trend line, so a memory regression across commits surfaces alongside the timing lines. `psutil` is a dev/bench-only dependency; when it is absent the RSS readings are zero and the trace degrades to timing-only. The trace is also **rendered to an interactive HTML report** — a self-contained, dependency-free canvas chart (`scripts/assets/perftrace_report.html`, filled by `perfbench.resource_report_html`) of memory over the run's real-time axis, with each point coloured by operation, before/after series toggles, drag-to-zoom, and a per-point hover tooltip (op, action, timing, RSS before→after). The report is uploaded as a per-run artifact and, on `main`, published to `gh-pages` under `dev/trace/` (latest-only, overwritten each run; historical copies live in the artifacts) alongside the `dev/bench/` scalar dashboard. An interactive page was chosen over a static PNG so a reviewer can zoom into the moment of a spike and read the exact call that caused it.

The harness (`scripts/perfbench.py`) is generic: it takes the target URL as an argument — no archetype is hardcoded (§0), the URL lives only in the workflow — and drives the same `explore` path as the rest of the suite; its `measure`/`benchmark_entries`/`check_drift` maths is pure and unit-tested with a hand-built graph, so the fast tier covers it without a browser. **Shipped so far: the harness and the small-set job** (`.github/workflows/perf.yml`), including the event-anchored resource trace, the peak-RSS trend line, and the interactive HTML trace report published to `gh-pages`. The committed baseline is seeded once via a `workflow_dispatch --update-baseline` run and inspected before the drift gate is relied on; until a baseline is committed the small-set job records and charts but does not block. The mid and large sets land next on the nightly schedule (superseding the placeholder `nightly.yml` drift stub).

## 6. Risks worth naming up front

- **Legal/ethical, and where responsibility actually sits**: default to respecting `robots.txt` and rate-limiting; make overriding it an explicit, documented opt-out, not the default. But say this plainly and prominently in the README, not buried here — **Spoor is a technical capability, not a legal opinion or a grant of permission.** Whether it's lawful or permitted to scrape or reverse-engineer a given target's API is a question only the person pointing Spoor at it can answer, for their own situation (the target's ToS, their jurisdiction, the target's own terms). The user, not Spoor, is legally responsible for how it's pointed and used.
- **Anti-bot arms race**: tier 4's "human-like" behavior raises the bar but won't defeat serious bot detection (e.g. Cloudflare's harder tiers) — don't oversell this in marketing copy.
- **Tier 3 accuracy**: self-healing locators are the hardest part to get right; budget real iteration time here, not just initial implementation time.
- **Maintenance load**: "for the masses" means issues and PRs from people scraping sites you've never seen — decide early how much support bandwidth you actually have, and say so in the README (e.g. "best-effort" vs. actively maintained).
- **API discovery is legally touchier than UI scraping**: reverse-engineering undocumented endpoints and calling them directly bypasses whatever rate-limiting/abuse controls the official UI enforces, and many ToS treat that distinction explicitly. Worth a clear README statement that captured/synthesized specs are for understanding a product's surface, not a license to hit endpoints outside normal usage patterns.

## 7. Complete tech stack reference

Everything named across this plan, in one place. "Depend" = a real runtime dependency; "Reference" = we're porting the algorithm/approach, not taking the project as a dependency (usually because it's a different language/runtime, like Crawljax's Java/Selenium base).

**Core crawling & browser control**
| Tool | Role | License | Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| Playwright (Python) | Browser control, tiers 2–4, HAR/video/WebSocket/CDP access | Apache-2.0 | Depend |
| Crawlee for Python | Considered for `AdaptivePlaywrightCrawler`, request queue, autoscaling/concurrency, `HttpCrawler` | Apache-2.0 | **Not adopted** — D1 decided against it (§9); tier 2 was already a thin Playwright adapter rather than `AdaptivePlaywrightCrawler` (§2, Phase-1 tier-2 decision), and link-following/sitemap-seeding (§2d, #169/#170) and the remaining concurrency/resume work (§9) are all built in-house instead |
| httpx | Async HTTP client for tier 1 (no browser) | BSD-3 | Depend |
| parsel | CSS/XPath selectors (Scrapy's selector engine, unbundled) | BSD-3 | Depend |

**Supported browsers/OSes: whatever Playwright (Python) supports, no separate matrix.** Target browser engines — Chromium, Firefox, WebKit. Host OS for running Spoor itself — Linux, macOS, Windows.

**Tier 3 — resilience / self-healing**
| Tool | Role | License | Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| Healenium (core, not "Pro") | DOM-fingerprint + similarity scoring *approach* for locator healing — studied for the idea only; Spoor's scorer is written from scratch with no Healenium dependency or vendored code | Apache-2.0 | Reference (not a dependency) |
| Pillow + numpy | Perceptual hash (difference-hash) on a cropped screenshot — decode/resize + the bit math, written from scratch; the shipped tier-3 visual signal (see the Phase-3 perceptual-hash decision note) | HPND / BSD | Depend (shipped) |
